Normal and Poisson approximation for Gibbs point processes with pair potentials
Christian Hirsch, Moritz Otto, Anne Marie Svane

Abstract
. We provide a Poisson approximation result for dependent thinnings of Gibbs point processes as well as qualitative and quantitative central limit theorems for geometric functionals of Gibbs point processes in increasing observation windows. The present paper extends prior work on finite-range Gibbs processes to processes with repulsive pairwise interaction of unbounded interaction range as well as processes on marked Euclidean space. The proofs rely on coupling different Gibbs processes using the disagreement coupling technique, which we generalize to infinite-volume domains under a suitable non-percolation condition. For the case of repulsive pairwise interactions, we introduce a version of disagreement coupling that constructs the Gibbs process by thinning a random connection model thus making previous approximation methods more flexible.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60K35. Secondary 60D05, 55U10.

1.
Introduction

Point processes are important in statistics as models for random point patterns such as locations of plant or animal species, cells in biological tissue, or disease occurrences
[
1
]
. The simplest point process model is the Poisson point process, which models complete spatial randomness, see
[
29
]
for a comprehensive overview. However, in many instances such a model is too simplistic due to spatial dependencies between points. This makes it essential to consider models for dependent point patterns. In the context of spatial statistics, a particularly popular class of models is Gibbs point processes. These are motivated by statistical physics as models for interacting particle systems
[
38
]
. The present paper considers functionals of Gibbs point processes observed in large windows. We provide two types of approximate distribution results.

(1)

Dependent thinnings of Gibbs point processes.
In such a thinning, each point of the Gibbs process is either kept or removed depending on the configuration of the surrounding points. We provide bounds on the Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance between the thinned process and a Poisson process. Results like this have recently been established for both Poisson processes
[
5
,
36
]
and also for Gibbs processes
[
26
]
. In the absence of complete spatial independence, one needs to impose mixing conditions on the Gibbs process. In the case of pair-wise interaction, this can be obtained by assuming an exponential decay condition on the diameter of connected clusters in a dominating random connection model. This relaxes the non-percolation assumption made in
[
26
]
. As a second step of generalization, we consider thinning functions that depend on the point configuration within a random radius of exponentially decaying size instead of a deterministic radius as in
[
26
]
.

(2)

Geometric functionals of point patterns.
We consider functionals of a point pattern such as the average nearest neighbor distance, Voronoi cell characteristics, and topological summaries called persistent Betti numbers
[
18
]
. We provide normal approximation results for such functionals when applied to a Gibbs point process observed in a large window. Results of this type have a long history for Poisson processes, see e.g.
[
27
,
37
]
, and more recently, results have also appeared for Gibbs point processes
[
2
,
8
,
19
,
39
,
43
]
. We show (i) a qualitative central limit theorem (CLT) requiring minimal assumptions on the stabilization of the functional in increasing windows and on the cluster sizes of a dominating percolation model and (ii) a quantitative CLT measured in the Kolmogorov distance under an additional exponential stabilization condition and assuming exponential tails of cluster diameters in the dominating percolation model.

For both types of problems, the main contribution of this paper is to generalize existing results to a larger class of Gibbs point processes. Since the asymptotic theory of Gibbs point process is a vigorous research stream, we briefly review the existing results in literature. A key challenge for deriving asymptotic results is handling the spatial correlations of the point process. Mainly two techniques have been used in the literature, namely a perfect simulation technique and disagreement coupling. Both seek to control spatial correlations by the clusters of an associated percolation model.

(1)

Perfect simulation.
The idea is to represent the Gibbs process as a thinning of a free birth-death process
[
14
,
15
]
. The application to CLTs for Gibbs processes was pioneered in
[
39
,
43
]
. Later, quantitative Wasserstein bounds were derived for point processes with sufficiently fast correlation decay
[
8
]
, which could then be verified for the Gibbs processes from
[
39
,
43
]
.
While the perfect simulation technique is easy to work with, the arguments are only valid for short-range interactions satisfying that a certain branching process derived from a space-time percolation model is sub-critical.

(2)

Disagreement coupling.
This technique was originally introduced for discrete models
[
41
]
and only recently transferred to the continuum case
[
21
,
20
,
26
]
. It provides explicit couplings between different Gibbs models by thinning a common dominating Poisson process allowing to control the long range effects of locally modifying the model. Disagreement coupling was used in
[
26
]
for Poisson approximation and some early CLTs for U-statistics were obtained in
[
2
]
. More general normal approximation results were obtained in
[
19
]
. There, the Gibbs point processes were restricted to live in Euclidean space and have a finite interaction range satisfying a non-percolation condition on a dominating Boolean model. This relaxes the branching process condition for the perfect simulation technique, but, as we will see in this paper, it is still unnecessarily strict in many cases.

This paper continues the disagreement coupling studies from
[
19
]
and considers two generalizations.

(1)

Models with local interactions.
We consider Gibbs processes in marked Euclidean space satisfying a local interaction property and further assume non-percolation of an associated random graph. Building on the general disagreement coupling framework of
[
26
]
in finite-volume domains, we generalize the constructions of
[
19
]
to show how Gibbs processes in infinite-volume domains can be explicitly constructed by Poisson thinnings and we give quantitative bounds on the effect of locally modifying the model. As a new result compared to
[
19
]
, we give explicit couplings between infinite-volume processes with differing boundary conditions.

(2)

Models with pair potential.
To weaken the percolation restriction and to allow models with infinite interaction range, we take up an idea from
[
3
]
to replace the associated percolation model by a random connection model (RCM), where the connection probabilities depend on the strength of the interaction between pairs of points. In
[
3
]
, this is done by approximating the RCM by models of the form in
[
26
]
. However, for our proofs, we need to set up a version of disagreement coupling acting directly by thinning the clusters of an RCM in both finite- and infinite-volume domains.

To summarize, the main contributions of the present paper is that it is the first to give both quantitative and qualitative Poisson and normal approximation results for a general class of pair potentials satisfying a suitable non-percolation condition, see (
15
) below.

The paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the Gibbs models we consider, and the main theorems are stated in Section
3
with examples of applications given in Section
4
. In Section
5
, we generalize the disagreement coupling results from
[
19
]
to standard Borel spaces, and in Section
6
, we set up a disagreement coupling scheme for the RCM case. In both sections, proofs are deferred to Section
11
and
12
, respectively, allowing the reader to skip them upon first reading. We first give the proof of Theorem
3.3
in Section
7
since this is conceptually simpler. Section
8
is devoted to some common Palm coupling framework for the proofs of Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
, which are then given in Sections
9
and
10
, respectively. Finally, Section
13
contains a proof of a technical lemma used in the proof of Section
10
.

2.
Model and main results

2.1.
Gibbs point processes

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a standard Borel space with Borel sigma-algebra
\mathcal{B}
equipped with a diffuse measure
\lambda
. We assume that there is a sequence
W_{1}\subseteq W_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathbb{X}
such that

(1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}=\bigcup_{n}W_{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda(W_{n})<\infty,\qquad n\geqslant 1.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}
denote the set of
bounded Borel sets
, i.e. sets
B\in\mathcal{B}
with
B\subseteq W_{n}
for some
n\geqslant 1
. Let
\mathbf{N}
denote the space of
locally finite
subsets of
\mathbb{X}
, i.e., sets
\varphi\subseteq\mathbb{X}
such that
\varphi\cap W_{n}
is finite for all
n\geqslant 1
. We sometimes identify elements
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
with their associated counting measures and write
\varphi(B)=|\varphi\cap B|
for the cardinality of
\varphi\cap B
.
We equip
\mathbf{N}
with the smallest sigma-algebra
\mathcal{N}
making the maps
\varphi\mapsto\varphi(B)
measurable for
all
B\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Let
\mathbf{N}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{N}
denote the subspace of finite sets. For
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we let
\mathbf{N}_{B}\subseteq\mathbf{N}
denote the subspace of locally finite sets contained in
B
and for
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
we write
\varphi_{B}:=\varphi\cap B\in\mathbf{N}_{B}
.

A
simple point process
on
\mathbb{X}
is a measurable map

(\Omega,\mathcal{F})\to(\mathbf{N},\mathcal{N})
where
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is some probability space. A Gibbs point process is defined in terms of a
Papangelou intensity
(PI) which is a function
\kappa:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbf{N}\to[0,\infty)
satisfying the so-called
cocycle condition

(2)

\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)\kappa({\bm{y}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})=\kappa({\bm{y}},\varphi)\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{y}}\}).

Definition 2.1
.

A point process
\mathcal{X}
is a
Gibbs point process on
\mathbb{X}
with PI
\kappa
if the
GNZ-equation

[
16
,
35
]

(3)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{X}}f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})\mathcal{X}({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big]=\int_{\mathbb{X}}\mathbb{E}\big[f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})\big]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

holds for any measurable
f:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbf{N}\to[0,\infty)
.
If
Q\subseteq\mathbb{X}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
, then
the Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
on
Q
with boundary conditions
\psi
and PI
\kappa
is a point process on
Q
satisfying

(4)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{Q}f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi))\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big]=\int_{Q}\mathbb{E}\big[f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\cup\psi)\big]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}).

For a more detailed introduction to Gibbs point processes, we refer the reader to
[
4
,
10
]
.
Note that
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
is the same as a Gibbs process on
\mathbb{X}
with PI
\kappa^{\prime}({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi\cup\psi)\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\in Q\}
.
We will assume throughout that
\kappa
is
locally stable
, i.e.

(5)

\sup_{\varphi\in\mathbf{N}}\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)\leqslant 1\quad\text{ for all }{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}.

Remark 2.2
.

In the literature, local stability is usually formulated as

\sup_{\varphi\in\mathbf{N}}\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)\leqslant\alpha({\bm{x}})
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
, for some
\alpha:\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
with

\int_{W_{n}}\alpha({\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})<\infty
for all
n\geqslant 1
. However, changing the reference measure to
\alpha\lambda
and the PI to
\kappa/\alpha
(with
0/0:=0
), we may assume
\alpha=1
.

Local stability ensures that the Gibbs point process is stochastically dominated by a Poisson process of intensity
\lambda
.
Throughout, we let
\mathcal{P}
denote a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{X}
with intensity measure
\lambda
and we let
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
denote its restriction to
Q\in\mathcal{B}
. We refer to
[
29
]
for a comprehensive introduction to Poisson point processes. Suppose
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Define
\tilde{\kappa}(\cdot,\psi):\mathbf{N}_{Q}\to[0,\infty)
by
\tilde{\kappa}(\varnothing,\psi)=1
and

\tilde{\kappa}(\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\},\psi)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\kappa({\bm{x}}_{i},\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{i-1}\}\cup\psi)

where
{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\in Q
are pairwise different points. The cocycle condition (
2
) ensures that
\tilde{\kappa}
does not depend on the ordering of
{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}
.
The
partition function
is given by

Z_{Q}(\psi)=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\kappa}(\mathcal{P}_{Q},\psi)]

As noted in
[
26
]
, the assumptions (
5
) and
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
ensure
0<Z_{Q}(\psi)<\infty
. In this case,
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
has a density with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
given by
\frac{1}{Z_{Q}(\psi)}\tilde{\kappa}(\cdot,\psi)
. In particular,
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
exists and its distribution is unique.

Without the assumption
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, the Gibbs point process is not guaranteed to exist. Moreover, the Gibbs distribution is not necessarily unique as demonstrated for the Widom-Rowlinson model in
[
9
]
. A phase transition between uniqueness and non-uniqueness is believed to occur for most models. Thus, we make some further assumptions to ensure uniqueness. The first is that the PI has
local interactions
, that is,
we assume that there is a symmetric relation
\sim
on
\mathbb{X}
, which is measurable in the sense that
({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\mapsto\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}\}
is measurable. Let
N({\bm{x}})=\{{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{X}\mid{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}\}
denote all neighbors of
{\bm{x}}
with respect to
\sim
. Then, we say that the model has
local interactions
if

(6)

\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi\cap N({\bm{x}})).

We assume
\lambda(N({\bm{x}}))<\infty
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
. Sometimes, we require this to hold uniformly in the sense that

(7)

\int_{W_{n}}\lambda(N({\bm{x}}))\lambda(\,{\rm d}{\bm{x}})<\infty,\qquad n\geqslant 1.

More generally, we write
N(B)=\bigcup_{{\bm{x}}\in B}N({\bm{x}})
for
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

For any
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
, the relation
\sim
defines a graph
G(\varphi)
with vertex set
\varphi
and an edge between
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\varphi
whenever
{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}
. For
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
, we let
C({\bm{x}},\varphi)
be the connected component of
{\bm{x}}
in
G_{{\bm{x}}}(\varphi):=G(\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})
. We refer to the vertices of a connected component as a
cluster
.
We write
A\leftrightsquigarrow_{\varphi}B
if there is a point in
\varphi\cap A
and a point in
\varphi\cap B
that are in the same connected component of
G(\varphi)
.
Intuitively,
C({\bm{x}},\varphi)
captures how far the interactions with
{\bm{x}}
propagate. This motivates the following non-percolation assumption: For
\lambda
-almost all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
,

(8)

\mathbb{P}(|C({\bm{x}},\mathcal{P})|<\infty)=1.

It will often be convenient to reformulate (
8
) as

(9)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(C({\bm{x}},\mathcal{P})\not\subseteq W_{n}\big)=0.

This ensures that the interactions with
{\bm{x}}
do not propagate infinitely far out. Note that
C({\bm{x}},\mathcal{P})
is the connected component of
{\bm{x}}
in the graph
G(\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{x}}})
where
\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{x}}}=\mathcal{P}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\}
denotes the Palm distribution of
\mathcal{P}
at
{\bm{x}}
.
With this assumption, we have the following result from
[
4
, Thm. 9.1]
.

Theorem 2.3

(Existence and uniqueness of Gibbs processes)
.

A Gibbs distribution
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
with PI satisfying (
5
), (
6
) and (
8
) is guaranteed to exist and be unique.

While the above setup is very general, our results will be stated for a more specific setting. We will consider two main scenarios:

Model (L)
. Gibbs models with
local interactions
on marked Euclidean spaces, and

Model (P)
. Gibbs processes with
pair potentials
on marked Euclidean spaces.

Below, we define the two models more precisely.

2.2.
Model (L): Gibbs models with local interactions on marked Euclidean spaces

In the following, we say that
\mathbb{X}
is a
marked Euclidean space
if
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
, where the mark space
\mathbb{M}
is some Polish space equipped with a probability measure
\mu
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is equipped with the Lebesgue measure
\mathsf{Leb}
. In this setting, we choose the sequence
W_{1}\subseteq W_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
to be marked
d
-cubes
W_{n}=[-n/2,n/2]^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
of side length
n
. We write points
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
as
{\bm{x}}=(x,m)
.

Definition 1
.

Model (L) is a Gibbs point process on marked Euclidean space with reference measure
\lambda=\alpha\mathsf{Leb}\otimes\mu
where
\alpha>0
is the
activity
. The PI is assumed to satisfy (
5
), (
6
), (
7
) and (
9
).

For
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the set translation
A+y=\{(x+y,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\mid(x,m)\in A\}
. We say that a PI
\kappa
on marked Euclidean space is
translation-invariant
if

\kappa\big((x,m),{\varphi}\big)=\kappa\big((x+y,m),{\varphi}+y\big)

for all
(x,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
,
{\varphi}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}}
, and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We sometimes need a stronger version of the non-percolation condition (
9
) requiring exponential tails of the spatial diameter of clusters. More precisely, we assume that there are
c_{\mathsf{D},1},c_{\mathsf{D},2}>0
such that

(10)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{diam}(C((x,m),\mathcal{P}))>r\big)\mu({\rm d}m)\leqslant c_{\mathsf{D},1}\exp(-c_{\mathsf{D},2}r).

Here,
\mathsf{diam}(A)
refers to the spatial diameter of the projection to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
.

Sometimes, we will also need to assume exponential tails of the neighborhood of a point in the sense that there are constants
c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{v},2}>0
such that

(11)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{X}\setminus B_{r}({\bm{x}})}\mathbbmss{1}\{(x,m)\sim(y,m^{\prime})\}\lambda({\rm d}(y,m^{\prime}))\mu({\rm d}m)\leqslant c_{\mathsf{v},1}\exp({-c_{\mathsf{v},2}r}),\qquad r>0,

where
B_{r}(x)
is the Euclidean ball of radius
r
around
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for
{\bm{x}}=(x,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
we write
B_{r}({\bm{x}}):=B_{r}(x)\times\mathbb{M}
. Note that (
11
) implies (
7
).

We now discuss specific examples of point processes that satisfy the conditions.

Example 2.4

(Euclidean point processes with finite interaction range)
.

A Gibbs point process on
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
has
finite interaction range

r_{0}>0
if

\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\kappa\big({\bm{x}},\varphi\cap B_{r_{0}}({\bm{x}})\big).

This corresponds to a symmetric relation given by
{\bm{x}}_{1}\sim{\bm{x}}_{2}
if and only if
\|{\bm{x}}_{1}-{\bm{x}}_{2}\|\leqslant r_{0}
. Thus, the condition (
8
) is equivalent to non-percolation of the Boolean model with intensity
\alpha
and balls of radius
r_{0}/2
. Moreover, the criterion (
9
) implies (
10
), see e.g.
[
12
,
45
]
. This was the setting studied in
[
19
]
.

Example 2.5

(Interacting particle models)
.

Another relevant model class is interacting particle processes studied in e.g.
[
2
,
4
]
. Here,
\mathbb{M}
is the family of compact sets having center of gravity at the origin. A pair
(x,C)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
is identified with the particle
x+C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In a model of non-overlapping particles, the PI is given by

\kappa\big((x,C),\varphi\big)=\mathbbmss{1}\bigg\{(x+C)\cap\bigcup_{(y,C^{\prime})\in\varphi}(y+C^{\prime})=\varnothing\bigg\}.

Here, we can take the symmetric relation to be
(x,C)\sim(y,C^{\prime})
if and only if
(x+C)\cap(y+C^{\prime})\neq\varnothing
. The percolation condition (
8
) corresponds to non-percolation of a germ-grain model with grain distribution
\mu
.
It immediately implies (
10
) when grain diameters are bounded
[
45
]
. In the case where the grains are balls of random radii and the radius distribution has exponential tails, (
8
) implies (
10
) by
[
12
]
. However, if the tail probabilities of the radii decay slower than exponentially, (
10
) cannot hold, but the non-percolation condition (
8
) may still hold for low intensities
\alpha

[
17
]
.
Other interacting particle models considered in the literature include the Widom-Rowlinson model
[
42
]
, segment processes
[
4
]
, and the general models considered in
[
2
,
4
]
.

2.3.
Model (P): Gibbs models with pairwise interaction on marked Euclidean space

Some of the most well-studied Gibbs models are models with pairwise interaction, i.e., the PI has the form

\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\exp\bigg(-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\}}v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\bigg),

where the
pair potential

v:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty]
is symmetric in its two arguments. The assumption
v\geqslant 0
is necessary to ensure (
5
) and implies that the process is repulsive at all distances. We remark that there are physically relevant models like the Lennard-Jones potential that do not satisfy this. In Model (P), we always require that

(12)

\int_{\mathbb{X}}\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})<\infty,\qquad{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X},

where

(13)

\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=1-e^{-v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})}.

This ensures existence of the Gibbs process
[
22
]
.
For the proofs, we further need that (
12
) is locally integrable in the sense that

(14)

\int_{W_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})<\infty,\qquad n\geqslant 1.

If the pair potential
v({\bm{x}},\cdot)
has bounded support for every fixed
{\bm{x}}
, one could define a symmetric relation as
{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}
if
v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})>0
and be in the setting of Model (L). However, the associated non-percolation criterion (
9
) would be unnecessarily strict.
Following
[
3
]
, one may instead relate the Gibbs process to a random connection model (RCM) where, rather than adding deterministic edges between
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}
whenever
v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})>0
, edges are only added with a certain probability depending on
v
. This leads to a larger percolation threshold and, moreover, does not require any restrictions on the support of
v
.
To make this idea precise, we first define the RCM.

Definition 2.6

(RCM
\Gamma(Q,\psi)
)
.

For
\varphi,\psi\in\mathbf{N}
, define the random graph
\Gamma(\varphi,\psi)
as the simple graph
with vertex set
\varphi\cup\psi
and edges added independently with probability
\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})

of having an edge between
{\bm{x}}\in\varphi
and
{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\cup\psi
. The
random connection model

\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{Q},\psi)
is the graph with vertex set consisting of the Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
together with a set of fixed points
\psi
. Conditionally on
\mathcal{P}_{Q}=\varphi
it has distribution
\Gamma(\varphi,\psi)
. The distribution of
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{Q},\psi)
is denoted
\Gamma(Q,\psi)
.

For
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
, the density of
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
is given, up to a normalizing constant, by

\tilde{\kappa}(\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{l}\},\psi)=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\exp\bigg(-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{i-1}\}\cup\psi}v({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}})\bigg)=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{i-1}\}\cup\psi}e^{-v({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}})}.

In particular,
\tilde{\kappa}\big(\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{l}\},\psi\big)
equals the probability that
\Gamma\big(\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{l}\},\psi\big)
has no edges. Thus, one may think of
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
as having a density with respect to the RCM
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{Q},\psi)
. The density is proportional to the indicator
\mathbbmss{1}\{\Gamma(Q,\psi)\text{ has no edges}\}
with normalizing constant
{Z}_{Q}(\psi)=\mathbb{P}(\Gamma(Q,\psi)\text{ has no edges}).

This suggests a close relation between the Gibbs process and the RCM, and in fact, in Section
6
we will construct
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
as a subset of the vertices of
\Gamma(Q,\psi)
that have no edges between them.

For
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
, let
\Gamma_{{\bm{x}}}(\mathcal{P},\varnothing):=\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{x}}},\varnothing)
be the RCM of the Palm process at
{\bm{x}}
and let
C\big({\bm{x}},\Gamma_{{\bm{x}}}(\mathcal{P},\varnothing)\big)
denote the connected component of
{\bm{x}}
in
\Gamma_{{\bm{x}}}(\mathcal{P},\varnothing)
. The equivalent of the non-percolation criterion (
9
) for the RCM is that for
\lambda
-almost all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}
,

(15)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}}\Big(C({\bm{x}},\Gamma_{{\bm{x}}}(\mathcal{P},\varnothing))\cap(\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{n})\neq\varnothing\Big)=0.

Theorem 2.7

(Existence and uniqueness of Gibbs processes with pair potential
[
3
,
22
]
)
.

A Gibbs distribution on
\mathbb{X}
with non-negative pair-potential satisfying (
12
) and (
15
) exists and is unique.

Our main results will again be concerned with models on marked Euclidean space, hence we define Model (P) as follows.

Definition 2
.

Model (P) is a Gibbs point process on marked Euclidean space with reference measure
\lambda=\alpha\mathsf{Leb}\otimes\mu
,
\alpha>0
, having a non-negative pair potential satisfying (
12
), (
14
) and (
15
).

In the main Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
below, we further require exponential decay of cluster sizes of the RCM analogous to (
10
). That is, there are
c_{\mathsf{D},1},c_{\mathsf{D},2}>0
such that

(16)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathsf{diam}\big(C((x,m),\Gamma_{(x,m)}(\mathcal{P},\varnothing))\big)>r\Big)\mu({\rm d}m)\leqslant c_{\mathsf{D},1}\exp(-c_{\mathsf{D},2}r).

Conditions for this to hold can be found in
[
6
]
.
We may also assume exponential decay of
\pi
, i.e., there exist
c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{v},2}>0
such that

(17)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\pi((x,m),(y,m^{\prime}))\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime})\leqslant c_{\mathsf{v},1}\exp\big(-c_{\mathsf{v},2}\|x-y\|\big),\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We will sometimes write
\mathcal{X}^{\lambda}(Q,\psi)
and
\Gamma^{\lambda}(Q,\psi)
to emphasize that the reference measure is
\lambda
and
\Gamma(Q,\psi,\pi)
if we wish to emphasize the connection probabilities
\pi
. Note that
\mathcal{X}^{\lambda}(Q,\psi)
has the same distribution as
\mathcal{X}^{\rho_{\psi}\lambda}(Q,\varnothing)
with reference measure
\rho_{\psi}\lambda
where

(18)

\displaystyle\rho_{\psi}({\bm{x}}):=\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\psi}\big(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\big),

since the same GNZ equation is satisfied.

Example 2.8
.

(Strauss model)
A popular model in spatial statistics is the
Strauss process

[
40
]
, which is a point process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with pair potential
v((x,m),(y,m^{\prime}))=-\ln(\gamma)\mathbbmss{1}\{\|x-y\|\leqslant R\}
, where
\gamma\in[0,1)
, and reference measure
\beta\mathsf{Leb}
. The special case
\gamma=0
is the hard-sphere model.

The non-overlapping particle model from Example
2.5
is another example with pairwise interaction. The percolation models arising from considering it as a model of type (L) or (P) coincide. An example of a model that does not have pairwise interactions is the Widom-Rowlinson model. See
[
34
]
for more examples.

Remark 3
.

A different condition for uniqueness of a Gibbs model with non-negative pair potential satisfying (
12
) was given in
[
33
]
. This was based on suitably counting self-avoiding walks in the RCM. The relation to the non-percolation criterion (
16
) is not fully understood, but the authors of
[
33
]
argue that at least in high dimensions, their criterion is better. Under the same criterion, they show a strong spatial mixing property in
[
32
]
. It is possible that this mixing condition suffice to derive some CLTs, however, the strong results presented here crucially rely on the coupling constructions in Section
5
and
6
available only under (
16
).

3.
Main results and conditions

In this section, we present the three main results of the paper: a Poisson approximation result in Theorem
3.1
, a quantitative CLT in Theorem
3.2
, and a qualitative CLT in Theorem
3.3
. All results hold for Gibbs models of type (L) and (P). In particular,
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
. Each result will have some additional assumptions on the Gibbs process as well as on the functional. Examples of functionals where the conditions can be verified are given in Section
4
.

Both Theorem
3.1
and
3.2
involve sums of stabilizing scores. We first give the common definitions. A
score function
is a measurable map
g:\mathbb{X}\times{\mathbf{N}}\to[0,\infty)
. Given a score function, we will consider the random measure

(19)

\displaystyle\Xi:=\Xi[\mathcal{X}]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})\,\delta_{\bm{x}},

where
\delta_{\bm{x}}
denotes the Dirac measure at
{\bm{x}}
. The
intensity measure
of
\Xi
is

(20)

\displaystyle\Lambda(A):=\mathbb{E}[\Xi(A)],\qquad A\in\mathcal{B}.

We assume that
g
is
locally stabilizing
, i.e. that there is a
stabilization radius

R({\bm{x}},\varphi)
such that for all
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
and
{\bm{x}}\in\varphi
, we have

g({\bm{x}},\varphi)=g\big({\bm{x}},\varphi\cap B_{R({\bm{x}},\varphi)}({\bm{x}})\big).

The stabilization radius must satisfy the stopping property

(21)

\big\{R({\bm{x}},\varphi)\leqslant r\big\}=\big\{R({\bm{x}},\varphi\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}}))\leqslant r\big\}

for all
r>0
.
Finally, we impose that the score function is
exponentially stabilizing
when applied to the Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}
, i.e. the stabilization radius has exponential tails in the sense that there are
c_{\mathsf{es},1},c_{\mathsf{es},2}>0
such that

(22)

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant k\leqslant 5\\
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}^{k}}\hskip-5.69046pt\mathbb{P}\big(R((x_{1},m_{1}),\mathcal{X}\cup\{(x_{1},m_{1}),\ldots,(x_{k},m_{k})\})>r\big)\mu({\rm d}m_{1})\cdots\mu({\rm d}m_{k})\leqslant c_{\mathsf{es},1}\exp(-rc_{\mathsf{es},2}).

We further assume that
g
satisfies the following
hereditary
property. For all
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{X}
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
,

(23)

\displaystyle g({\bm{x}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad g\big({\bm{x}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\}\big)=0.

We will assume that
\Xi
is observed in a window
Q_{n}:=[-n/2,n/2]^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
for some
n\geqslant 1
such that
\lambda(Q_{n})=\alpha n^{d}
. This window shape is chosen for convenience. The proof techniques can easily be adapted to other window shapes, see
[
19
,
37
]
, but one has to be more careful about boundary effects.

3.1.
Poisson approximation

If the score function
g
takes values in
\{0,1\}
, then the random measure
\Xi
in (
19
) becomes a new point process, which is a thinning of
\mathcal{X}
.
Our Poisson approximation result gives an upper bound on the distance between
\Xi\cap Q_{n}
and a Poisson process on
Q_{n}
having the same intensity measure
\Lambda
as
\Xi
.
We formulate our result in the
Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance
between two point processes
\xi
and
\zeta
on
Q_{n}
, defined by

(24)

\displaystyle{d_{\mathsf{KR}}}(\xi,\zeta):=\sup_{h\in\text{Lip}}\big|\mathbb{E}[h(\xi)]-\mathbb{E}[h(\zeta)]\big|.

Here,
Lip
is the class of functions
\mathbf{N}_{Q_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}
which are 1-Lipschitz with respect to the total variation distance on
\mathbf{N}_{Q_{n}}
(considered as a space of counting measures) given by

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\varphi,\psi)=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B},A\subseteq Q_{n}}|\varphi(A)-\psi(A)|,\qquad\varphi,\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{Q_{n}}.

Having introduced this terminology, we now state the Poisson approximation result.

Theorem 3.1

(Poisson approximation for dependent thinning of Gibbs processes)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an infinite-volume Gibbs point process of Model (L) or (P) satisfying conditions (
10
) and (
11
) or (
16
) and (
17
), respectively. Assume
\mathbb{M}
is locally compact and
that
g_{n}
is a
\{0,1\}
-valued exponentially stabilizing score function, i.e. satisfying (
21
), (
22
), and (
23
). Let
\Xi_{n}
be the corresponding point process (
19
) and let
\mathcal{M}_{n}
be a Poisson process on
\mathbb{X}
with the same intensity measure
\Lambda_{n}
given by (
20
).
Then,

\displaystyle d_{KR}(\Xi_{n}\cap Q_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}\cap Q_{n})\leqslant c_{\mathsf{poi}}\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1}+2\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,x}\cap S_{n,y}\neq\varnothing\big\}\Lambda_{n}({\rm d}x)\Lambda_{n}({\rm d}y)

\displaystyle\,+2\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\big\}\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\})g_{n}({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{y}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{y}}\})\kappa({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})\Big]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}),

where
S_{n,{\bm{x}}}=B_{3c_{\mathsf{S}t}\log n}({\bm{x}})
with
c_{\mathsf{S}t}:=14\max(c_{\mathsf{es},2},c_{\mathsf{v},2},c_{\mathsf{D},2})
and
c_{\mathsf{poi}}=c_{\mathsf{poi}}(d,\alpha,c_{\mathsf{es},1},c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{D},1})
.

While the right-hand side of the bound in Theorem
3.1
is more involved than the corresponding result for Poisson approximation of Poisson functionals (see
[
5
]
), it exhibits a similar structure. The first term puts a bound on long range effects due to exponential stabilization and spatial interactions in the Gibbs process. Noting that
\Lambda_{n}({\rm d}{\bm{x}})=\mathbb{E}[g_{n}({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})
, the first integral on the right-hand side controls the first-order contributions of the score function
g_{n}
, while the last term controls its local second-order contributions. More specifically, if the second integral vanishes asymptotically as
n\to\infty
, this implies that the local influence of an atom
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
with
g_{n}({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})=1
on the score value
g_{n}({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})
of other atoms
{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{X}
with
S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing
is asymptotically negligible.

The proof reveals that the
c_{\mathsf{poi}}\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1}
term in Theorem
3.1
can be made to be of order
\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m}
,
m\geqslant 1
, by choosing
c_{\mathsf{S}t}
larger. However, it is usually the two integrals that determine the rate when
n\to\infty
.
In Section
4.1
, we discuss an example where the rate of the integrals can be determined. Theorem
3.1
generalizes the Poisson approximation results from
[
26
]
in several key ways. First, we extend the class of Gibbs models where, instead of assuming subcriticality of an underlying random geometric graph as in Model (L), we impose the weaker condition of exponential decay for the underlying RCM, as specified in (
16
). Second, we relax the stabilization condition on
g_{n}
, which in
[
26
]
was required to have a deterministic stabilization radius.

3.2.
Quantitative CLT for exponentially stabilizing functionals

Our second main result is a quantitative normal approximation result in the spirit of
[
7
]
for functionals of the form
\Xi(Q_{n})
. In this section, score functions are no longer restricted to be
\{0,1\}
-valued.
We impose the following bounded moment condition.

(25)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geqslant 1}\sup_{k\leqslant 5}\sup_{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{k}}\big[g_{n}((x_{1},m_{1}),\mathcal{X}\cup\{(x_{1},m_{1}),\dots,(x_{k},m_{k})\})^{6}\big]\mu({\rm d}m_{1})\cdots\mu({\rm d}m_{k})=:c_{\mathsf{m}}<\infty.

The results are given in terms of the
Kolmogorov distance
which is given for two real-valued stochastic variables
X
and
Y
as

d_{\mathsf{K}}(X,Y)=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}(X\leqslant u)-\mathbb{P}(Y\leqslant u)|.

Theorem 3.2

(Quantitative normal approximation of Gibbsian score sums)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an infinite-volume Gibbs point process from Model (L) satisfying (
10
) and (
11
) or from Model (P) satisfying (
16
) and (
17
). Assume that
g_{n}
is an exponentially stabilizing score function satisfying (
21
), (
22
), (
23
), and (
25
). Then, for
n\geqslant 1
,
\Xi_{n}(Q_{n})
defined at (
19
) satisfies

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{K}}\bigg({\frac{\Xi_{n}(Q_{n})-\Lambda_{n}(Q_{n})}{\sqrt{\mathsf{Var}(\Xi_{n}(Q_{n}))}}},N(0,1)\bigg)\leqslant c_{\mathsf{norm}}\frac{\lambda(Q_{n})(\log\lambda(Q_{n}))^{2d}}{\mathsf{Var}({\Xi_{n}(Q_{n})})^{3/2}},

where
N(0,1)
is a standard normal variable and
c_{\mathsf{norm}}=c_{\mathsf{norm}}(d,\alpha,c_{\mathsf{m}},c_{\mathsf{es},1},c_{\mathsf{es},2},c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{v},2},c_{\mathsf{D},1},c_{\mathsf{D},2})
.

This result is comparable to
[
19
, Thm. 2.4]
with the main novelty being the generalization to larger classes of Gibbs processes. The logarithmic factor in the bound is usually found in the Gibbs literature
[
39
,
43
]
, but we remark that it can be avoided in the Poisson case
[
27
]
and it is open whether this is also true in the Gibbs case.

3.3.
Qualitative CLT for weakly stabilizing functionals

We finally give a qualitative CLT for a Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
with translation-invariant PI. We consider a functional
H:\mathbf{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
which is

translation-invariant
, i.e.
H(\varphi+y)=H(\varphi)
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
. For
n\geqslant 1
, we let
H_{n}
denote the functional

(26)

H_{n}(\varphi)=H(\varphi\cap Q_{n}),\qquad\varphi\in\mathbf{N}.

The CLT requires a
weak stabilization
condition. Let
Q_{z,n}:=Q_{n}+z
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We require that almost surely,

(27)

H(\mathcal{X})-H(\mathcal{X}\setminus Q_{l}):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(H(\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{w_{n},m_{n}})-H((\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{w_{n},m_{n}})\setminus Q_{l})\big)

exists for all
l\geqslant 0
and all sequences
m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and
w_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigcup_{k\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant k}Q_{w_{n},m_{n}}
. Note that the limit is necessarily independent of the sequence
Q_{w_{n},m_{n}}
.

We also need a moment condition. Since the proof relies on a general CLT for sums of martingale differences, this condition is stated in terms of these martingale differences. Let
Z_{n+\sqrt{n}}
be the set of lattice points
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
Q_{z,1}
intersects the window
Q_{n+\sqrt{n}}
. Then,
Q_{n+\sqrt{n}}\subseteq\bigcup_{z\in Z_{n+\sqrt{n}}}Q_{z,1}
. Order
Z_{n+\sqrt{n}}
lexicographically as
z_{1},\dots,z_{k_{n}}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{0,n}
be the trivial
\sigma
-algebra and
\mathcal{F}_{k_{n},n}=\sigma(\mathcal{X})
. Moreover, define

\mathcal{F}_{i,n}=\mathcal{F}_{z_{i}}:=\sigma\Big(\mathcal{X}\cap\bigcup_{{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},z\preceq z_{i}}}Q_{z,1}\Big),\qquad 0<i<k_{n},

where
\preceq
denotes lexicographical ordering on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Then,
\mathcal{F}_{i,0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\subseteq\dotsm\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{k_{n},n}
is an increasing sequence of
\sigma
-algebras containing information about
\mathcal{X}
in increasing domains of
\mathbb{X}
. In particular,
\mathbb{E}\big[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{F}_{i,n}\big]
define a martingale for fixed
n
. We require the following moment condition on the martingale differences.

(28)

\sup_{n\geqslant 1}\sup_{z_{i}\in Z_{n+\sqrt{n}}}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\mathbb{E}\big[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{F}_{i,n}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{F}_{i-1,n}\big]\big)^{4}\big]<\infty.

Theorem 3.3

(CLT for translation-invariant functionals)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an infinite-volume Gibbs point process of Model (L) or Model (P) with translation-invariant PI. Let
H
be a translation-invariant functional and define
H_{n}
as in (
26
). Assume that
H
is weakly stabilizing (
27
) and the conditional moment condition (
28
) holds.
Then, the limit

\Delta_{0}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}\big[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{F}_{0}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{F}_{0_{-}}\big]\right)

exists in
L^{2}
, where
0_{-}
is the point before 0 in the lexicographical ordering of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Let
\sigma^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{0}^{2}]
. Then,

\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1}\mathsf{Var}(H_{n}(\mathcal{X}))\to\sigma^{2}

and

\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1/2}\left(H_{n}(\mathcal{X})-\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})]\right)\to N(0,\sigma^{2})

in distribution.

The weak stabilization condition is generally weaker than exponential stabilization, see the discussion in
[
19
]
. Moreover, note that we do not rely on any assumptions on the tails of the spatial cluster size distribution in the associated percolation model. The price for this is that the moment condition is more involved. To verify it, it will often be convenient to make assumptions on the spatial cluster sizes. More easily checkable conditions that imply (
28
) are given in
[
19
]
under the additional assumption of exponential tails of cluster sizes (
10
) or (
16
), see Section
4.4
for a discussion of the general case. Positivity of the limiting variance
\sigma^{2}
is not guaranteed, see
[
19
]
for an example of how to verify this.

4.
Examples

In this section, we present specific examples of functionals to which our main results, Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
, and
3.3
, apply. In
[
19
]
, we discussed a number of examples for the case of finite-range interaction. Since we succeeded in proving Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
under essentially the same stabilization and moment conditions as in the finite-range case from
[
19
]
, also the examples carry over. To avoid redundancy, we keep the presentation here short and refer to
[
19
]
for details. One important difference is that
[
19
]
does not investigate the question of Poisson approximation, i.e., our Theorem
3.1
. Hence, we will be more detailed here.

An additional assumption in many of the examples in
[
19
]
is
Poisson-likeness
. Following
[
39
]
, a Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called Poisson-like if (i) it is stochastically dominated by a homogeneous Poisson point process, and (ii) there exist
c_{\mathsf{PL}},r_{1}>0
such that for all
r\geqslant r_{1},\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}
, it holds that

(29)

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{B_{r}(x)}=\varnothing\mid\mathcal{X}_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}=\varphi)\leqslant e^{-c_{\mathsf{PL}}r^{d}}.

Poisson-likeness was shown in
[
19
, Proposition A.3]
under a very mild condition for processes with finite interaction range. For Model (P) with infinite interaction range, Poisson-likeness does not hold in general because the left hand side of (
29
) can typically be made arbitrarily small by adding more points to
\varphi
. This is solved in
[
39
]
by assuming a hard-core radius
r_{0}>0
such that
v(x,y)=\infty
whenever
\|x-y\|\leqslant r_{0}
. This ensures that
\varphi
cannot be too dense, and Poisson-likeness was shown in
[
39
, Lemma 3.3]
under this condition. A full study of when Poisson-likeness holds is beyond the scope of this article, and we will leave it as an assumption in the examples below.

4.1.
Example for Theorem
3.1
: Isolated points

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Poisson-like infinite-volume Gibbs point process of Model (P) on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We consider the thinning
\Xi_{n}
of
\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{n}
consisting of those points
x\in\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{n}
for which the distance to the nearest neighbor in
\mathcal{X}
exceeds
u_{n}(x)
for some measurable function
u_{n}:Q_{n}\to[0,\infty)
. The process
\Xi_{n}
can also be understood as a Matérn type I thinning of
\Xi_{n}
(see
[
26
, Section 10]
). We can write
\Xi_{n}
in the form (
19
) by letting

g_{n}(x,\mathcal{X})=\mathbbmss{1}\{(\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\})\cap B_{u_{n}}(x)=\varnothing\}.

Choose
u_{n}
so large that

(30)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing)\leqslant\frac{\beta_{2}}{\lambda(Q_{n})}.

for some positive constant
\beta_{2}
. This is clearly possible because of Poisson-likeness (
29
).

The hereditary property (
23
) obviously holds. Moreover,
R(x,\mathcal{X})=d(x,\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\})
, where is
d(x,\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\})
is the distance from
x
to the closest point in
\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\}
, is a stabilization radius independent of
n
satisfying the stopping condition (
21
). The stabilization condition (
22
) follows from Poisson-likeness of
\mathcal{X}
, since

\mathbb{P}(R(x,\mathcal{X})>r)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{r}(x)=\varnothing)\leqslant e^{-c_{\mathsf{PL}}r^{d}},\quad x\in Q_{n}.

We now apply Theorem
3.1
. Note that (
30
) implies

\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x\})\Big]=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing)\leqslant\frac{\beta_{2}}{\lambda(Q_{n})},\quad x\in Q_{n}.

Since
\kappa\leqslant 1
, the first integral term on the right-hand side in Theorem
3.1
is bounded by

\displaystyle\iint\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,x}\cap S_{n,y}\neq\varnothing\big\}\Lambda_{n}^{2}({\rm d}x,{\rm d}y)

\displaystyle\quad=\alpha^{2}\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,x}\cap S_{n,y}\neq\varnothing\big\}\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x\})\kappa(x,\mathcal{X})\Big]\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}(y,\mathcal{X}\cup\{y\})\kappa(y,\mathcal{X})\Big]{\rm d}y{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\frac{\alpha^{2}\beta_{2}^{2}}{\lambda(Q_{n})^{2}}\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,x}\cap S_{n,y}\neq\varnothing\big\}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y\in O\Big(\frac{(\log\lambda(Q_{n}))^{d}}{\lambda(Q_{n})}\Big).

Before bounding the second integral term from Theorem
3.1
, we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x,y\})g_{n}(y,\mathcal{X}\cup\{y\})\kappa(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{y\})\kappa(y,\mathcal{X})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing,\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(y)}(y)=\varnothing\Big)\mathbbmss{1}\{\|x-y\|\geqslant\max\{u_{n}(x),u_{n}(y)\}\}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing,\mathcal{X}\cap B_{\frac{u_{n}(y)}{2}}\Big(y+\frac{u_{n}(y)}{2}\frac{y-x}{\|y-x\|}\Big)=\varnothing\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing\Big)\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{\frac{u_{n}(y)}{2}}\Big(y+\frac{u_{n}(y)}{2}\frac{y-x}{\|y-x\|}\Big)=\varnothing\Big|\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing\Big).

Since the two balls considered in the last line are disjoint, we obtain from Poisson-likeness that the conditional probability in the last display is bounded by

\exp(-c_{\mathsf{PL}}(u_{n}(y)/2)^{d})
.
Since
\mathcal{X}
can be realized as a thinning of
\mathcal{P}
(see e.g. Proposition
6.16
below),

e^{-\alpha\kappa_{d}u_{n}(y)^{d}}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}\cap B_{u_{n}(y)}(y)=\varnothing)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(y)}(y)=\varnothing)\leqslant\frac{\beta_{2}}{\lambda(Q_{n})}.

It follows that

\exp(-c_{\mathsf{PL}}(u_{n}(y)/2)^{d})\leqslant(\beta_{2}/\lambda(Q_{n}))^{c_{\mathsf{PL}}/(2^{d}\alpha\kappa_{d})}.

This gives

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,x}\cap S_{n,y}\neq\varnothing\big\}\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x,y\})g_{n}(y,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x,y\})\kappa(x,\mathcal{X}\cup\{y\})\kappa(y,\mathcal{X}\cup\{x\})\Big]{\rm d}x{\rm d}y

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\Big(\frac{\beta_{2}}{\lambda(Q_{n})}\Big)^{\frac{c_{\mathsf{PL}}}{2^{d}\alpha\kappa_{d}}}\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{S_{n,x}\cap S_{n,y}\neq\varnothing\big\}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap B_{u_{n}(x)}(x)=\varnothing){\rm d}x{\rm d}y\in O\Big(\frac{(\log\lambda(Q_{n}))^{d}}{\lambda(Q_{n})^{c_{\mathsf{PL}}/(2^{d}\alpha\kappa_{d})}}\Big).

Hence, we conclude from Theorem
3.1
that

{d_{\mathsf{KR}}}(\Xi_{n}\cap Q_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}\cap Q_{n})\in O\Big(\frac{(\log\lambda(Q_{n}))^{d}}{\lambda(Q_{n})^{c_{\mathsf{PL}}/(2^{d}\alpha\kappa_{d})}}\Big).

4.2.
Example for Theorem
3.2
: Edge lengths in
k
-nearest neighbor graph

In the
k
-nearest neighbor (kNN) graph with vertices in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, connections are drawn from any given vertex to the
k
other vertices that are closest in Euclidean distance. More precisely, given a locally finite vertex set
\varphi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
an edge is drawn between two points
x,y\in\varphi
if
x
is one of the
k
-nearest neighbors of
y
or the other way around. Normal approximation for the total edge length of kNN graphs with vertices on a Gibbs point process was considered in
[
19
, Section 3.1]
and
[
8
, Theorem 4.1]
. Formally, we can put this example in the setting of score functions by setting
g(x,\varphi)
to be one half of the length of all kNN edges incident to
x
. Then, we consider the total length given as

\displaystyle H^{n}(\varphi):=\sum_{x\in\varphi\cap Q_{n}}g(x,\varphi).

To apply our normal approximation result Theorem
3.2
, we need to verify the stabilization and moment conditions (
22
) and (
25
). These conditions are identical to the finite-range case from
[
19
]
, so assuming Poisson-likeness, no additional work is needed. The exponential decay of the percolation probabilities was automatic in the setting of
[
19
]
, but this is an assumption in general. In the case of Model (P), we refer to
[
6
]
for a more detailed discussion.

4.3.
Example for Theorem
3.2
with marks: Edge counts in Gilbert graphs

Example
4.2
considers a situation of quantitative normal approximation involving unmarked Gibbs processes. Now, we discuss an example illustrating the marked case. Here, the mark space is given by
\mathbb{M}:=[0,\infty)
and the marks are interpreted as random radii. Given a locally finite set
\varphi\subseteq\mathbb{X}
, we connect
{\bm{x}}=(x,m)
and
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},m^{\prime})
by an edge if
\|x-y\|\leqslant m+m^{\prime}
. Then, we consider the functional given by the number of edges in the associated graph. Similarly as in Example
4.2
, we can define the score function
g({\bm{x}},\varphi)
to be the number of neighbors in the graph that have a smaller mark than
{\bm{x}}
. Then, we consider the total edge count given as

\displaystyle H^{n}(\varphi):=\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\varphi\cap Q_{n}}g({\bm{x}},\varphi).

The stabilization radius can be chosen as twice the mark, i.e.,
R\big((x,m),\varphi\big)=2m
.
For Gibbs processes whose PI does not depend on the marks, the resulting Gibbs process will be an independently marked Gibbs process. Hence, we can treat the percolation conditions in the same way as in Example
4.2
. Moreover, for the stabilization condition (
22
), it is sufficient to assume an exponential tail of the original mark distribution. Finally, the moment condition (
25
) follows since the Gibbs process is dominated by a Poisson point process which has finite moments of any order.

4.4.
Example for Theorem
3.3
: Persistent Betti numbers

Persistent Betti numbers
\beta_{q}^{r,s}
,
0<r<s
and
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, are summaries of point processes coming from topological data analysis. We will not give the details here, but see
[
13
]
for a general introduction to topological data analysis and
[
18
]
for the definition of persistent Betti numbers. CLTs for Betti numbers of Poisson processes were first given in
[
18
,
44
]
. In
[
19
, Sec. 3.1]
, this was extended to the Gibbs processes in Example
2.4
. We can generalize these results to other processes of Model (L) as well as Model (P). The weak stabilization condition (
27
) holds deterministically
[
18
]
, so Theorem
3.3
applies if we can verify the moment condition (
28
). This was done in
[
19
]
using only the exponential tails of the cluster diameters for the Boolean model. Carefully inspecting the proof, one finds that it suffices that
\mathbb{P}_{o}(o\leftrightsquigarrow Q_{n}^{c})
is of order
O(n^{-20d-5-\varepsilon})
, where
o
denotes the origin. Hence, the proof also holds in the case of pairwise interactions when the clusters of the RCM satisfy the analogous criterion. Poisson-likeness was not an assumption, but the variance positivity proof in
[
19
]
assumed finite interaction range.

5.
Disagreement coupling

The key technical tool of our proofs is disagreement coupling. This technique explicitly constructs a Gibbs process as a thinning of its dominating Poisson process. Moreover, it allows Gibbs processes with different boundary conditions to be coupled to the same Poisson process in such a way that they disagree only on specific clusters of the corresponding percolation model. This section is devoted to setting up the framework for Model (L) while Section
6
considers the generalization to Model (P).

We rely on the basic disagreement coupling framework from
[
26
]
for Gibbs processes on bounded domains, which we recall in Section
5.1
. Section
5.2
gives bounds on the disagreement between Gibbs processes with locally perturbed PIs. In Section
5.3
, we show how to extend disagreement coupling to unbounded domains. Most proofs are very similar to those from
[
19
]
for the models in Example
2.4
with the only difference being that
\mathbb{R}^{d}
must be replaced by
\mathbb{X}
. Hence, the proof for those direct analogs are omitted.
Proofs of new results and proofs where the arguments substantially differ from
[
19
]
are deferred to Section
11
. While our applications are to marked Euclidean spaces, the theory is set up in an arbitrary Polish space
\mathbb{X}
and specialized to the marked Euclidean case only when necessary.

5.1.
Couplings with a Poisson process

In this section, we consider the construction of a Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
on a bounded domain
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
with boundary conditions
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
given by thinning a Poisson process with intensity measure
\lambda
. We first review the basic version of the algorithm from
[
26
, Sec. 5]
in Section
5.1.1
. As in
[
19
]
, this will be the building block for the more flexible disagreement coupling algorithm introduced in Section
5.1.2
.

Henceforth, for
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we let
B^{*}=B\times[0,1]
. We let
\mathcal{P}^{*}
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{X}^{*}
with intensity measure
\lambda\times\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}
, where
\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}
denotes Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
. We write
\mathcal{P}
for the projection of
\mathcal{P}^{*}
onto the
\mathbb{X}
coordinate, which is a Poisson process on
\mathbb{X}
with intensity measure
\lambda
. More generally, we use
\varphi^{*}
to denote elements of
\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{*}}
and
\varphi
denotes the projection of
\varphi^{*}
to the
\mathbb{X}
-coordinate. For
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we let
\varphi^{*}_{B}=\varphi^{*}\cap B^{*}
and
\varphi_{B}=\varphi\cap B
.

5.1.1.
The standard Poisson embedding

Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. In particular,
Q
has finite volume
\lambda(Q)<\infty
. We recall the definition of the thinning map
T_{Q,\psi}:\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}\to\mathbf{N}_{Q}
from
[
26
]
in Definition
5.1
below. For
\varphi^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}
, the set
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})
will be a thinning of
\varphi
in the sense that
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})\subseteq\varphi
. The last coordinate of a point in
\varphi^{*}
is only used to decide whether or not to keep the point in the thinning.
The key property of
T_{Q,\psi}
is that when applying it to a Poisson process
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}
we obtain the Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
, that is,
T_{Q,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
, where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}
denotes equality in distribution. We refer to
T_{Q,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
as the
standard Poisson embedding
, since it provides an embedding
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{Q}
. We call it standard because it will be the main ingredient for other Poisson embeddings introduced in later sections under the name disagreement couplings.

To define
T_{Q,\psi}
, we need an injective measurable map
\iota\colon Q\to(0,1)
, which is guaranteed to exist in any standard Borel space. We write
T_{Q,\psi,\iota}
when we wish to emphasize that
T_{Q,\psi}
depends on
\iota
. The map
\iota
defines a total ordering
\leqslant_{\iota}
on
Q
. We sometimes refer to the map
\iota
itself as an ordering.
To construct
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})
, we go through the points of
\varphi
in the order given by
{\iota}
and decide for each point whether or not to keep it in the thinned process. For
{\bm{x}}\in Q
, let

\displaystyle Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}{}:=\{{\bm{y}}\in Q\colon{\bm{y}}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}\},\quad Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}{}:=\{{\bm{y}}\in Q\colon{\bm{y}}>_{\iota}{\bm{x}}\},

\displaystyle Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}}]}{}:=\{{\bm{y}}\in Q\colon{\bm{y}}\leqslant_{\iota}{\bm{x}}\},\quad Q_{[{\bm{x}},\infty)}{}:=\{{\bm{y}}\in Q\colon{\bm{y}}\geqslant_{\iota}{\bm{x}}\}.

For
{\bm{x}}\in Q
and
\psi\subseteq Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}
, define the retention probability

(31)

\displaystyle p({\bm{x}},Q,\psi):=\kappa({\bm{x}},\psi)\frac{Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}\big(\psi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\}\big)}{Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}(\psi)}.

It is shown in
[
26
, Lem. 4.1]
that under Assumption (
5
),
0\leqslant p({\bm{x}},Q,\psi)\leqslant 1
.

We now have all the ingredients needed for an algorithmic definition of the standard Poisson embedding.

Definition 5.1

(Standard Poisson embedding)
.

Let
\varphi^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}
. Then,
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})=\{{\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2},\dots\}\subseteq\varphi
is the point set obtained by the following algorithm.

\bullet

To define the first point
{\bm{x}}_{1}:={\bm{x}}_{1}(\varphi^{*})
, consider the set

(32)

\big\{{\bm{x}}\in Q\colon({\bm{x}},u)\in\varphi^{*}\text{ and }u\leqslant p({\bm{x}},Q,\psi)\big\}.

We choose
{\bm{x}}_{1}
to be the smallest element in (
32
) with respect to the
\iota
-ordering. If (
32
) is empty, then the construction terminates with
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})=\varnothing
.

\bullet

Otherwise, we proceed inductively. Suppose, we have defined the points
{\bm{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{x}}_{k}
. This will be the restriction of
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})
to
Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}}_{k}]}
. It remains to define
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})
on
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)}
. We define
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}={\bm{x}}_{k+1}(\varphi^{*})
to be the
\iota
-smallest element of the set

(33)

\big\{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)}\colon({\bm{x}},u)\in\varphi^{*}\text{ and }u\leqslant p({\bm{x}},Q,\psi\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{k}\})\big\}.

\bullet

If (
33
) is empty, the construction terminates with
T_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})=\{{\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2},\ldots,{\bm{x}}_{k}\}
.

The key result of
[
26
, Thm. 5.1]
is that the thinned process
T_{Q,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
is the Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

Proposition 5.2

(Correctness of the Poisson embedding
[
26
]
)
.

Assume that the PI satisfies (
5
). Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Then,
T_{Q,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{Q}
is distributed as the Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

5.1.2.
Disagreement coupling in finite-volume domains

The standard Poisson embedding defines a coupling between
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
and
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
for any choice of boundary conditions
\psi
, but it is not clear how different choices of
\psi
affect the embeddings of
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
. In this section, we modify the standard Poisson embedding to define
disagreement coupling
, which provides more flexible ways of constructing Gibbs processes as Poisson thinnings. The name disagreement coupling refers to one of the main uses, namely obtaining a coupling between Gibbs processes
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
and
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi^{\prime})
with differing boundary conditions
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
such that the two Gibbs processes only disagree near
\psi\Delta\psi^{\prime}
, where
\Delta
denotes the symmetric set difference. We first describe the general framework for constructing disagreement couplings in Definition
5.3
and then introduce the specific version we are going to use in Definition
5.5
. The construction is the immediate generalization of the one in
[
19
]
to general spaces
\mathbb{X}
.

For the definition, we need the notion of a
stopping set
. A stopping set
S
on a Borel set
Q\subseteq\mathbb{X}
assigns to each
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{Q}
a Borel set
S(\varphi)\subseteq Q
. We require that the map
({\bm{x}},\varphi)\mapsto\mathbbmss{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}\in S(\varphi)\}}
is measurable and that
S
has the property that
S(\varphi)
depends only on
\varphi\cap S(\varphi)
, i.e.
S(\varphi)=S(\varphi\cap S(\varphi))
for all
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{Q}
. For instance,
S(\varphi)=Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}}]}
, where
{\bm{x}}
is the
\iota
-smallest point of
\varphi
, is a stopping set. We write
S=S(\varphi)
when
\varphi
is clear from the context. A key property of a stopping set is the following, see e.g.
[
28
, Thm. A.3]
: If
S
is a stopping set on
Q
and
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
is a Poisson processes on
Q
with intensity measure
\lambda
, then

(34)

\text{conditionally on }\mathcal{P}_{S(\mathcal{P}_{Q})},\,\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus S(\mathcal{P}_{Q})}\text{ is a Poisson process on $Q\setminus S(\mathcal{P}_{Q})$ with intensity $\lambda_{Q\setminus S(\mathcal{P}_{Q})}$.}

To define disagreement coupling, we need the following ingredients:

\bullet

A sequence
S_{0}^{*},S_{1}^{*},S_{2}^{*},\dots
of stopping sets on
Q^{*}
of the form
S_{n}^{*}=S_{n}\times[0,1]
such that for any
\varphi^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}
,

S_{0}^{*}(\varphi^{*})=\varnothing\subseteq S_{1}^{*}(\varphi^{*})\subseteq S_{2}^{*}(\varphi^{*})\subseteq\cdots\quad\text{ and }\quad\bigcup_{n}S_{n}^{*}(\varphi^{*})=Q^{*}.

\bullet

A family of injective orderings
\iota_{n}:=\iota_{n}^{\varphi^{*}}:Q\setminus S_{n}(\varphi^{*})\to\mathbb{R}
defined for
n\geqslant 0
and
\varphi^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}
such that
\iota_{n}^{\varphi^{*}}
depends only on
\varphi^{*}
via
\varphi^{*}\cap S_{n}^{*}(\varphi^{*})
. Moreover, we require

(35)

\iota_{n}({\bm{x}})<\iota_{n}({\bm{y}})\quad\text{ for all }\quad{\bm{x}}\in S_{n+1}\setminus S_{n},\,{\bm{y}}\in Q\setminus S_{n+1}.

We extend
\iota_{n}
to a map
\tilde{\iota}_{n}:=\tilde{\iota}_{n}^{\varphi^{*}}:Q\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
by setting
\tilde{\iota}_{n}^{\varphi^{*}}({\bm{x}})=-\infty
if
{\bm{x}}\in S_{n}(\varphi^{*})
and require that the map
({\bm{x}},\varphi^{*})\mapsto\tilde{\iota}^{\varphi^{*}}_{n}({\bm{x}})
is measurable as a function
Q\times\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
.

We think of the stopping sets as providing a step-wise exploration of
Q
, where the set
S_{n}(\varphi^{*})\setminus S_{n-1}(\varphi^{*})
is explored in step
n
.
In disagreement coupling, the point pattern
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{Q}
is thinned during this exploration, that is, the points
\varphi\cap(S_{n}(\varphi^{*})\setminus S_{n-1}(\varphi^{*}))
are thinned in step
n
. For the definition, recall that we write
T_{Q,\psi,\iota}
to highlight the dependence of the thinning map
T_{Q,\psi}
on the ordering
\iota
.

Definition 5.3

(Disagreement coupling)
.

Let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Disagreement coupling is a map
T^{\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\psi}:\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}\to\mathbf{N}_{Q}
. The thinning
T^{\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*})\subseteq\varphi
of a point pattern
\varphi^{*}
is constructed inductively in
n
by constructing the thinned point pattern
\xi_{n}:=\xi_{n}(\varphi^{*})
on the set
S_{n}\setminus S_{n-1}
as follows:

1.

First, set
\chi_{1}:=\chi_{1}(\varphi^{*}):=T_{Q,\psi,\iota_{0}}(\varphi^{*})
. Let
\xi_{1}:=\chi_{1}\cap S_{1}
.

2.

Assume inductively that we have constructed
\xi_{i}
on
S_{i}\setminus S_{i-1}
for
i\leqslant n
. The thinning of
\varphi\cap S_{n}
is
\xi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\xi_{n}
. Next construct the thinning on
S_{n+1}\setminus S_{n}
. For this, add
\xi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\xi_{n}
to the boundary conditions
\psi
and apply the standard thinning map
T_{Q\setminus S_{n},\psi\cup\xi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\xi_{n},\iota_{n}}
on
Q\setminus S_{n}
to obtain
\chi_{n+1}:=\chi_{n+1}(\varphi^{*}):=T_{Q\setminus S_{n},\psi\cup\xi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\xi_{n},\iota_{n}}(\varphi^{*}_{Q\setminus S_{n}})
. Set
\xi_{n+1}:=\chi_{n+1}\cap(S_{n+1}\setminus S_{n})
.

3.

Finally, define
T^{\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\psi}(\varphi^{*}):=\bigcup_{n\geqslant 1}\xi_{n}
.

Taking
S_{n}(\varphi^{*})=Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}}_{n}]}
, where
{\bm{x}}_{n}
is the
n
th smallest point of
\varphi
, we recover the standard embedding map from Definition
5.1
. The following proposition shows that applying
T^{\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\psi}
to a Poisson process yields a Gibbs process generalizing Proposition
5.2
.

Proposition 5.4

(Correctness of disagreement coupling)
.

Suppose the PI satisfies (
5
). Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Then,
T^{\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the proof of
[
19
, Prop. 4.4]
after replacing
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\mathbb{X}
.
∎

We want to relate the disagreement probabilities of Gibbs processes with different boundary conditions to percolation clusters. For this, we introduce the following cluster-based version of disagreement coupling, which is the version mainly used in this paper.

Definition 5.5
.

[Cluster-based thinning]
Assume the existence of a symmetric relation
\sim
on
\mathbb{X}
such that (
6
) holds.
Let
B\subseteq\mathbb{X}\setminus Q
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. The
cluster-based thinning map
, denoted by
T_{Q,B,\psi}^{\mathsf{cl}}:\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}\to\mathbf{N}_{Q}
, is the version of disagreement coupling arising from the following choice of stopping sets and orderings: Choose an initial ordering
\iota:Q\to(0,1)
.
Let
S_{1}:=N(B)\cap Q
be the deterministic set consisting of all points in
Q
that are neighbors to a point in
B
under the relation
\sim
. Suppose
S_{n}
is defined. We continue with one of the two cases below.

1.

If
N(\varphi\cap S_{n})\cap(Q\setminus S_{n})\neq\varnothing
, we take
S_{n+1}:=S_{n}\cup N(\varphi\cap S_{n})\cap Q
.

2.

Otherwise, let
S_{n+1}=S_{n}\cup Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}}]}
where
{\bm{x}}
is the
\iota
-smallest point in
(Q\setminus S_{n})\cap\varphi
. If no such point exists, we set
S_{n+1}=Q
.

Define the orderings
\iota_{n}:Q\setminus S_{n}\to(0,1)
satisfying (
35
) by

\displaystyle\iota_{0}({\bm{x}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}{\{{\bm{x}}\in S_{1}\}}+(\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})+\tfrac{1}{2})\mathbbmss{1}{\{{\bm{x}}\in Q\setminus S_{1}\}},

\displaystyle\iota_{n}({\bm{x}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}{\{{\bm{x}}\in N(\varphi\cap S_{n})\cap(Q\setminus S_{n})\}}+(\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})+\tfrac{1}{2})\mathbbmss{1}{\{{\bm{x}}\in Q\setminus(S_{n}\cup N(\varphi\cap S_{n}))\}},\qquad n\geqslant 1.

In the cluster-based thinning, we thus take
S_{n+1}
to be all neighbors of the so far explored points
\varphi\cap S_{n}
. If all these neighbors have already been explored, we instead take
S_{n+1}\setminus S_{n}
to consist of all so far unexplored points up to and including the smallest point in
\mathbb{S}_{n}\setminus\varphi
in the
\iota
-ordering. The stopping sets thus explore the clusters of
\mathcal{G}(\varphi)
starting from the points of
\varphi
that are neighbors to a point in
B
. As long as we are in case 1., we explore the same clusters, while in case 2., we jump to a new cluster. The order in which the remaining clusters are visited is determined by the
\iota
-smallest point in the cluster.

The advantage of the cluster-based thinning is that when we jump to a new cluster in 2., it means that
(\varphi\cap S_{n})\cup(\psi\cap B)
is not neighbor to any point in
Q\setminus S_{n}
. Hence, by Assumption (
6
), the retention probabilities (
31
) used in the remainder of the algorithm are not influenced by any of the points in
(\varphi\cap S_{n})\cup(\psi\cap B)
. Therefore, the boundary points
\psi\cap B
only affect the thinning of clusters connected to
B
, and the thinning of each new cluster does not depend on the thinning decisions made on the previous clusters.
This means that when we change the boundary conditions locally in
B
, it only affects the thinning of the clusters of
\varphi
connected to a point in
B
.

We summarize the main properties of the cluster-based thinning in Proposition
5.6
.
The last statement of the proposition bounds the
total variation distance
between two Gibbs processes. The total variation distance between two random variables
X,Y
with values in a common measurable space
S
is defined by

(36)

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(X,Y):=\sup_{f\colon S\to[0,1]}\big|\mathbb{E}[f(X)]-\mathbb{E}[f(Y)]\big|,

where the supremum is taken over all measurable functions
f\colon S\to[0,1]
.

Proposition 5.6

(Properties of the cluster-based thinning)
.

Suppose (
5
) and (
6
) holds. Let
Q\subseteq\mathbb{X}
with
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and let
B\subseteq\mathbb{X}\setminus Q
be a countable set. The cluster-based thinning has the property that if
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
with
\psi\Delta\psi^{\prime}\subseteq B
, then
T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,B,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
and
T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,B,\psi^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
differ only on the clusters of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{Q})
that have a point that is neighbor to a point in
B
. In particular, for
A\subseteq Q

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,B,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\neq T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,B,\psi^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup B}B),

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\cap A,\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi^{\prime})\cap A)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup B}B).

We conclude this section by noting that disagreement coupling can also be used to show that (
9
) implies uniqueness in distribution of infinite-volume Gibbs processes, see
[
4
, Thm 9.1]
.

5.2.
Local perturbation of PIs

In this section, we show a bound on how much local changes in the PI affect the Poisson embedding. Several important consequences will be drawn from this lemma in later sections. In the following, let
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(Q,\psi^{\prime})
denote Gibbs processes with PIs
\kappa
,
\kappa^{\prime}
and boundary conditions
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
, respectively. A ”prime” will be used to denote quantities associated with
\kappa^{\prime}
, e.g.
T_{Q,\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime}
denotes the standard thinning using the PI
\kappa^{\prime}
.

Lemma
5.7
below bounds the disagreement probabilities between the first points in the respective Poisson embeddings in terms of the total variation distance betwen the corresponding Gibbs processes.
Since the total variation distance has an interpretation as the infimum of disagreement probabilities over all couplings, the lemma and its corollary below show that the Poisson embedding is not too far from optimal.

Lemma 5.7

(Disagreement probability of first point)
.

Let
A\subseteq Q
be elements of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
,
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
, and
\iota:Q\to(0,1)
be injective with
\iota({\bm{x}})<\iota({\bm{y}})
whenever
{\bm{x}}\in A
and
{\bm{y}}\in Q\setminus A
. Suppose that
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
are PIs satisfying (
5
).
Then,

\mathbb{P}\big(\inf{}_{\iota}(T_{Q,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)\neq\inf{}_{\iota}(T^{\prime}_{Q,\psi^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)\big)\leqslant(2+\lambda(A))\,d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\cap A,\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(Q,\psi^{\prime})\cap A),

where

\inf_{\iota}
denotes the smallest point with respect to the ordering
\leqslant_{\iota}
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of
[
19
, Lem. 4.21]
after replacing
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\mathbb{X}
and is hence omitted.
∎

For the next extension, assume that
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
are closely related on some
P\subseteq Q
via a common PI
\kappa_{0}
satisfying (
5
), i.e. there are
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus P}
such that for all
{\bm{x}}\in P
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
,

\displaystyle\kappa({\bm{x}},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap P_{1})\cup\eta)

(37)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}({\bm{x}},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap P_{2})\cup\eta^{\prime})

where
P\subseteq P_{1},P_{2}\subseteq Q
. In the statement below, there are no boundary conditions, as these can always be put as part of
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
, respectively, as noted in Section
2.1
.

Corollary 5.8

(Disagreement probabilities for standard Poisson embedding)
.

Suppose that
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
are of the form (
37
) and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Let
A\subseteq P
be a Borel set. Let
\iota:Q\to(0,1)
be an injective map such that
\iota({\bm{x}})<\iota({\bm{y}})
whenever
{\bm{x}}\in A
and
{\bm{y}}\in Q\setminus A
. Then, with
\bar{\eta}:=\eta\Delta\eta^{\prime}
,

\mathbb{P}\big(T_{Q,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\neq T^{\prime}_{Q,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\big)\leqslant 2\int_{A}\mathbb{P}\big({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta}\big)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}).

Proof.

See Section
11
.
∎

We can apply Corollary
5.8
step-wise to give disagreement probabilities for perturbed PIs when using the cluster-based thinning. We will assume
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
is marked Euclidean space and
Q=Q^{0}\times\mathbb{M}
. Define the initial ordering is as follows. We divide
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into cubes of diameter at most 1. We enumerate the cubes intersecting
Q^{0}
as
C_{l}^{0}
,
l=1,2,\ldots,L
and let
C_{l}=C_{l}^{0}\times\mathbb{M}
. On
C_{l}
we choose an ordering
\iota^{l}:C_{l}\cap Q\to(2^{-l-1},2^{-l})
and let
\iota^{0}:\mathbb{X}\setminus Q\to(0,2^{-L-1})
. We piece these orderings together to form an ordering
\iota:\mathbb{X}\to(0,1)
. We call this a
cube-wise
ordering.

The result is expressed in terms of probabilities of two error events, where we use the notation for
A\subseteq\mathbb{X}
and
s>0
,

B_{s}(A)=\{(x,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}\mid\|x-y\|\leqslant s\text{ for some }(y,m^{\prime})\in A\}.

(1)

For
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, let

E_{\mathsf{dens}}:=\big\{\mathcal{P}(Q)>4\lambda(Q)\big\}

denote the event that
\mathcal{P}(Q)
substantially exceeds its expectation.

(2)

Let
A\subseteq Q
be Borel. Then, we define

E_{\mathsf{perc}}(A,s):=\big\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{Q}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus B_{2s}(A)}\big\}
.

We obtain the following bound on the disagreement probabilities similar to
[
19
, Thm. 4.18]
. The result here is only stated in the generality needed for our main theorems.

Theorem 5.9
.

(Disagreement probabilities for cluster-based thinning)
Let
A\subseteq P=P_{0}\times\mathbb{M}\subseteq Q=Q^{0}\times\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
Fix
s>1
and assume
B_{2s}(A)\subseteq P\subseteq Q
. Let
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}
and let
\iota
be a cube-wise ordering. Suppose that
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
are PIs of the form (
37
) with a common PI
\kappa_{0}
satisfying (
5
), (
6
), and (
7
).
Then, with
\bar{\eta}=\eta\Delta\eta^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\neq T^{\prime,\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\big)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{dens}})+\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{perc}}(A,s))

\displaystyle+4\int_{B_{2s}(A)}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{x}}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})+2\int_{Q\setminus B_{2s}(A)}\int_{B_{s}(A)}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}\}\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}).

Proof.

See Section
11
.
∎

5.3.
Thinning constructions of infinite-volume Gibbs processes

The cluster-based thinning in Section
5.1
was only defined in a bounded window
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
. We now extend this to a thinning algorithm on arbitrary
U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
by taking limits of cluster-based thinnings in bounded domains
U_{n}=U\cap W_{n}
.

Proposition 5.10

(Convergence of cluster-based thinning)
.

Assume that the PI satisfies (
5
), (
6
) and (
9
) and that the ordering
\iota:\mathbb{X}\to(0,1)
satisfies
\iota(W_{n+1}\setminus W_{n})\subseteq(2^{-(n+1)},2^{-n})
. Let
A\subseteq U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
with
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U}
. Set
\xi_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus W_{n}}
. Then, on the event
\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{U}}\xi_{n}\}
, it holds for all
m\geq n
,

(38)

T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{n},\xi_{n},\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})\cap A=T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{m},\xi_{m},\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{m}})\cap A.

In particular, there is a limiting process on
U
,

(39)

\displaystyle T^{\infty}_{U,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U}):=\bigcup_{n_{0}\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant n_{0}}T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{n},\xi_{n},\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})

such that for all
n\geqslant 1
,

\mathbb{P}\big(T^{\infty}_{U,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})\cap A\neq T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{m},\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{m}})\cap A\text{ for some $m\geqslant n$}\big)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{U}}\xi_{n}).

The process
T^{\infty}_{U,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})
is the infinite-volume Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
with PI
\kappa
.

The theorem below shows how we can also construct the Gibbs process on
\mathbb{X}
in two steps. The proof is essentially the DLR-equations
[
11
,
25
]
. However, since these are usually only stated when
U\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, we state the result below.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
be Borel and assume (
5
), (
6
), and (
9
) hold. Let
\mathcal{X}
be an infinite-volume Gibbs process on
\mathbb{X}
, let
\xi=\mathcal{X}\cap(\mathbb{X}\setminus U)
, and define
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\xi\cup T^{\infty}_{U,\xi}({\mathcal{P}}^{*}_{U})
, where
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
is independent of
\mathcal{X}
. Then,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is again an infinite-volume Gibbs process on
\mathbb{X}
.

Proofs of Proposition
5.10
and
5.11
are given in Section
11
.
The above construction of the Gibbs process in unbounded domains does not allow us to control what happens if we locally change the boundary conditions
\psi
inside a bounded set
B\subseteq\mathbb{X}\setminus U
. This requires a generalization of Proposition
5.10
which will be given later in Proposition
6.19
in Section
6.4.2
.

6.
Disagreement coupling for Gibbs processes with pair potential

The close relation between Gibbs point processes with pair potential and the RCM indicated in Section
2.3
motivates the idea that disagreement coupling could be constructed by working on the clusters of an RCM rather on the graph with edges given deterministically by the relation
\sim
. This poses some extra challenges since the edges of the RCM are random. In
[
3
]
, this was solved by approximating the RCM by a sequence of graphs with deterministic edges in some enlarged space. However, for the proofs of the main Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
, and
3.3
, we need to construct the Gibbs process directly as a thinning of an RCM. Therefore, we extend the disagreement coupling framework to the RCM in this section. The proofs are deferred to Section
12
, but we remark that the main proofs rely on the approximation idea of
[
3
]
, which allows us to draw on the machinery set up in Section
5
.

6.1.
Standard embedding in the RCM in finite-volume domains

We first give a version of the standard embedding where the Gibbs process on some
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
is constructed by thinning an RCM by visiting the vertices in the order given by some injective ordering map
\iota:\mathbb{X}\to(0,1)
