. We will assume that
\sup\iota(\mathbb{X}\setminus Q)\leqslant\inf\iota(Q)
.

6.1.1.
Graph construction from marked points

By its definition, the RCM requires additional randomness to decide whether edges are inserted in the model or not. In this section, the additional randomness is added to the model as random marks on the vertices. From these marked points, the RCM can be constructed by a deterministic construction rule.
More generally, we construct graphs on
Q
from elements
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
, where
\bar{Q}^{*}:=Q^{*}\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{Z}}=Q\times[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{Z}}
is equipped with the product measure
\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. The first coordinate of a point
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\bar{\varphi}^{*}
corresponds to a vertex of the graph, while the third coordinate is used to define the edges. The second coordinate is only used later when we construct a thinning of the graph and is sometimes omitted from the notation. When we project the points in
\bar{\varphi}^{*}
onto one or more coordinates, we obtain the point sets
\varphi^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{Q^{*}}
,
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{Q}
, and
\bar{\varphi}\in\mathbf{N}_{Q\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{Z}}}
, respectively. If two points in
\bar{\varphi}^{*}
project to the same point in
\varphi
, we say that that
\varphi
has
multiple points
.

Let
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
and let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}
be a fixed set of points. Assume that
\varphi
does not have multiple points. For
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\bar{\varphi}^{*}
, we associate some of the coordinates in the sequence
{\bm{r}}=(r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
with points in
\psi\cup\varphi
. For this, let
\tilde{\iota}
be an ordering such that
\sup\tilde{\iota}(W_{n+1}\setminus W_{n})\leqslant\inf\tilde{\iota}(W_{n})
. This induces a total ordering on
\varphi\cup\psi
. We associate the
i
th coordinate
r_{i}
of
{\bm{r}}
, with the
i
th point after
{\bm{x}}
in the
\tilde{\iota}
-ordering of
\psi\cup\varphi
. If
{\bm{y}}
is the
i
th point after
{\bm{x}}
, we write
r_{i}=r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
. In particular,
r_{0}=r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}}
. Often, we write
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\bar{\varphi}^{*}
as
({\bm{x}},u,(r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}})_{{\bm{y}}\in\psi\cup\varphi})
. The coordinates of
{\bm{r}}
that are not matched to a point in
\varphi\cup\psi
will never be used. The ordering
\tilde{\iota}
is fixed throughout and is only used to make this assignment. We construct a graph from an element
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
as follows.

Definition 6.1

(Graph construction from
\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
)
.

Let
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. If
\varphi
does not have multiple points, we form a graph
\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
with vertex set
\varphi\cup\psi
and an edge between
{\bm{x}}\in\varphi
and
{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\cup\psi
if
{\bm{y}}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}
and
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\leqslant\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
. If
\varphi
has multiple points, we set
\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
to be the empty graph.

Let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Let
\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi}
be a Poisson process on
\bar{Q}^{*}
, which we will think of as
a Poisson process
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}
on
Q^{*}
with points independently marked by a sequence of i.i.d. uniform points in
[0,1]
. The
\psi
in the notation
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*}
indicates that for each point
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi}
, we think of
{\bm{r}}
as a sequence
{\bm{r}}=(r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}})_{{\bm{y}}\in\psi\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q}}
as explained above.
Applying the graph construction in Definition
6.1
to
\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi}
, we get that
\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi},\psi)
is a random graph with vertex set
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup\psi
and given
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
, there is an edge between
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q}
and
{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup\psi
if
{\bm{y}}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}
and
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\leqslant\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
. As a result, there is an edge between
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
with probability
\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
and all edges are independent of each other. Thus, the distribution of
\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi},\psi)
is that of the RCM
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{Q},\psi)
introduced in Definition
2.6
. Note that there are two marks,
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
and
r_{{\bm{y}},{\bm{x}}}
, that could be used for deciding if there is an edge between
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
. Here, we used
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
when
{\bm{y}}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}
. We will later see different rules for choosing between
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
and
r_{{\bm{y}},{\bm{x}}}
that lead to different constructions of the RCM.

6.1.2.
Standard embedding in the RCM

We now introduce a version of the standard Poisson embedding, where the Gibbs process is embedded in the vertex set of the RCM
\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi},\psi)
in such a way that there are no edges between the Gibbs points. Given the boundary conditions
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
, we define a thinning map
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}:\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}\to\mathbf{N}_{Q}
as follows.
Let
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
. If
\varphi
contains multiple points, we set
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})=\varnothing
. Otherwise, let
\Gamma=\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
. Then,
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})
is defined by following the thinning scheme from Definition
5.1
on
\varphi^{*}
, but using the retention probabilities

(40)

p^{\mathsf{RCM}}({\bm{x}},Q,\phi,\Gamma):=p({\bm{x}},Q,\phi)\frac{\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma}\phi\}}{\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}))}=\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma}\phi\}\frac{Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}(\phi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})}{Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}(\phi)},

where
{\bm{x}}\in\varphi
,
\phi\subseteq(\varphi\cup\psi)\cap Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}
and
{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma}\phi
means that there are no direct edges from
{\bm{x}}
to any point
\phi
in
\Gamma
. If the denominator is zero, then
{\bm{x}}
is connected to a point in
\phi
with probability 1 and we use the convention
0/0:=0
.
Note that the construction only depends on
\bar{\varphi}^{*}
via
\Gamma
and
\varphi^{*}
. Each time we consider a point for thinning, it is automatically removed if it has an edge to a point previously kept. Otherwise, it is only removed with a certain probability. Thus, the subgraph of
\Gamma
with vertex set
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})
will have no edges. Applied to
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*}
and the associated RCM
\Gamma=\Gamma(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*},\psi)
, we show in Proposition
6.2
that
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose the PI is given in terms of a non-negative pair-potential.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
.
Then,
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

Proof.

See Section
12.2
.
∎

6.2.
Disagreement coupling in finite-volume domains for the RCM

The aim of this section is to construct flexible disagreement couplings allowing us to construct the Gibbs process by cluster-wise thinning of an RCM similar to Definition
5.5
. We first give the construction of a single step of the algorithm in Section
6.2.1
. An important difference from Definition
5.5
is that the RCM is not fixed in advance. Instead, it is constructed by a step-wise exploration procedure. Moreover, rather than exploring the underlying Poisson process in increasing parts of space as in Definition
5.5
, we divide it into a sequence of point processes, each of which is Poisson given the previous ones, and explore one at a time. A similar sequential construction was successfully used earlier in literature
[
31
, Proposition 2]
. The construction of the RCM is given in Section
6.2.2
. The thinning takes place during this iterative construction of the RCM. This is explained in Section
6.2.3
.

6.2.1.
Poisson embedding from fixed points

In this section, we construct a thinning map
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}:\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}\to\mathbf{N}_{Q}
, where
\nu\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
is a fixed set of points (not necessarily disjoint from
\psi
) playing the role of
B
in Section
5
. Applying
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
to the process
\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}
, we obtain a process with distribution
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
, but the thinning now starts by considering the vertices of
\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi\cup\nu)
connected by a direct edge to a point in
\nu
. The thinning
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
will be used to form a single exploration step in the exploration-based thinning in Section
6.2.2
.
We first give another algorithm for constructing an RCM from
\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}
that is different from
\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)
on a realization level but has the same distribution.

Definition 6.3

(Long-range percolation model
\Gamma_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
from a finite set
\nu
)
.

Let
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
and
\psi,\nu\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
(not necessarily disjoint). Then, we let

\rhd

V:=V_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)=\{{\bm{x}}\in\varphi\mid{\bm{x}}\sim_{\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\cup\nu)}\nu\}
be the vertices of
\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\cup\nu)
having a direct edge to a point in
\nu
;

\rhd

\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
denote the subgraph of
\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\cup\nu)
with vertices
V\cup\psi
and all induced edges;

\rhd

\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}_{\nu}\big(\bar{\varphi}^{*},V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu)\big)
be the graph with vertex set
\varphi\cup(\psi\setminus\nu)
where there is an edge between
{\bm{x}}\in\varphi\setminus V
and
y\in V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu)
if
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\leqslant\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
and an edge between
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\setminus V
if
{\bm{x}}>_{\iota}{\bm{y}}
and
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\leqslant\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
;

\rhd

\Gamma_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi):=\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu}\big(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\big)\cup\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}_{\nu}\big(\bar{\varphi}^{*},V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu)\big),

where the union of graphs is given by taking union of vertices and edges.

\varphi
\nu
\psi
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}
V

Figure 1.
Illustration of the construction of
\Gamma_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
. The green point is
\nu
, and the red points are
\psi
. The orange points are
V
, which consists of the points connected to
\nu
.
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}
is shown in blue as the subgraph with vertex set
V\cup\psi
and
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}
is the graph shown in black.

The construction of the long-range percolation model
\Gamma_{\nu}
is illustrated in Figure
1
. Note that the green edges are not part of
\Gamma_{\nu}
since
\nu
is disjoint from
\psi
in this example and hence
\nu
is not part of the vertex set of
\Gamma_{\nu}
. The green edges are only used for defining
V
. Note that
\Gamma_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
and
\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
have the same edges between points in
V\cup\psi
and between points in
(\varphi\setminus V)\cup\psi
, but the edges connecting
V
to
\varphi\setminus V
(edges between orange and black points in Figure
1
) may be different because different marks may have been used to decide if edges are present. The next lemma shows that applying
\Gamma_{\nu}
to
\Phi_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}^{*}
, we obtain an RCM.

Lemma 6.4

(The graph
\Gamma_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)
is an RCM)
.

The point processes
V=V_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})
and
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus V
are independent Poisson processes on
Q
with intensity measures
(1-\rho_{\nu})\lambda
and
\rho_{\nu}\lambda
, respectively, where

(41)

\displaystyle\rho_{\nu}({\bm{x}}):=\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\nu}(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})).

Moreover,
\Gamma_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
and conditionally on
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)
, we have
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
is an RCM
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(Q,V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
on
Q
with underlying Poisson intensity
\rho_{\nu}\lambda
.

Proof.

See Section
12.3
.
∎

Next, we give a different way of constructing the Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
as a thinning of
\Gamma_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi\cup\nu)
. For this we define a thinning map
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}:\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}\to\mathbf{N}_{Q}
. For
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
, we let
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})=\varnothing
if
\varphi
has multiple points. Otherwise, let
\Gamma=\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\cup\nu)
and
V=V_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})
.
The thinning proceeds by first thinning
V
and given the result
\xi\subseteq V
, construct a thinning of
\varphi\setminus V
.

\rhd

For the thinning of
V
, we visit the points of
V
in the order of
\iota
. Suppose that
{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{k}\in V\cap Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}
are the points so far kept in the thinning when considering the thinning of
({\bm{x}},u)\in V^{*}
. Let
\phi=\psi\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{x}}_{k}\}
.
Let
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}(Q,\psi\cup\nu)
be an RCM with vertices on a Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{Q}^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}
of intensity
\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda
where

\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}({\bm{y}})=\rho_{\nu}({\bm{y}})\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}\}+\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}\}.

Then,
{\bm{x}}
is kept if
u\leqslant p({\bm{x}},Q,\nu,\phi,\Gamma)
where

(42)

p({\bm{x}},Q,\nu,\phi,\Gamma)=\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma}\phi\}\frac{Z_{Q}(\phi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\},\nu,{\bm{x}})}{Z_{Q}(\phi,\nu,{\bm{x}})}

with

\displaystyle Z_{Q}(\phi,\nu,{\bm{x}})={\mathbb{E}}\bigg(\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}}^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}(Q,\phi\cup\nu)}\phi\setminus\nu\}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}(Q,\phi\cup\nu)}\phi\}

(43)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{{\bm{y}}_{1},{\bm{y}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q}^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}_{1}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{{\bm{x}}}\lambda}(Q,\phi\cup\nu)}{\bm{y}}_{2}\}\bigg).

\rhd

Given the result
\xi\subseteq V
of thinning
V
, we follow the Poisson embedding procedure on
\varphi^{*}\setminus V^{*}
with the retention probabilities (
40
) but where
\Gamma
is now the graph
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
, the boundary conditions are
\xi\cup(\psi\setminus\nu)
, the reference measure is
\rho_{\nu}\lambda
when computing partition functions, and the ordering is

\iota_{\text{new}}({\bm{x}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\notin Q\setminus V\}+\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}}))\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\in Q\setminus V\}.

The latter is a technical detail, since we always assume the boundary points to come first in the ordering.
Denote the thinning of
\varphi\setminus V
by
\xi^{\prime}
.

\rhd

The resulting thinned process is denoted
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})=\xi\cup\xi^{\prime}
.

Applying
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
to
\Phi_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}^{*}
, we obtain a Gibbs process.

Lemma 6.5

(Correctness of thinning construction starting from
\nu
)
.

Let
\kappa
be a PI with non-negative pair potential.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\nu,\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Then,

T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
. Conditionally on
\xi=T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\cap V
, the distribution of
\xi^{\prime}=T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\setminus\xi
is
\mathcal{X}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(Q,\xi\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Section
12.3
.
∎

6.2.2.
An exploration-based construction of the RCM

We next give an exploration-based construction of the RCM by iterative use of Definition
6.3
.
Let
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
and
\nu,\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
.
Then, we construct the graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
by a step-wise procedure.
At the beginning of step
k+1
, we have

\rhd

a set
S_{k}\subseteq\varphi\cup\psi
of so far explored points,

\rhd

a graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
with vertex set
S_{k}
.

During the step
k+1
, we further introduce a reference point
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
which will be the starting point of the cluster explored in step
k+1
.
The details are given in Definition
6.6
below. We write
S_{k}^{*}
and
\bar{S}_{k}^{*}
when the marks from
\bar{\varphi}^{*}
are kept and write
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k})^{*}
for the graph corresponding to
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}
but having vertex set
S_{k}^{*}\cup\psi
. For
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
, we use a slight abuse of notation writing
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{A}^{*}
where for
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{A}^{*}
, the sequence
{\bm{r}}
is still identified with
(r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}})_{{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\cup\psi\cup\nu}
in the same way as the corresponding point in
\bar{\varphi}^{*}
.

Definition 6.6

(Exploration based construction of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}
)
.

Let
\bar{\varphi}^{*}\in\mathbf{N}_{\bar{Q}^{*}}
and
\nu,\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
.
If
\varphi
has multiple points, we set
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}=\varnothing
. Otherwise, initialize the construction by setting
\nu_{0}=\varnothing
,
S_{0}=V_{0}=\psi
,
{\bm{x}}_{0}=-\infty
,
\xi_{0}=\varnothing
,
\iota_{0}=\iota
, and
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{0}
the graph with vertex set
\psi
and no edges. Suppose step
k
is completed and
\varphi\setminus S_{k}\neq\varnothing
.
Then, we first determine a set
V_{k+1}
of points to be explored in the next step as follows.

(1)

Define the window of unexplored points
Q_{k+1}=Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)}\setminus S_{k}.

(2)

Define the ordering

\iota_{k+1}({\bm{x}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota_{k}({\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus Q_{k+1}\}+(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\iota_{k}({\bm{x}}))\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{k+1}\}

that reorders the explored points to come before
Q_{k+1}
.

(3)

Determine a subset
\nu_{k+1}
of
(\nu\cup S_{k})\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})
by some deterministic rule only depending on
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k})^{*}
and
\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l}
.

(4)

Construct the graph
\Gamma_{\nu_{k+1}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})
as in Definition
6.3
using the ordering
\iota_{k+1}
.

(5)

Let
V_{k+1}=V_{\nu_{k+1}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})
be the set of points in
\varphi\setminus S_{k}
connected to
\nu_{k+1}
in
\Gamma_{\nu_{k+1}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})
. Those are the so far unexplored points connected to
\nu_{k+1}
.

To determine the next step, we distinguish two cases depending on whether or not the exploration step was successful. That is, whether
V_{k+1}\neq\varnothing
or
V_{k+1}=\varnothing
.

Case 1 – Successful exploration
\bm{V_{k+1}\neq\varnothing}
.

In this case, we keep exploring the same cluster, see Figure
2
left.

(1)

Let
S_{k+1}=S_{k}\cup V_{k+1}
be the points explored after the
(k+1)
st step and keep the reference point, i.e.,
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}={\bm{x}}_{k}
.

(2)

Define

\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu_{k+1}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})

(3)

If
S_{k+1}=\varphi\cup\psi
, the algorithm terminates with
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}=\cup_{l=0}^{k+1}\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{l}
.

Case 2 – Failed exploration
\bm{V_{k+1}=\varnothing}
.

In this case, we start exploring a new cluster, see Figure
2
right.

(1)

Let
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}=\inf_{\iota_{k+1}}(\varphi\setminus S_{k})
be the first point in the new cluster and
S_{k+1}=S_{k}\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}
the points explored after step
k+1
.

(2)

Define
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\mathcal{G}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}}
where
\mathcal{G}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}}
consists of the vertices
\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}\cup\psi
and all edges in
\Gamma_{\nu_{k+1}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})
between
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
and
\psi\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}
.

(3)

If
S_{k+1}=\varphi\cup\psi
, the algorithm terminates with
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}=\cup_{l=0}^{k+1}\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{l}
.

In both cases, we let
V_{k+1}^{\prime}=S_{k+1}\setminus S_{k}
be the set of points that are explored in step
k+1
.

{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
S_{k}
V_{k+1}
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
S_{k}

Figure 2.
Left: Successful exploration
V_{k+1}\neq\varnothing
. Right: Failed exploration
V_{k+1}=\varnothing
.

Note that the graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}
is not given from start, but is being built while exploring it. The definition can be seen as a repeated use of the graph construction from fixed points in Definition
6.3
where in each step we only keep the
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}
part of the graph. The only free choice in the algorithm is how we choose the sets
\nu_{k}
.

The main property of this construction will be that
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})
is an RCM
\Gamma(Q,\psi)
.

Proposition 6.7

(Distributional correctness of the exploration based graph construction)
.

Let
\psi,\nu\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
.
Then,
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
.

Proof.

See Section
12.3
.
∎

The choice for
\nu_{k}
that we will mainly work with in the following is the one given below.

Definition 6.8

(Cluster-based graph construction
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{\nu}
)
.

The
cluster-based graph construction
(denoted
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{\nu}
) is the graph resulting from Definition
6.6
if we set
\nu_{1}=\nu
and
\nu_{k+1}=V_{k}^{\prime}
for
k\geqslant 1
.

The cluster-based graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{\nu}
is built by first building the clusters connected to
\nu
. In the first step, we explore all points directly connected to
\nu
, in the second step all points connected by two (but not one) edges and so on. The first time we encounter
V_{k}=\varnothing
, these clusters are completed, and we start a new cluster from
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
. Each time
V_{l}=\varnothing
, a cluster is completed and a new one is started from
{\bm{x}}_{l+1}
.
The point
{\bm{x}}_{l}
will be the
\iota
-smallest point in the cluster, and the sequence of starting points
{\bm{x}}_{l}
is increasing with respect to the
\iota
-ordering, meaning that the clusters are explored in the order of their
\iota
-smallest point. This is very similar to the cluster-based exploration from Definition
5.5
, where neighboring points were determined by the relation
\sim
. The difference is that the edges are not there from the beginning, but are being built from the marks
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
.

6.2.3.
Disagreement coupling for the RCM

Next, we define a version of disagreement coupling for the RCM. It assumes an exploration based graph construction. In the
k
th exploration step, the thinning is applied to the explored set
V_{k}^{\prime}
. The precise definition is given below.

Definition 6.9

(Disagreement coupling for the RCM)
.

Assume an exploration based graph construction
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
as in Definition
6.6
.
If
\varphi
has multiple points, we set
T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})=\varnothing
. Otherwise, we construct thinnings
\xi_{k}\subseteq V_{k}^{\prime}
step-wise as follows. Let
B_{k}
denote a set of boundary conditions for step
k
and initialize
B_{0}=\psi
and
\xi_{0}=\varnothing
. Suppose step
k
is completed and we have constructed
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
.

(1)

Set
B_{k+1}=(B_{k}\setminus\nu_{k})\cup\xi_{k}
.

(2)

Set
\xi_{k+1}=T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q_{k+1},\nu_{k+1},B_{k+1},\iota_{k+1}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*}_{Q_{k+1}})\cap V_{k+1}^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
S_{k+1}=\varphi\cup\psi
, the algorithm terminates with
T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\bar{\varphi}^{*})=\cup_{l=1}^{k+1}\xi_{l}
.

The next theorem shows that applying this construction to
\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}
yields a Gibbs process.

Theorem 6.10

(Distributional correctness of disagreement coupling for the RCM)
.

Let
\kappa
be a PI with non-negative pair potential.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi,\nu\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Then,

T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{dc}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Section
12.3
.
∎

Again, we will work with the following version.

Definition 6.11

(Cluster-based thinning for the RCM)
.

In the case where the exploration based graph construction is the cluster-based construction
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{\nu}
from Definition
6.8
, we call the corresponding thinning algorithm the
cluster-based thinning of the RCM
and denote it by
T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
.

The advantage of the cluster-based thinning is that the part of the boundary conditions
\psi\cap\nu
will only affect the clusters connected to
\nu
allowing us to control the effect of changing boundary conditions inside
\nu
.

Corollary 6.12
.

Suppose
\kappa
has a non-negative pair potential.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
A\subseteq Q
. Let
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. Then,

\mathbb{P}\Big(T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\psi\Delta\psi^{\prime},\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\psi^{\prime}})\cap A\neq T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\psi\Delta\psi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\psi^{\prime}})\cap A\Big)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\psi\Delta\psi^{\prime}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma(Q,\psi\Delta\psi^{\prime})}\mathcal{P}_{A}).

6.3.
Local perturbation of PIs

We now consider a slight modification of our models with pair potential, namely processes with PI of the form

(44)

\bar{\kappa}({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\bar{\alpha}({\bm{x}})\exp\bigg(-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\varphi}\bar{v}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\bigg)

where
\bar{v}
is a pair potential and
\bar{\alpha}:Q\to[0,1]
is an
activity function
. The only difference from the models with pair potential considered so far is the activity function.
We could handle the activity function by changing the reference measure to
\bar{\alpha}\lambda
, see Remark
2.2
. However, we later consider a family of Gibbs models of the form (
44
) and want to have
\lambda
as a common reference measure. We will assume that there is a dominating PI
v
such that
\bar{v}\leqslant v
for all the processes. We construct them by thinning of a common RCM
\Gamma^{\lambda}(Q,\psi\cup\nu,\pi)
, where the connection probabilities are
\pi=1-e^{-v}
. We denote this thinning by
\bar{T}^{\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
. It explores the clusters of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi_{Q,\nu\cup\psi}^{*},\nu\cup\psi,\pi)
in the same order as
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
. However, the retention probabilities will depend on
\bar{\alpha}
and
\tilde{v}
. We refer to Section
12.4
for the precise definition of
\bar{T}^{\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}
and the proofs of Theorem
6.13
and Lemma
6.14
below.

In the following, we assume that
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
is marked Euclidean space and
\kappa
is a PI with pair potential
v
. Consider two PIs
\bar{\kappa}
and
\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}
of the form (
44
). We assume that there is a
P\subseteq Q
such that for all
{\bm{x}}\in P
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\alpha({\bm{x}})\kappa({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap P_{1})\cup\eta)=\alpha({\bm{x}})e^{-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\eta}v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})}e^{-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\varphi}v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in P_{1}\}}

(45)

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\alpha({\bm{x}})\kappa({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap P_{1}^{\prime})\cup\eta^{\prime})=\alpha({\bm{x}})e^{-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\eta^{\prime}}v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})}e^{-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\varphi}v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in P_{1}^{\prime}\}}.

We require
P\subseteq P_{1}\cap P_{1}^{\prime}
,
P_{1},P_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq Q
, and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}
with
\eta\Delta\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus P}
and
\alpha:P\to[0,1]
. Note that on
P
,
\bar{\kappa}
has the form (
44
) with
\bar{\alpha}({\bm{x}})=\alpha({\bm{x}})e^{-\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\eta}v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})}
and
\bar{v}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=v({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in P_{1}\}
and similarly for
\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}
.

The theorem below bounds the disagreement between the two processes in (
45
) on
A\subseteq P
. It uses the notation
E_{\mathsf{perc}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(A,s)=\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma(Q,\varnothing)}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus B_{s}(A)}\}
for
s>0
.

Theorem 6.13
.

(Disagreement for perturbed PIs with pair potential)
Let
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
and assume
\iota
is a cube-wise ordering as in Section
5.2
.
Let
Q=Q_{0}\times\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
A=A_{0}\times\mathbb{M}\subseteq P=P_{0}\times\mathbb{M}\subseteq Q
. Let
\kappa
be a PI with pair potential satisfying (
12
), (
14
), and (
15
). Let
\bar{T}^{\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}
and
\bar{T}^{\prime,\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}
denote thinnings with respect to PIs
\bar{\kappa}
and
\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}
of the form (
44
) that satisfy (
45
) on
P
. Let
s>1
and assume
B_{2s}(A)\subseteq P
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{T}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}(\Phi_{Q,\varnothing}^{*})\cap A\neq\bar{T}^{\prime,\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}(\Phi_{Q,\varnothing}^{*})\cap A)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{dens}})+\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{perc}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(A,s))

\displaystyle+16\lambda(Q)\int_{B_{2s}(A)}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{x}}(Q,\bar{\eta})}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})+8\lambda(Q)\int_{Q\setminus B_{2s}(A)}\int_{B_{s}(A)}\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}),

where
\bar{\eta}=\eta\Delta\eta^{\prime}
.

We will apply the theorem above to PIs of the form given in the following lemma. In Section
8
, we will apply it in the concrete case where
Q
is a window and
P_{1}=Q
,
P_{1,-}=Q\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})
,
P_{3}=P_{2,-}=\varnothing
and
A=Q\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}})
. This is illustrated in the left panel of Figure
3
. In Section
10
, we consider the case
P_{1}=Q
,
P_{1,-}=B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
,
P_{3}=B_{s}({\bm{x}})
,
P_{2,-}=B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})\setminus B_{s}({\bm{y}})
,
A=B_{r}({\bm{x}})
and
P=B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
, see right panel of Figure
3
.

Lemma 6.14

(Perturbed PIs)
.

Let
\kappa
be a PI induced by a pair potential
v
satisfying (
12
), (
14
), (
15
). Let
Q=Q_{0}\times\mathbb{M}
,
P_{i}=(P_{i})_{0}\times\mathbb{M}
for
i=1,2,3
and
P_{i,-}=(P_{i,-})_{0}\times\mathbb{M}
for
i=1,2
be such that
P_{i,-}\subseteq P_{i}\subseteq Q
for
i=1,2
,
P_{1,-}\cap P_{2,-}=\varnothing
and
P_{3}\subseteq P_{1,-}
. Moreover, let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus P_{1}},\psi_{i}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus P_{i,-}}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Consider PIs
\kappa^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime\prime}
defined as

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}({\bm{x}},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\kappa\big({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap(P_{1}\setminus P_{3}))\cup\psi\big)\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\in P_{1}\setminus P_{3}\},

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime\prime}({\bm{x}},\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\kappa\big({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap(P_{1,-}\setminus P_{3}))\cup\psi_{1}\big)&{\bm{x}}\in P_{1,-}\setminus P_{3},\\
\kappa\big({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap P_{2,-})\cup\psi_{2}\big)&{\bm{x}}\in P_{2,-},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then,

\kappa^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime\prime}
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
6.13
with
P=P_{1,-}
and
\alpha({\bm{x}})=\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\notin P_{3}\}
.

bounds the probability that the two processes disagree on the medium sized ball
A
around
{\bm{x}}
.

6.4.
Extension to unbounded domains

So far, we have constructed thinnings of the RCM only for Gibbs processes
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
in bounded domains
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
. In this section, we consider the extension to the infinite-volume Gibbs process
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
on an unbounded Borel set
U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
with boundary conditions
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U}
. We are going to use this for the proof of Theorem
3.3
in the setting where
U=\bigcup_{w\succ z}Q_{w,1}
is the union of all cubes preceding a fixed cube
Q_{z,1}
in the lexicographic order, see illustration in Figure
4
.
In Section
6.4.1
, we show that the cluster-based thinning in increasing windows converges to give a Gibbs process in unbounded domains. However, this construction does not control what happens when changing boundary conditions
\psi
locally. Thus, in Section
6.4.2
, we give a modified construction that is better able to control the effect of changing
\psi
. In the proof of Theorem
3.3
, we will apply the result when
\psi
is changed inside
Q_{z,1}
, see Figure
4
.

6.4.1.
A cluster-based thinning construction in infinite-volume domains

The idea is to construct
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
as the limit of Gibbs processes
\mathcal{X}(U_{n},\psi)
, where
U_{n}=U\cap W_{n}
, by letting
n\to\infty
. The processes are obtained by thinning a common process
\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi}
with underlying Poisson process
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U}
and given
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U}
, each
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi}
consists of an
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U}
equipped with a sequence
{\bm{r}}=(r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}})_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{U}\cup\psi}
of i.i.d. uniform variables in
[0,1]
.
Let
\nu_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus U_{n}}
. Then,
\mathcal{X}(U_{n},\psi)
can be constructed as
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{U_{n},\nu_{n},\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\nu_{n}\cup\psi})
, where
\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\nu_{n}\cup\psi}
is the restriction of
\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi}
to
U_{n}
using the same association of marks with points
{\bm{r}}=(r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}=(r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}})_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{U}\cup\psi}
as the original
\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi}
.
The exploration-based graphs
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\nu_{n}\cup\psi},\psi)
converge when
n\to\infty
to a well-defined graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi},\psi)
, which is distributed as an RCM
\Gamma(U,\psi)
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Consider a PI with non-negative pair potential
\kappa
satisfying (
12
) and (
15
). Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
be a Borel set,
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U}
and
\nu_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus U_{n}}
. Assume
\iota
is such that
\iota(U_{n+1}\setminus U_{n})\subseteq(2^{-(n+1)},2^{-n})
and
\iota(\mathbb{X}\setminus U)\subseteq(-\infty,0)
.
The connected components of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\nu_{n}\cup\psi},\nu_{n}\cup\psi)
not connected to
\nu_{n}
are unchanged when increasing
n
. The limiting graph

\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi},\psi):=\bigcup_{n_{0}\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant n_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\nu_{n}\cup\psi},\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(U,\psi)

is the union of all these connected components.

Proof.

See Section
12.5

∎

We next show that the Gibbs processes

T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{U_{n},\nu_{n},\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\nu_{n}\cup\psi})
converge to
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
.

Proposition 6.16
.

Consider a PI with non-negative pair-potential
\kappa
satisfying (
12
) and (
15
).
Let
A\subseteq W_{n}
be a Borel set. On the event
\{\mathcal{P}_{A\cap U}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma}\nu_{n}\}
, where
\Gamma=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi},\psi)
,

T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{m},\nu_{m},\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{m},\psi\cup\nu_{m}})\cap A=T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{n},\nu_{n},\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\psi\cup\nu_{n}})\cap A

almost surely whenever
m\geqslant n
. Let

T^{\mathsf{RCM,\infty}}_{U,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\nu_{n}\cup\psi})=\bigcup_{n_{0}\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant n_{0}}T^{\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{n},\nu_{n},\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\psi\cup\nu_{n}})

denote the limiting process. Then,
T^{\mathsf{RCM,\infty}}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}(\Phi^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
and if
\Psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)\cap\setminus U
, then
\Psi\cup T^{\mathsf{RCM,\infty}}_{U,\Psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\Psi})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
.

Proof.

See Section
12.5

∎

Q_{z,1}

Figure 4.

The set
U
is the union of the white and the light grey region consisting af all squares succeeding
Q_{z,1}
lexicographically. The window
W_{n}
is the middle
3\times 3
square, so
U_{n}
is the white region and
U\setminus U_{n}
is the light grey region. The points in the dark grey region are the boundary points
\psi
while the points in the light grey region are
\nu_{n}
. The points in the white region is the Poisson process that we are thinning.

6.4.2.
Disagreement coupling for infinite-volume Gibbs processes

In Section
7
, we need to compare infinite-volume Gibbs processes on
U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
with locally differing boundary conditions. For simplicity, we will assume that the boundary conditions disagree only on
W_{1}
and
W_{1}\cup U=\varnothing
. Let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(U\cup W_{1})}
and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbf{N}_{W_{1}}
. We compare the two sets of boundary conditions
\psi\cup\mu_{1}
and
\psi\cup\mu_{2}
. As in Section
6.4
, we first construct Gibbs processes on finite domains
U_{n}=U\cap W_{n}
and then let
n\to\infty
, but we use a modification of the cluster-based disagreement coupling.
Assume
\mu_{1}\cup\mu_{2}\subseteq\mu\subseteq W_{1}
and
\nu_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus U_{n}}
.
We denote the modified cluster-based disagreement coupling by
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}})
. It starts from
\nu_{1}=\mu
. In each step, we take
\nu_{k+1}=V_{k}
until at some point
V_{k_{0}}=\varnothing
, i.e. we have explored all clusters connected to
\nu_{1}
. In this case, we set
V_{k_{0}}^{\prime}=\varnothing
and
\nu_{k_{0}+1}=\nu_{n}
. For
k>k_{0}
, we set
\nu_{k+1}=V_{k+1}^{\prime}
. Thus, we first construct all clusters connected to
\mu
. We denote the union of all these clusters by
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
. Then, we follow the usual cluster-based exploration scheme of Definition
6.8
and explore all clusters connected to
\nu_{n}
. Finally, the remaining clusters are explored in the order of their
\iota
-smallest point. The resulting graph is denoted
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}},\psi\cup\mu)
and the associated thinning
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}})
. The first failed exploration step slightly deviates from the set-up in Definition
6.6
, however, it is easily verified that
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}},\psi\cup\mu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(U_{n},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{n})
and
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(U_{n}\psi\cup\mu_{i})
.

We want to obtain a thinning
T^{\mathsf{RCM,\infty},2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{i}}
as a limit of
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}
when
n\to 0
. To see that this is well-defined, we first note that by a proof similar to Lemma
6.15
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 6.17
.

Assume that (
12
) and (
15
) hold. Let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(U\cup W_{1})}
,
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbf{N}_{W_{1}}
and
\mu_{1}\cup\mu_{2}\subseteq\mu
. Suppose
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\subseteq W_{n}
.
The connected components of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{n})
not connected to
\nu_{n}
are unchanged when increasing
n
. The limiting graph

\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi\cup\mu},\psi\cup\mu):=\bigcup_{n_{0}\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant n_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\mu\cup\psi\cup\nu_{n}},\psi\cup\mu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(U,\psi\cup\mu)

is the union of all these connected components.

We next show that the thinnings
T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U,\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}
converge when
n\to\infty
. That the thinning of the clusters not connected to
\mu\cup\nu_{n}
stabilize when
n\to\infty
can be shown as in Proposition
6.16
. However, to show that the thinning of
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
also stabilizes requires an additional proof, which makes use of Corollary
5.8
and the perturbed PIs from Section
6.3
. The resulting thinnings will have the property (
54
) below that exchanging the boundary conditions
\mu_{1}
by
\mu_{2}
only affects the clusters
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
connected to
\mu
.

For the proof, we make some extra assumptions. Let
\Gamma
denote an RCM
\Gamma(U,\varnothing)
with vertex set
\mathcal{P}_{U}
.
By dominated convergence, if the PI satisfies (
15
), then, for every
n\geqslant 1
,

\lim_{n^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{U_{n}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus U_{n^{\prime}}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})=0.

Thus, after possibly replacing
W_{n}
by a subsequence, we may assume in the proof that

(46)

\int_{U_{n}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus U_{n+1}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})<2^{-n}.

Note that this means that Proposition
6.18
only holds for this subsequence, but this is sufficient for our purposes.
If (
14
) holds, we may further assume

(47)

\int_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{n+1}}\int_{W_{n}}\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})<2^{-n}.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{B}
with
U\cap W_{1}=\varnothing
,
A\subseteq U
with
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U}
.
Let
\kappa
be a PI with non-negative pair potential satisfying (
12
), (
14
) and (
15
).
Assume
\mu_{1}\cup\mu_{2}\subseteq\mu
where
\mu
is a Poisson process on
W_{1}\setminus U
with intensity measure
c\lambda
for some
c>0
. Assume that the sequence
W_{n}
satisfies (
46
) and (
47
). Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
denote the union of all clusters connected to
\mu
in
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi\cup\mu},\psi\cup\mu)
. Then, for
i=1,2
,

(48)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\bigcap_{n\geqslant 1}\bigcup_{m\geqslant n}\Big\{T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{m},\mu,\nu_{m},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{m},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{m}})\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\neq T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{n}})\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\Big\}\Big)=0.

If
A\subseteq U_{n}
, then on the event
\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma}\nu_{n}\}
, where
\Gamma=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl},2}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi\cup\mu},\psi\cup\mu)
, we have

(49)

T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{m},\mu,\nu_{m},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{m},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{m}})\cap(A\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\mu})=T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{n}})\cap(A\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\mu})

almost surely whenever
m\geqslant n
. Let

T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi\cup\mu}):=\bigcup_{n_{0}\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant n_{0}}T^{\mathsf{RCM,cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\Phi^{*}_{U_{n},\psi\cup\mu\cup\nu_{n}})

denote the limiting process. Then,

(50)

\mathbb{P}\big(T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{1}}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi\cup\mu})\cap A\neq T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{2}}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\psi\cup\mu})\cap A\big)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma}\mu).

If
\Psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)\cap(\mathbb{X}\setminus U)
with
\Psi\cap W_{1}\subseteq\mu
, then

(51)

\Psi\cup T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\Psi}(\Phi^{*}_{U,\Psi\cup\mu})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing).

A similar thinning
T^{\mathsf{cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})
can be given for the processes considered in Section
5
. Again, we first explore all clusters of
G(\mathcal{P}_{U_{n}})
connected to
\mu
, then all clusters connected to
\nu_{n}
, and finally the remaining clusters. Under the assumption (
9
), we may assume that the sequence
W_{n}
(or a subsequence) satisfies

(52)

\int_{W_{n}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}}\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{n+1})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})<2^{-n},

and under (
7
) also

(53)

\int_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{n+1}}\int_{W_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}\}\lambda(\,{\rm d}{\bm{x}})\lambda(\,{\rm d}{\bm{y}})<2^{-n}.

We have the following version of Proposition
6.18
, whose proof is similar to Proposition
6.18
, but simpler, so we omit it here.

Proposition 6.19
.

Consider a PI satisfying (
5
), (
6
), (
7
) and (
9
).
Assume that the sequence
W_{n}
satisfies (
52
) and (
53
). Let
A\subseteq U\subseteq\mathbb{X}
with
U\cap W_{1}=\varnothing
,
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(U\cup W_{1})}
. Assume
\mu_{1}\cup\mu_{2}\subseteq\mu
where
\mu
is a Poisson process on
W_{1}\setminus U
of intensity
c\lambda
for some
c>0
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
denote the union of all clusters connected to
\mu
in
G(\mathcal{P}_{U}\cup\mu)
. Then, for
i=1,2

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(\bigcup_{m\geqslant n}\{T^{\mathsf{cl},2}_{U_{m},\mu,\nu_{m},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{m}})\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\neq T^{\mathsf{cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\}\Big)=0.

On the event
\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}}\nu_{n}\}
,

T^{\mathsf{cl},2}_{U_{m},\mu,\nu_{m},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{m}})\cap(A\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\mu})=T^{\mathsf{cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})\cap(A\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\mu})
almost surely whenever
m>n
. Let

T^{\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U}):=\bigcup_{n_{0}\geqslant 1}\bigcap_{n\geqslant n_{0}}T^{\mathsf{cl},2}_{U_{n},\mu,\nu_{n},\psi\cup\mu_{i}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})

denote the limiting process. Then,

(54)

\mathbb{P}\big(T^{\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{1}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})\cap A\neq T^{\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\psi\cup\mu_{2}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})\cap A\big)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma}\mu).

If
\Psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)\cap(\mathbb{X}\setminus U)
and
\Psi\cap W_{i}\subseteq\mu
, then
\Psi\cup T^{\infty,2}_{U,\mu,\Psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
.

7.
A qualitative CLT under weak stabilization

The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
3.3
. Thus, we consider a Gibbs process on marked Euclidean space
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
with translation invariant PI.

7.1.
Gibbs processes with translation-invariant PI

We first show some general properties of Model (L) and (P) when the PI is translation-invariant.

Proposition 7.1
.

Consider Model (L) or Model (P) with a translation-invariant PI. For
U\in\mathcal{B}
and
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U}
,
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)+y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(U+y,\psi+y)
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular,
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
is stationary. Moreover,
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
is mixing and hence ergodic.

Proof.

We give the argument only for Model (P), since Model (L) is similar.
If
\kappa
is translation-invariant, then
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
satisfies the same GNZ equation (
4
) as
\mathcal{X}(U+y,\psi+y)
for
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The first claim follows since the Gibbs distribution satisfying the GNZ equation is unique by Theorem
2.3
and
2.7
, respectively.

To see that
\mathcal{X}:=\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
is mixing, we need to show

(55)

\lim_{\|x\|\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\mathcal{X}+x)g(\mathcal{X})]=\mathbb{E}[f(\mathcal{X})]\mathbb{E}[g(\mathcal{X})]

for any
f,g:\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}}\to[0,1]
of the form
f(\varphi)=f(\varphi\cap W_{s})
,
g(\varphi)=g(\varphi\cap W_{s})
for some large
s
. We may assume that
\|x\|
is so large that
W_{s}\cap(W_{s}-x)=\varnothing
.

Construct realizations of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
as follows:
Set
\Psi=T_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty}(\Phi^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing})\cap W_{s}
and
\tilde{\Psi}=T_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty}(\tilde{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing})\cap W_{s}
, where
\tilde{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}
is an independent copy of
\Phi^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}
.
Then
\Psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\Psi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)\cap W_{s}
by Proposition
6.16
,
and
\mu=\mathcal{P}_{W_{s}}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}}
is a Poisson process of intensity
2\lambda
with
\Psi\cup\tilde{\Psi}\subseteq\mu
.
Extend
\Psi
and
\tilde{\Psi}
to
\mathcal{X}=\Psi\cup\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}=\tilde{\Psi}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
where

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\mu,\Psi}(\check{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\mu})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\mu,\tilde{\Psi}}(\check{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\mu})
with
\check{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\mu}
independent of
\Phi^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}
and
\tilde{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}
. Then
\mathcal{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
by Proposition
6.18
. Let
\check{\Gamma}:=\Gamma(\check{\Phi}^{*}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\mu},\mu)
.
If
\{\mu\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\check{\Gamma}}\check{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}-x}\}
, then
\mathcal{X}\cap(W_{s}-x)=\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\cap(W_{s}-x)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}[f(\mathcal{X}+x)g(\mathcal{X})]-\mathbb{E}[f(\mathcal{X}+x)]\mathbb{E}[g(\mathcal{X})]|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{E}[f(\mathcal{X}\cap(W_{s}-x)+x)(g(\Psi)-\mathbb{E}[g(\Psi)])]|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\mathbb{E}[f(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\cap(W_{s}-x)+x)(g(\Psi)-\mathbb{E}[g(\Psi)])]\big|+2\mathbb{P}(\mu\leftrightsquigarrow_{\check{\Gamma}}\check{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}-x})

\displaystyle=\big|\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[f(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\cap(W_{s}-x)+x)\mid\Psi](g(\Psi)-\mathbb{E}[g(\Psi)])]\big|+2\mathbb{P}(\mu\leftrightsquigarrow_{\check{\Gamma}}\check{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}-x})

\displaystyle=\big|\mathbb{E}[f(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\cap(W_{s}-x)+x)]\mathbb{E}[g(\Psi)-\mathbb{E}[g(\Psi)]]\big|+2\mathbb{P}(\mu\leftrightsquigarrow_{\check{\Gamma}}\check{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}-x})

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{P}(\mu\leftrightsquigarrow_{\check{\Gamma}}\check{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}-x}).

To obtain the second last equality, note that, even though the realization of
\mu
affects the thinning algorithm and is correlated with
\Psi
, the conditional distribution of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
given
\Psi
is always
\mathcal{X}\big(\mathbb{X}\setminus W_{s},\tilde{\Psi}\big)
, which is independent of
\Psi
. Since
\lim_{\|x\|\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mu\leftrightsquigarrow_{\check{\Gamma}}\check{\mathcal{P}}_{W_{s}-x})=0
, we obtain (
55
).
∎

7.2.
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Throughout this section we let
\mathcal{X}
denote the infinite-volume Gibbs process on
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
from Model (L) or Model (P).
Recall the notation from Section
3.3
. The key observation in the proof is that

H_{n}(\mathcal{X})-\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})]=\sum_{i\leqslant k_{n}}\Delta_{i,n},

where

\Delta_{i,n}:=\Delta_{z_{i},n}:=\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{F}_{i,n}]-\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{F}_{i-1,n}].

For each
n
, the sequence
\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\mathcal{F}_{i,n}]
for
i=1,\ldots,k_{n}
defines a martingale with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{i,n}
.
A CLT for
H_{n}(\mathcal{X})
can therefore be derived by a standard martingale method
[
30
]
. Conditions for this method to apply are given in
[
19
, Prop. 5.1]
. Theorem
3.3
will be proved by verifying these conditions.

We define sets

V_{z}=\bigcup_{w\preceq z}Q_{w,1},\quad U_{z}=\bigcup_{w\succ z}Q_{w,1},

where
\preceq
refers to the lexicographic ordering on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In Figure
4
,
V_{z}
corresponds to the dark grey set and
U_{z}
corresponds to the union of the white and the light grey set. Define
V_{z,n}:=V_{z}\cap Q_{n}
and
U_{z,n}:=U_{z}\cap Q_{n}
. In the following, for
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we let
z_{-}
denote the point in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
that comes just before
z
in the lexicographic order.

Fix a
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. For the proof, we construct
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)
using the disagreement coupling construction for Gibbs processes in unbounded domains from Section
6.4.2
. In the case of Model (L), we first construct
\mathcal{X}=T^{\infty}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*})
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{2}
be independent copies of
\mathcal{P}^{*}
. Define
\mu_{z}=(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2})\cap Q_{z,1}
and

\displaystyle\eta_{z}=\mathcal{X}\cap V_{z},\quad\mathcal{X}_{z}=\eta_{z}\cup T^{\infty,2}_{U_{z},\mu_{z},\eta_{z}}((\mathcal{P}_{1})^{*}_{U_{z}}),

\displaystyle\eta_{z_{-}}=\mathcal{X}\cap V_{z_{-}},\quad\zeta_{z_{-}}=T^{\infty}_{U_{z_{-}},\eta_{z_{-}}}((\mathcal{P}_{2})^{*}_{U_{z_{-}}})\cap Q_{z,1},\quad\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}=\eta_{z_{-}}\cup\zeta_{z_{-}}\cup T^{\infty,2}_{U_{z},\mu_{z},\eta_{z_{-}}\cup\zeta_{z_{-}}}((\mathcal{P}_{1})^{*}_{U_{z}}).

For the thinnings, we use the sequence
W_{n}=Q_{z,n}
and an ordering
\iota_{z}
where
\iota_{z}(W_{n+1}\setminus W_{n})\subseteq(2^{-n-1},2^{-n})
. Then,
\mathcal{X}_{z}\sim\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\sim\mathcal{X}
by Proposition
5.11
and
6.19
.

In the case of a PI of Model (P), we similarly construct
\mathcal{X}=T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty}_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}(\Phi_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}^{*})
. Let
(\Phi_{2})^{*}_{U_{z_{-}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U_{z_{-}}}}
and
(\Phi_{1})^{*}_{U_{z},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U_{z}}\cup\mu_{z}}
be independent of each other and of
\Phi_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}^{*}
.
Here,
\mu_{z}=(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2})\cap Q_{z,1}
where
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are the Poisson processes underlying
\Phi_{\mathbb{X},\varnothing}^{*}
and
(\Phi_{2})^{*}_{U_{z_{-}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U_{z_{-}}}}
, respectively. Note that the second subscript
\psi
in
\Phi_{U,\psi}^{*}
is only used to indicate how coordinates are identified with points, and hence, this is not part of the independence requirement. Define

\displaystyle\eta_{z}=\mathcal{X}\cap V_{z},\quad\mathcal{X}_{z}=\eta_{z}\cup T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{U_{z},\mu_{z},\eta_{z}}((\Phi_{1})^{*}_{U_{z},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U_{z}}\cup\mu_{z}}),

\displaystyle\eta_{z_{-}}=\mathcal{X}\cap V_{z_{-}},\quad\zeta_{z_{-}}=T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty}_{U_{z_{-}},\eta_{z_{-}}}((\Phi_{2})^{*}_{U_{z_{-}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U_{z_{-}}}})\cap Q_{z,1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{z}=\eta_{z_{-}}\cup\zeta_{z_{-}}\cup T^{\mathsf{RCM},\infty,2}_{U_{z},\mu_{z},\eta_{z_{-}}\cup\zeta_{z_{-}}}((\Phi_{1})^{*}_{U_{z},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus U_{z}}\cup\mu_{z}}).

We take
W_{n}
and
\iota_{z}
as in Model (L).
Then,
\mathcal{X}_{z}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{X}
by Proposition
6.16
and
6.18
.

In both cases we get

\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X})|\eta_{z_{-}}]=\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})|\eta_{z_{-}}]=\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})|\eta_{z}]
.
Thus, we can write the martingale differences as

\Delta_{z,n}=\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})\mid\eta_{z}]-\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\mid\eta_{z_{-}}]=\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\mid\eta_{z}].

By construction,
\mathcal{X}_{z}
and
\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}
both equal
\eta_{z_{-}}
on
V_{z_{-}}
. On
Q_{z,1}
they may differ, but
(\mathcal{X}_{z}\cup\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\cap Q_{z,1}\subseteq\mu_{z}
. Since they are extended over
U_{z}
by the same cluster based thinning algorithm starting from
\mu_{z}
, they will agree on
\mathbb{X}\setminus Q_{z,n}
if
\{\mu_{z}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{(\mathcal{P}_{1})_{U_{z}}\cup\mu_{z}}(\mathcal{P}_{1})_{U_{z}\setminus Q_{z,n}}\}
or
\{\mu_{z}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{P}_{1})_{U_{z}\setminus Q_{z,n}}\}
, respectively, where
\Gamma:=\Gamma((\Phi_{1})^{*}_{U_{z},\mu_{z}},\mu_{z})
. In particular, (
9
) and (
15
), respectively, ensure that there is almost surely an
N_{z}(\omega)<\infty
such that
\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,N_{z}}=\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\setminus Q_{z,N_{z}}
.

With these constructions, the proof is almost identical to the proof of
[
19
, Thm. 5.3]
. We give the details for completeness.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

The result follows from
[
19
, Prop 5.1]
with
a_{n}=n+\sqrt{n}
,
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}
and
\rho(a_{n})=2\sqrt{n}
if we can verify Condition (i) and (ii) of that proposition. Condition (i) is exactly assumption (
28
). It remains to show (ii). Since
\mathcal{X}
is stationary and ergodic by Proposition
7.1
this condition states:

(ii)

For every
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, there is a random variable
\Delta_{z}=\Delta_{z}(\mathcal{X})
such that
\Delta_{z,n}\to\Delta_{z}
in
L^{2}
.
The convergence must be uniform in the sense that

(56)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{z\in Z_{n-\sqrt{n}}}\|\Delta_{z}-\Delta_{z,n}\|_{L^{2}}=0.

The limit is shift invariant meaning that for any
z_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\Delta_{z+z_{0}}(\mathcal{X}+z_{0})=\Delta_{z}(\mathcal{X})
.

Fix
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. As noted above, there is an
N_{z}(\omega)
such that
\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,N_{z}}=\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\setminus Q_{z,N_{z}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle H(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{n\to\infty}(H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}(H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,N_{z}})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})+H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\setminus Q_{z,N_{z}}))

exists almost surely by the weak stabilization condition (
27
). Moreover,

\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[H(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})|\eta_{z}]^{4}]\leqslant M:=\sup_{n\geqslant 1,z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{z,n}^{4}]

by the conditional Fatou’s Lemma.

We now show that

\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\mid\eta_{z}]\to\mathbb{E}[H(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\mid\eta_{z}]=:\Delta_{z}\text{ in }L^{2}.

First, note that (
27
) implies that for any
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
l\geqslant 1
, and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a random variable
R(z,l,\varepsilon)
such that whenever
Q_{z,R(z,l,\varepsilon)}\subseteq Q_{w,m}
,

|(H(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,l}))-(H(\mathcal{X}_{z}\cap Q_{w,m})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z}\cap Q_{w,m}\setminus Q_{z,l}))|<\varepsilon,

see
[
19
, Proof of Thm. 5.3]
for details. Similarly, there exists a random variable
R_{-}(z,l,\varepsilon)
such that whenever
Q_{z,R_{-}(z,l,\varepsilon)}\subseteq Q_{w,m}
,

|(H(\mathcal{X}_{z-})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z-}\setminus Q_{z,l}))-(H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\cap Q_{w,m})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\cap Q_{w,m}\setminus Q_{z,l}))|<\varepsilon.

The distributions of
R(z,l,\varepsilon)
and
R_{-}(z,l,\varepsilon)
are independent of
z
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given and fix
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. For
l,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define events

E_{z,l}:=\{\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,l}=\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\setminus Q_{z,l}\},\quad F_{z,l,n}:=\big\{\max\{R(z,l,\varepsilon),R_{-}(z,l,\varepsilon)\}\leqslant\sqrt{n}\big\}.

Choose
l
such that
\mathbb{P}(N_{z}>l)<\varepsilon
. Then

\mathbb{P}(E_{z,l})>1-\varepsilon
. For any
n\geqslant l
so large that

\mathbb{P}(F_{z,l,n})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
, the conditional Jensen inequality and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yield

\displaystyle\big\|\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\mid\eta_{z}]-\mathbb{E}[H(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})\mid\eta_{z}]\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\big\|\mathbbmss{1}_{E_{z,l}\cap F_{z,l,n}}((H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}))-(H(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})))\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+4\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{z,n}^{4}+\Delta_{z}^{4}]^{1/2}(\mathbb{P}(E_{z,l}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(F_{z,l,n}^{c}))^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\big\|\mathbbmss{1}_{F_{z,n,l}}(H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,l})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z})+H(\mathcal{X}_{z}\setminus Q_{z,l}))\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+4\big\|\mathbbmss{1}_{F_{z,n,l}}(H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})-H_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\setminus Q_{z,l})-H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}})+H(\mathcal{X}_{z_{-}}\setminus Q_{z,l}))\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+8M^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\varepsilon^{2}+4\sqrt{6}M^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1/2}

Since the distributions of
N_{z}
,
R(z,\varepsilon,l)
and
R_{-}(z,\varepsilon,l)
can be assumed independent of
z
, the bound is uniform for all
z\in Q_{n-\sqrt{n}}
. Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, this shows (
56
). The fact that
\Delta_{z+z_{0}}(\mathcal{X}+z_{0})=\Delta_{z}(\mathcal{X})
follows immediately by construction.
∎

8.
Palm couplings

In this section, we discuss general results on Palm couplings, i.e., couplings involving some random measure and its Palm kernel. These results will play a central role in the proof of both the Poisson and the quantitative normal approximation results in Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
, respectively. Palm couplings were already used successfully for Poisson and normal approximation in
[
5
,
26
]
and
[
7
,
19
]
, respectively.
We first explain the overall idea behind this method. After that we show how to obtain Palm couplings using disagreement coupling. We will mainly focus on the case of pair-wise interactions, i.e., Model (P). Model (L) is similar and a bit easier. Hence, we only add some brief remarks on the steps that are substantially different. Throughout Section
8
,
9
and
10
, we leave out the subscript
n
on
g_{n}
,
\Lambda_{n}
and
\Xi_{n}
.

Recall that we consider the random measure

(57)

\displaystyle\Xi:=\Xi[\mathcal{X}]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})\,\delta_{{\bm{x}}}

based on the score function
g
. This random measure perspective makes the task amenable to the use of Palm theory. We recall that the random measures
\{\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}\}_{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{X}}
form a
Palm kernel
of a random measure
\Xi
if

(58)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{X}}f({\bm{x}},\Xi)\,\Xi({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big]=\int_{\mathbb{X}}\mathbb{E}[f({\bm{x}},\Xi_{{\bm{x}}})]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

for all non-negative measurable
f
, where
\Lambda:=\mathbb{E}[\Xi]
denotes the intensity measure. We refer to
[
24
]
for details on Palm theory.

The Palm kernel is defined on a distributional level. Hence, the definition alone does not give any relation between the Palm kernel and the original process on the level of realizations. However, in the Poisson approximation setting where
\Xi
is a point process, a key finding is that if one can provide a coupling of the original process
\Xi
and its Palm kernel
\Xi_{\bm{x}}
in such a way that they are identical outside a small neighborhood of
{\bm{x}}
, then
\Xi
is close to a Poisson process. More precisely, from
[
5
, Theorem 3.1]
, we obtain that

(59)

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{KR}}\big(\Xi\cap{Q_{n}},\mathcal{M}\cap{Q_{n}}\big)\leqslant 2\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[(\Xi\Delta\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{!})(Q_{n})\big]\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}),

where
d_{\mathsf{KR}}
is the Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance
from (
24
),
\mathcal{M}
is a Poisson process of intensity
\Lambda
, and
\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{!}:=\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\}
is a reduced Palm kernel. Likewise, in the normal approximation setting, a result of
[
7
]
, stated precisely in Theorem
10.1
below, provides a bound on

d_{\mathsf{K}}\bigg(\frac{\Xi(Q_{n})-\mathbb{E}[\Xi(Q_{n})]}{\mathsf{Var}(\Xi(Q_{n}))^{1/2}},N(0,1)\bigg)

in terms of a coupling between
\Xi
and
\Xi_{{\bm{x}}},{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}
. This motivates the construction of such a coupling.

We write
\{\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\}_{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}}
for a Palm kernel of
\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\Xi
in
Q_{n}
. That is, for all measurable
f:Q_{n}\times\mathbf{N}\to[0,\infty)
,

(60)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{Q_{n}}f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})\,\Xi({\rm d}{\bm{x}})=\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}).

We note that assumption (
25
) ensures the
\sigma
-finiteness of
\Lambda
, and thereby the existence of a Palm kernel
\{\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\}_{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}}
, see
[
24
]
.
From (
58
) and (
60
) it follows that
\{\Xi[\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}]\}_{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}}
is a Palm kernel
\{\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}\}_{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}}
of
\Xi
. Hence, a coupling between
\Xi
and
\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}
will be provided via a coupling between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
.
The following lemma from
[
19
]
shows that the Palm process
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}:=\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\}
is a Gibbs process and hence a coupling with
\mathcal{X}
can be obtained by disagreement coupling.

Lemma 8.1

(Lemma 6.2 in
[
19
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Gibbs process satisfying the conditions in Theorem
3.1
. Assume that
g
satisfies the hereditary condition (
23
). Then, for almost all
{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}
, the reduced process
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}
is a Gibbs process with PI
\kappa_{{\bm{x}}}
given by

\kappa_{{\bm{x}}}({\bm{y}},\varphi):=\kappa({\bm{y}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\frac{g({\bm{x}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\})}{g({\bm{x}},\varphi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})}

where
0/0:=1
.

We now provide the precise construction of the Palm coupling in our setting. The specific coupling construction proceeds loosely as in
[
26
]
and
[
19
]
, but there are some differences. Most importantly, for Model (P) we use the RCM-adapted thinning algorithm from Section
6
.

Let
r:=6s:=6c_{\mathsf{S}t}\log(\lambda(Q_{n}))
with
c_{\mathsf{S}t}>0
a constant that will be specified later. While in the random measure
\Xi\cap Q_{n}
we only sum over points in
Q_{n}
, the dependence through the score functions means that
\Xi_{n}
also depends on the points outside
Q_{n}
. Therefore, we introduce the enlarged window
Q_{n}^{+}=B_{2r}(Q_{n})
.

We first construct
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
be a Gibbs process on
\mathbb{X}
with PI
\kappa
. Outside
Q_{n}^{+}
we will use the boundary condition
\Psi:=\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\setminus Q_{n}^{+}
. Inside
Q_{n}^{+}
we construct
\mathcal{X}
by thinning an independent process
\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
. We put
\Psi
as part of the PI, that is, we use the PI

(61)

\kappa^{\prime}({\bm{x}},\varphi)=\kappa({\bm{x}},(\varphi\cap{Q_{n}^{+}})\cup\Psi)\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}^{+}\}

and empty boundary conditions. This is a perturbed PI of the form (
45
), and hence we can construct it as a thinning of the form

\bar{T}^{\prime,\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}
with dominating PI
\kappa
, where the ”prime” refers to the PI
\kappa^{\prime}
. Thus, we set

(62)

\mathcal{X}:=\bar{T}^{\prime,\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}(\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*})\cup\Psi.

Next, we construct
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
,
{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}
, be an independent family of Palm processes of
\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\Xi
that are independent of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
and
\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
. This is possible by the existence of uncountable product measures, see
[
23
, Corollary 6.18]
. Let
\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}=\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\cap(B_{s}({\bm{x}})\cup(\mathbb{X}\setminus Q_{n}^{+}))
be the boundary conditions and define

\displaystyle{\kappa^{\prime}_{{\bm{x}}}}(\bm{z},\varphi):=\kappa_{{\bm{x}}}(\bm{z},\varphi\cap(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))\cup\Psi_{{\bm{x}}})\mathbbmss{1}\{\bm{z}\in Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})\}.

If
R({\bm{x}},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\leqslant s
, then

(63)

\displaystyle{\kappa^{\prime}_{{\bm{x}}}}(\bm{z},\varphi)=\kappa(\bm{z},\varphi\cap(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))\cup\Psi_{{\bm{x}}})\mathbbmss{1}\{\bm{z}\in Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})\}.

We define

(64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}:=\begin{cases}\bar{T}^{\prime,{\bm{x}},\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}(\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*})\cup\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}&\text{ if $R({\bm{x}},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\leqslant s$,}\\
{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}}&\text{ if $R({\bm{x}},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s$,}\end{cases}

where
\bar{T}^{\prime,{\bm{x}},\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}
denotes thinning with respect to the PI
\kappa_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime}
with the same dominating RCM as
\bar{T}^{\prime,\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}
.
It follows analogously to the argument given in
[
19
]
that
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
is a Palm process of
\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\Xi
at
{\bm{x}}
.
In the thinnings, we always use a cube-wise ordering as introduced in Section
5.2

We note that
\kappa^{\prime}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{{\bm{x}}}
given by (
61
) and (
63
) are in the form of Lemma
6.14
with
P_{1}=Q_{n}^{+}
,
P=P_{1,-}=Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})
,
P_{3}=P_{2,-}=\varnothing
,
\psi=\Psi
and
\psi_{1}=\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}
. Hence, for
A:=Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}})
, Lemma
6.14
and Theorem
6.13
yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap(Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}}))\neq\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}\cap(Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}})))\leqslant\mathbb{P}(R({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s)+\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{dens}})

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{perc}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}}),s))+4\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\Psi\cup\Psi_{{\bm{x}}})}\mathcal{P}_{B_{s}({\bm{x}})}\cup(\Psi\Delta\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}))\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})

(65)

\displaystyle+\int_{(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{2s}(Q_{n}))\cup B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\int_{B_{s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{3s}({\bm{x}})}\pi(\bm{z},{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}),

where the expectation on the right-hand side is with respect to
\Psi
and
\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}
. For Model (L), we get a similar bound using Theorem
5.9
instead of Theorem
6.13
. The following lemma will prove helpful to control potential long-range effects both for Poisson and normal approximation.

Lemma 8.2

(Bound on disagreement between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{x}}^{!,\Xi}
)
.

Let
m\geqslant 1
. Let
g:\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
be measurable and satisfying (
21
), (
22
), (
23
) and (
25
). Then, with
c_{\mathsf{St}}\geqslant 2(m+2)\max(c_{\mathsf{es},2}^{-1},c_{\mathsf{v},2}^{-1},c_{\mathsf{D},2}^{-1})
in the definition of
s
,

\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\cap(Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}}))\neq\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi_{n}}\cap(Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}})))\Lambda_{n}({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\in O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m}).

Proof.

We deal with the terms in (
65
) separately.

First term.

We use (
60
) and the GNZ equation (
3
) to see that

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}(R({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s)\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})>s\}]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})^{2}]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}(R({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})>s)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}.

By the stabilization and moment conditions (
22
) and (
25
), the right-hand side is of order
O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m-1})
.

Second term.

By the Poisson concentration property, we have
\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{dens}})\in O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m-1})
, and therefore, since
\Lambda(Q_{n})\in O(\lambda(Q_{n}))
by the GNZ-equation (
3
) and (
25
),
\Lambda(Q_{n})\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{dens}})\in O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m})
.

Third term.

Using the GNZ equation (
3
) as in the first step, we see

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}\big(E_{\mathsf{perc}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}}),s)\big)\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})^{2}]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}_{Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}})}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma(Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing)}\mathcal{P}_{(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}(Q_{n}))\cup B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\Big)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})^{2}]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}\setminus B_{4s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing)}\mathcal{P}_{(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}(Q_{n}))\cup B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\Big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}

By the exponential cluster size property (
16
), the second factor is of order
\lambda(Q_{n})^{(-2m-2)/2}
. Thus, the total order is
O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m-1/2})
. For Model (L) we use Condition (
10
) instead.

Fourth term.
The fourth term is bounded by

(66)

\displaystyle 4\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\Psi)}\mathcal{P}_{B_{s}({\bm{x}})}\cup\Psi\big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})+4\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\Psi_{{\bm{x}}})}\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}\big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z}).

To bound the first integral, we use that
\Psi
can be assumed to be contained in a Poisson process
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
of intensity
\lambda
independent of
\mathcal{P}
and hence, in order for
\bm{z}
to be connected to
\mathcal{P}_{B_{s}({\bm{x}})}\cup\Psi
in
\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\Psi)
, it must be connected to a point in
\mathcal{P}_{B_{s}({\bm{x}})}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}
inside
\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}),\mathcal{P}_{B_{s}({\bm{x}})}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))})
. The
\Lambda
-integral of the first integral in (
66
) is bounded by

\displaystyle 4\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}2\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}\big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}).

Using (
16
) for Model (P) and (
10
) for Model (L), we get that this is of order
O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-2m-2})
.

For the second integral in (
66
), since
\Psi_{\bm{x}}=\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\bm{x}}^{\Xi}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))
, we have from (
60
), (
58
) and (
3
) that

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\Psi_{{\bm{x}}})}\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}\Big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\{{\bm{x}}\}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))})}\{{\bm{x}}\}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}\Big)g({\bm{x}},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigg(\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{E}[g({\bm{x}},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})^{2}]\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\{{\bm{x}}\}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))})}\{{\bm{x}}\}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}\Big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\bigg)^{1/2},

where we used that
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
for some Poisson process
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
independent of
\Gamma(Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing)
. The last factor may be bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}(2\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)}{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus B_{s}(\bm{z})})+\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\{{\bm{x}}\})}{\bm{x}}))\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigg(\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{B_{2s}(Q_{n})\setminus B_{2s}({\bm{x}})}(3\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)}{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus B_{s}(\bm{z})})+\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{{\bm{x}}}(\mathbb{X},\varnothing)}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})})+\pi({\bm{x}},\bm{z}))\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\bigg)^{1/2}.

To get the first bound, we used that in order for
\bm{z}
to be connected to
\{{\bm{x}}\}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}
it must either be connected to
{\bm{x}}
or to a point in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus(Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}))}
by a path in
Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})
. The second bound used that since the distance between
{\bm{x}}
and
\bm{z}
is at least
2s
, either there is a direct edge between them, or at least one of them must be connected to a point of
\mathcal{P}
at distance at least
s
away. Using (
16
) and (
17
) in the case of Model (P) and (
10
) and (
11
) in case of Model (L), we deduce that the whole term is
O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m})
.

Fifth term.

Since the distance between
\bm{z}
and
{\bm{y}}
is at least
s
, we may bound
\pi(\bm{z},{\bm{y}})
using (
17
) for Model (P) and (
11
) for Model (L).
This means that the integral is of order
O\big(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-2m-2}\big)
and hence the
\Lambda
-integral of this is of order

\Lambda(Q_{n})\lambda(Q_{n})^{-2m-2}\in O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-2m-1}).

∎

9.
Poisson approximation

In this section, we prove the Poisson approximation property stated in Theorem
3.1
using the theory of Palm couplings set up in Section
8
.
We again focus on Model (P) as it is the more challenging one.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Take
c_{\mathsf{St}}:=14\max(c_{\mathsf{es},2}^{-1},c_{\mathsf{v},2}^{-1},c_{\mathsf{D},2}^{-1})
such that Lemma
8.2
holds for with
m\leqslant 5
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
,
{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}
, be constructed as in Section
8
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a Poisson process with intensity measure
\Lambda
. For
{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
, we let
S_{n,{\bm{x}}}:=B_{3s}({\bm{x}})
. We bound the symmetric difference
(\Xi\Delta\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{!})(Q_{n})
in the integral on the right-hand side of (
59
) by the following three terms:

(67)
