\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}\Xi({\rm d}{\bm{y}})+\hskip-3.69899pt\int_{Q_{n}}\hskip-4.26773pt\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{!}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})+\hskip-2.84544pt\int_{Q_{n}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}=\varnothing\}(\Xi\Delta\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{!})({\rm d}y).

We now analyze the integral with respect to
\Lambda
of the expectation of each of the three terms separately.

First term.

For the first term, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}g({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})\mathcal{X}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big]=\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}).

Second term.
Recall that
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}:=\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\}
, where
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
is a Palm kernel of
\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\Xi
. The
\Lambda
-integral of the expectation of the second term in (
67
) is by the definition of
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
(
60
) and by the GNZ equation (
3
) given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}g({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi})\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X})g({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\})(\mathcal{X}\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\})({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\mathcal{X}({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}\neq\varnothing\}\mathbb{E}[g({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\})g({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{y}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{y}}\})\kappa({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}).

Third term.
We write the third term on the right-hand side of (
67
) as

\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}=\varnothing\}(\Xi[\mathcal{X}]\Delta(\Xi[\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}]\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\}))({\rm d}{\bm{y}}).

Now, note that a point
{\bm{y}}\in\Xi[\mathcal{X}]\Delta(\Xi[\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}]\setminus\{{\bm{x}}\})
with
S_{n,{\bm{x}}}\cap S_{n,{\bm{y}}}=\varnothing
can only contribute to the symmetric difference if
{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{X}
and
R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})>s
, if
{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
and
R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s
, or if
\max(R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}),R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}))\leqslant s
(with
R({\bm{x}},\varphi)=0
if
{\bm{x}}\notin\varphi
) and
(\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\cap Q_{n}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})
. Therefore, the third term in (
67
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})>s\}\mathcal{X}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})+\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s\}\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})

(68)

\displaystyle+(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})(Q_{n}\setminus S_{n,{\bm{x}}})\mathbbmss{1}\{(\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\cap Q_{n}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}.

The expectation of the first term in (
68
) is given by

(69)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})>s\}\mathcal{X}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\Big]=\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{y}}\})>s\}\kappa({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}}).

Hence, it follows from (
5
), (
22
) and the fact that
s\geqslant 3c_{\mathsf{es},2}^{-1}\log(\lambda(Q_{n}))
that the
\Lambda
-integral of (
69
) is
O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1})
. Similarly, the
\Lambda
-integral of the expectation of the second term in (
68
) is given by

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s\}\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\Big]\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{Q_{n}}\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbbmss{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\})>s\}g_{n}({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}\cup\{{\bm{y}}\})\kappa({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})]\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}),

which is bounded using Cauchy-Schwarz and a similar argument as (
69
) using
s\geqslant 6c_{\mathsf{es},2}^{-1}\log(\lambda(Q_{n}))
. For the
\Lambda
-integral of the expectation of the third term in (
68
) we have by Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi})(Q_{n}\setminus S_{n,{\bm{x}}})^{2}]\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{P}(Q_{n})^{2}]=2\lambda(Q_{n})^{2}+2\lambda(Q_{n})
by Poisson domination (since
\kappa_{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\leqslant 1
),

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi})(Q_{n}\setminus S_{n,{\bm{x}}})\mathbbmss{1}\{(\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\cap Q_{n}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}]\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi})(Q_{n}\setminus S_{n,{\bm{x}}})^{2}]\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}\big((\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\cap Q_{n}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\big)\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}

(70)

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(2\lambda(Q_{n})(\lambda(Q_{n})^{2}+\lambda(Q_{n}))\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}\big((\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\cap Q_{n}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\big)\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{1/2}.

By Lemma
8.2
with
m=5
, we have

(71)

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}\big((\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\cap Q_{n}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\big)\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\in O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-5}).

Hence, we conclude that (
9
) is in
O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1})
.
∎

10.
Quantitative normal approximation

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.2
, i.e., the quantitative normal approximation of the considered geometric functionals. Similarly as in
[
19
, Section 6]
, this will be achieved by applying the normal approximation method from
[
7
]
using Palm couplings. However, one important difference to
[
19
]
is that now we need to work with possibly unbounded interaction ranges. This requires us to invest substantial efforts into proving the novel Lemma
10.6
, which is a disagreement result for a thinning based on a Poisson point process that is independently resampled in some region.
Besides this novel ingredient, we briefly recall the main ideas of the Palm coupling from
[
7
]
to make our presentation self-contained. As in Sections
8
and
9
, we concentrate on Model (P), since Model (L) is very similar.

We again work in the setting of Section
8
. That is, we consider a coupling of the random measure
\Xi
from (
57
) and its Palm kernel
\{\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}\}_{{\bm{x}}\in Q}
defined in (
58
).
To state the main result, we put

(72)

\displaystyle Y_{{\bm{x}}}:=\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}(Q)-\Xi(Q),\quad\Delta_{{\bm{x}}}:=\frac{Y_{{\bm{x}}}}{\sigma}=\frac{\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}(Q)-\Xi(Q)}{\sigma},

where
\sigma^{2}:=\mathsf{Var}(\Xi(Q))
denotes the variance of the total mass of the random measure
\Xi
. To make our presentation self-contained, we recall
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
, which will be the key tool in the proof.

Theorem 10.1

(Theorem 3.1 in
[
7
]
)
.

Let
\Xi
,
\{\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}\}_{{\bm{x}}\in Q}
,
Y_{{\bm{x}}}
, and
\Delta_{{\bm{x}}}
be as in (
72
). Then,

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{K}}\Big(\frac{\Xi(Q)-\mathbb{E}[\Xi(Q)]}{\sigma},N(0,1)\Big)\leqslant 2E_{1}+5.5E_{2}+5E_{3}+10E_{4}+7E_{5}.

Here

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big|\int_{Q}(Y_{{\bm{x}}}\,\mathbbmss{1}\{|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|\leqslant\sigma\}-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{{\bm{x}}}\,\mathbbmss{1}\{|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|\leqslant\sigma\}\big])\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big|,

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sigma^{3}}\int_{Q}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\,\mathbbmss{1}\{|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|\leqslant\sigma\}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{Q}\mathbb{E}\big[|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|\,\mathbbmss{1}\{|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|>\sigma\}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle E_{4}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{Q}\int_{Q}\mathsf{Cov}\big(\phi_{{\bm{x}}}(t),\phi_{{\bm{y}}}(t)\big)\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle E_{5}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sigma}\Big(\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{Q}\int_{Q}|t|\,\mathsf{Cov}\big(\phi_{{\bm{x}}}(t),\phi_{{\bm{y}}}(t)\big)\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\,{\rm d}t\Big)^{1/2},

where to simplify the notation, we write

\displaystyle\phi_{{\bm{x}}}(t)=\begin{cases}\mathbbmss{1}\{1\geqslant\Delta_{{\bm{x}}}>t>0\},\quad&t>0,\\
\mathbbmss{1}\{-1\leqslant\Delta_{{\bm{x}}}<t<0\},\quad&t<0.\end{cases}

Remark 10.2
.

A subtle difference between the Palm couplings needed for (
59
) and Theorem
10.1
is that in (
59
), it is enough to have pairwise couplings of
\Xi
and
\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}
for each
{\bm{x}}
, while for Theorem
10.1
it is necessary to couple
\Xi
and
\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in Q
simultaneously. The couplings (
62
) and (
64
) are targeted to both applications, however, the need for simultaneous couplings in Theorem
10.1
substantially complicates the disagreement coupling set-up.

As in Section
9
, we always fix the scales
r:=6s:=6c_{\mathsf{St}}\log(\lambda(Q_{n}))
where
c_{\mathsf{St}}>0
is a constant that will be chosen according to Lemma
8.2
as well as Lemma
10.3
,
10.4
,
10.5
and
10.6
below.

10.1.
2-ball coupling.

In Section
9
, we provided the couplings between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
,
{\bm{x}}\in Q_{n}^{+}
, given in (
62
) and (
64
). In order to apply Theorem
10.1
, we need to compare not only the disagreement between
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
with
\mathcal{X}
but also between
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\Xi}
for
{\bm{y}}\neq{\bm{x}}
.
More precisely, we need to compare
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\Xi}
for
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in Q_{n}
satisfying
\|x-y\|>4r
. Since this is challenging to achieve directly, we define an intermediate process, which can be compared to both
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\Xi}
.
We first define the PI
\kappa^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
associated with the intermediate process. More precisely,

(73)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}(\bm{z},\varphi):=\begin{cases}\kappa\big(\bm{z},(\varphi\cap(B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})))\cup\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}\big),&\bm{z}\in B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}}),\\
\kappa\big(\bm{z},(\varphi\cap(B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})\setminus B_{s}({\bm{x}})))\cup\Psi_{{\bm{y}}}\big),&\bm{z}\in B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})\setminus B_{s}({\bm{y}}),\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then
\kappa_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime}
has the form of
\kappa^{\prime\prime}
in Lemma
6.14
. Thus, we can construct it by thinning a dominating RCM corresponding to the PI
\kappa
.
Denoting this thinning by
\bar{T}^{\prime\prime,{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}},\mathsf{RCM,cl}}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}:=\bar{T}^{\prime\prime,{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}},\mathsf{RCM,cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}(\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*})\cup(\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}\cap B_{s}({\bm{x}}))\cup(\Psi_{{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{s}({\bm{y}})).

Note that
\kappa^{\prime}_{{\bm{x}}},\kappa_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy Lemma
6.14
with
P_{1}=Q_{n}^{+}
,
P_{1,-}=B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
,
P_{3}=B_{s}({\bm{x}})
,
P_{2,-}=B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})\setminus B_{s}({\bm{y}})
,
\psi_{1}=\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}
,
\psi_{2}=\Psi_{{\bm{y}}}
and
\psi=\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}
, see also Figure
3
. Thus, by Theorem
6.13
with
A=B_{r}({\bm{x}})
and
P=B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})\neq\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\big(R({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s\big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\bar{T}^{\prime,{\bm{x}},\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}\big(\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}\big)\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})\neq\bar{T}^{\prime\prime,{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}},\mathsf{RCM},\mathsf{cl}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing,\varnothing}\big(\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}\big)\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\big(R({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})>s\big)+\mathbb{P}(E_{\mathsf{dens}})+\mathbb{P}\big(E_{\mathsf{perc}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(B_{r}({\bm{x}}),s)\big)+4\int_{B_{r+2s}({\bm{x}})}\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{z}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\Gamma_{\bm{z}}(Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing)}\mathcal{P}_{Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})}\big)\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})

(74)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+\int_{Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{r+2s}({\bm{x}})}\int_{B_{r+s}({\bm{x}})\cap Q_{n}^{+}}\pi(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\prime})\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z})\lambda({\rm d}\bm{z}^{\prime}).

For Model (L), the only necessary change is to invoke Theorem
5.9
instead of Theorem
6.13
.
The key task is to show that when integrated over
Q_{n}^{2}
, this disagreement expression is negligible as
n\uparrow\infty
.

Lemma 10.3

(Bound on 2-point error integrals)
.

Suppose the PI satisfies the conditions of Theorem
3.2
.
Let
m\geqslant 1
. If
c_{\mathsf{St}}>0
is chosen sufficiently large (depending on
m
), then

\iint_{Q_{n}^{2}\cap\{\|x-y\|>4r\}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{!,\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})\neq\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})\big)\Lambda^{2}({\rm d}{\bm{x}},{\rm d}{\bm{y}})\in O(\lambda(Q_{n})^{-m}).

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
10.3
is very similar to the proof of Lemma
8.2
. Therefore, we omit it.
∎

10.2.
Two auxiliary lemmas

Having specified the couplings, we now proceed with the application of the general Theorem
10.1
to our setting. While bounding the expressions in Theorem
10.1
for general Palm measures may be cumbersome, for score sums of Gibbs processes, the task simplifies substantially. In earlier work, we reduced the problem to two key auxiliary results, namely
[
19
, Lemmas 6.3, 6.4]
. We restate them below to make the manuscript self-contained. The first of the results is a moment bound on
Y_{{\bm{x}}}
.

Lemma 10.4

(Moment bound for
Y_{{\bm{x}}}
)
.

Assume that the PI
\kappa
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
3.2
. For
m\leqslant 4
and
n\geqslant 1
, if
c_{\mathsf{St}}
is sufficiently large, we have

\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|^{m}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\leqslant c_{\mathsf{Y}}\lambda(Q_{n})s^{dm},

where
c_{\mathsf{Y}}=c_{\mathsf{Y}}(d,\alpha,c_{\mathsf{es},1},c_{\mathsf{es},2},c_{\mathsf{D},1},c_{\mathsf{D},2},c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{v},2},c_{\mathsf{m}})>0
.

The second of these results is a covariance bound.

Lemma 10.5

(Covariance bound)
.

Assume that the PI
\kappa
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
3.2
. Let
F:\mathbf{N}\to[0,\infty)
be measurable and
p>0
and assume that there is some
q_{0}=q_{0}(d,\alpha,c_{\mathsf{es},1},c_{\mathsf{es},2},c_{\mathsf{D},1},c_{\mathsf{D},2},c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{v},2},c_{\mathsf{m}})>0

such that
F(\varphi)\leqslant\lambda(Q_{n})^{p}\varphi(\mathbb{X})
for
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}
and
\lambda(Q_{n})>q_{0}
. Then, for
c_{\mathsf{St}}
sufficiently large and
\lambda(Q_{n})>q_{0}
,

\iint_{Q_{n}^{2}\cap\{\|x-y\|>4r\}}\mathsf{Cov}\big(F_{\bm{x}},F_{\bm{y}})\big)\,\Lambda^{2}({\rm d}{\bm{x}},{\rm d}{\bm{y}})\leqslant\lambda(Q_{n})^{-1},

where
F_{\bm{z}}=F(\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}(\bm{z}))
.

The analogue of Lemma
10.5
holds when one or more of
F_{\bm{x}}
and
F_{\bm{y}}
are replaced by
F(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}}))
or
F(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{y}}))
, but we only give the proof in the case stated in the lemma.
We now prove Lemma
10.4
and
10.5
. The proofs rely on disagreement coupling through Lemma
8.2
and
10.3
.

Proof of Lemma
10.4
.

We decompose
Y_{{\bm{x}}}
as in
[
19
]
. That is, set

(75)

\displaystyle\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{\leqslant}:=\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\cap Q_{n}}g({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\mathds{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi})\leqslant s\}\delta_{{\bm{y}}},\qquad\Xi^{\leqslant}:=\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{n}}g({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})\mathds{1}\{R({\bm{y}},\mathcal{X})\leqslant s\}\delta_{{\bm{y}}}.

and let
\Xi^{>}:=\Xi-\Xi^{\leqslant}
and
\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{>}:=\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}-\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{\leqslant}
.
Then, we can decompose
Y_{{\bm{x}}}
as

\displaystyle\big(|\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{>}|-|\Xi^{>}|\big)+\big(\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{\leqslant}(B_{r}({\bm{x}}))-\Xi^{\leqslant}(B_{r}({\bm{x}}))\big)+\big(\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{\leqslant}(B_{r}({\bm{x}})^{c})-\Xi^{\leqslant}(B_{r}({\bm{x}})^{c})\big)\mathbbmss{1}\{\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}.

The main idea of the proof is then to apply Lemma
8.2
to bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}}))
.

From the Hölder inequality applied to both the expectation and the integral with respect to
\Lambda
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}[|Y_{{\bm{x}}}|^{m}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\leqslant\hskip-11.38092pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{6})},\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{6}=m\end{subarray}}d_{I}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{\leqslant}\big(B_{r}({\bm{x}})\big)^{m}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{i_{1}}{m}}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Xi^{\leqslant}\big(B_{r}({\bm{x}})\big)^{m}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{i_{2}}{m}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}^{>}|^{m}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{i_{3}}{m}}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\Xi^{>}|^{m}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{i_{4}}{m}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}|^{m}\mathbbmss{1}\{\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{i_{5}}{m}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\Xi|^{m}\mathbbmss{1}\{\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{i_{6}}{m}}\Bigg]

for some
d_{I}>0
. As explained in
[
19
, Lemma 6.3]
, the most difficult task is the fifth integral. First, by the Hölder inequality, this integral is bounded above by

\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}|^{m}\mathbbmss{1}\{\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\leqslant\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\hskip-8.5359pt\mathbb{E}\big[|\Xi_{{\bm{x}}}|^{m+1}\big]\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\Big(\int_{Q_{n}}\hskip-8.5359pt\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\big)\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)^{\frac{1}{m+1}}.

Now, we can apply Lemma
8.2
to control the integral

\int_{Q_{n}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\not\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\big)\,\Lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})
, thereby concluding the proof.
∎

We next sketch the proof of Lemma
10.5
and explain the main differences from
[
19
]
.

Proof of Lemma
10.5
.

Assume that
\|x-y\|>4r
. For
\bm{z}\in\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\}
, we set
R_{\bm{z}}:=R(\bm{z},\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\Xi})
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Cov}\big(F_{{\bm{x}}},F_{{\bm{y}}}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Cov}\big(\tilde{F}_{{\bm{x}}},\tilde{F}_{{\bm{y}}}\big)+\mathsf{Cov}\big(\tilde{F}_{{\bm{x}}},F_{{\bm{y}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{R_{{\bm{y}}}>s\}\big)+\mathsf{Cov}\big(F_{{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{R_{{\bm{x}}}>s\},F_{{\bm{y}}}\big),

where

\tilde{F}_{\bm{z}}:=F\big(\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}(\bm{z})\big)\mathbbmss{1}\big\{R_{\bm{z}}\leqslant s\big\}.

We only discuss the first covariance since the second and third can be bounded using (
60
) and the exponential stabilization (
22
) as in
[
19
, Lemma 6.4]
.

We introduce the agreement event
S_{\bm{z}}:=\big\{(\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\Xi}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime})\cap B_{r}(\bm{z})=\varnothing\big\}
. Lemma
10.3
provides bounds on the probability of the complementary event
\mathbb{P}(S_{\bm{x}}^{c})
. This allows us to further reduce to the consideration of the covariance
\mathsf{Cov}\big(\tilde{F}_{{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}_{S_{{\bm{x}}}},\tilde{F}_{{\bm{y}}}\mathbbmss{1}_{S_{{\bm{y}}}}\big)
. However, this covariance vanishes since
\Psi_{{\bm{x}}}
and
\Psi_{{\bm{y}}}
are independent by construction and given these,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})
are independent. To see this, note that one can construct
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime}
by a Poisson thinning that first considers
B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
and then
B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})
. Since the PI on
B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})
does not depend on the result of thinning
B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
, the distributions of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{r+3s}({\bm{x}})
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cap B_{r+3s}({\bm{y}})
are independent.
∎

10.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.2

In this section,
C>0
denotes a constant which may change value from line to line. It may depend on
d,\alpha,c_{\mathsf{es},1},c_{\mathsf{es},2},c_{\mathsf{D},1},c_{\mathsf{D},2},c_{\mathsf{v},1},c_{\mathsf{v},2},c_{\mathsf{m}}
, but not on
n
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Given Lemma
10.4
and
10.5
, the proof of Theorem
3.2
follows exactly as in
[
19
]
. There is one error in that proof, which we now correct. When bounding
E_{4}
from Theorem
10.1
, the computation was reduced to showing that for a given
l\geqslant 1
,
c_{\mathsf{St}}
can be chosen so large that

\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathsf{Cov}(\phi_{(x,m)}(t),\phi_{(y,m^{\prime})}(t))\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime})\leqslant C\lambda(Q_{n})^{-l}

whenever
\|x-y\|>4r
. In the following, let
{\bm{x}}=(x,m)
and
{\bm{y}}=(y,m^{\prime})
.
Similar to
[
19
]
, we let

U_{\bm{x}}:=\big\{\mathcal{X}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\bm{x}}^{\Xi}\subseteq B_{4s}({\bm{x}})\}\cap\big\{\max_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{n}}R({\bm{w}},\mathcal{X})\leqslant s\big\}\cap\big\{\max_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\bm{x}}^{\Xi}\cap Q_{n}}R({\bm{w}},\mathcal{X}_{\bm{x}}^{\Xi})\leqslant s\big\}.

Then, since
0\leqslant\phi_{{\bm{x}}}(t)\leqslant 1
,

|\mathsf{Cov}(\phi_{{\bm{x}}}(t),\phi_{{\bm{y}}}(t))-\mathsf{Cov}(\phi_{\bm{x}}(t)\mathbbmss{1}\{U_{\bm{x}}\},\phi_{\bm{y}}(t)\mathbbmss{1}\{U_{\bm{y}}\})|\leqslant\mathbb{P}(U_{\bm{x}}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(U_{\bm{y}}^{c}).

Under
U_{\bm{x}}
,

(76)

\displaystyle\phi_{{\bm{x}}}(t)=\phi_{{\bm{x}},r}(t):=\begin{cases}\mathbbmss{1}\{1\geqslant\frac{\Xi[\mathcal{X}_{\bm{x}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})](B_{5s}({\bm{x}}))-\Xi[\mathcal{X}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})](B_{5s}({\bm{x}}))}{\sigma}>t>0\},\quad&t>0,\\
\mathbbmss{1}\{-1\leqslant\frac{\Xi[\mathcal{X}_{\bm{x}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})](B_{5s}({\bm{x}}))-\Xi[\mathcal{X}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})](B_{5s}({\bm{x}}))}{\sigma}<t<0\},\quad&t<0.\end{cases}

Thus,

|\mathsf{Cov}(\phi_{{\bm{x}},r}(t),\phi_{{\bm{y}},r}(t))-\mathsf{Cov}(\phi_{\bm{x}}(t)\mathbbmss{1}\{U_{\bm{x}}\},\phi_{\bm{y}}(t)\mathbbmss{1}\{U_{\bm{y}}\})|\leqslant\mathbb{P}(U_{\bm{x}}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(U_{\bm{y}}^{c}).

The integral of
\mathbb{P}(U_{\bm{x}}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(U_{\bm{y}}^{c})
can be bounded using Lemma
8.2
and (
22
). Hence, it is enough to bound the integral over the marks of
\mathsf{Cov}(\phi_{{\bm{x}},r}(t),\phi_{{\bm{y}},r}(t))
.
However,
\phi_{{\bm{x}},r}(t)
is not of the form
F(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}}))
, as claimed in
[
19
]
, since it depends on the joint distribution of
\mathcal{X}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\Xi}\cap B_{r}({\bm{x}})
. Hence, Lemma
10.5
does not apply.

Instead, we introduce four interpolating PIs of the form

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{z}}({\bf w},\varphi)=\kappa\big({\bf w},\varphi\cap B_{r+3s}(\bm{z})\big)\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bf w}\in B_{r+3s}(\bm{z})\}

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}_{\bm{z}}({\bf w},\varphi)=\kappa\big({\bf w},(\varphi\cap(B_{r+3s}(\bm{z})\setminus B_{s}(\bm{z})))\cup\Psi_{\bm{z}}\big)\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bf w}\in B_{r+3s}(\bm{z})\setminus B_{s}(\bm{z})\},

where
\bm{z}\in\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\}
. Denote the corresponding processes by
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
.
With high probability,
\mathcal{X}\cap B_{r}(\bm{z})=\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap B_{r}(\bm{z})
and
\mathcal{X}^{\Xi}_{\bm{z}}\cap B_{r}(\bm{z})=\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}\cap B_{r}(\bm{z})
by the same argument as in Lemma
8.2
and
10.3
. It follows that

\big|\mathsf{Cov}({\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}(t),{\phi}_{{\bm{y}},r}(t))-\mathsf{Cov}({\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),{\phi}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t))\big|\leqslant C\lambda(Q_{n})^{-l},

where
{\phi}_{\bm{z},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
are computed by inserting the processes
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
in (
76
).

It remains to bound
\mathsf{Cov}({\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),{\phi}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t))
. While the processes
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
are pairwise independent of
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
, the coupling
(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi})
may be correlated with the coupling
(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi})
because the underlying Poisson process is explored in the same order.
Loosely speaking, we will instead show that the joint distribution of
(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi})
is close to independent of the joint distribution
(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi})
.

To formalize this for Model (L), let
\check{\check{\mathcal{P}}}^{*}
be an independent copy of
\mathcal{P}^{*}
. Define a new Poisson process
{\check{\mathcal{P}}}^{*}=\mathcal{P}_{B_{2r}({\bm{x}})}^{*}\cup\check{\check{\mathcal{P}}}^{*}_{Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{2r}({\bm{x}})}
. We write
\check{\mathcal{X}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
\check{\check{\mathcal{X}}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
etc. depending on which Poisson process was used in the thinning and
\check{\phi}_{\bm{z},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
or
\check{\check{\phi}}_{\bm{z},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
when
\check{\mathcal{X}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\check{\mathcal{X}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
or
\check{\check{\mathcal{X}}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\check{\check{\mathcal{X}}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
, respectively, was used to define (
76
).

The joint distribution of the four processes
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
,
\bm{z}\in\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\}
, will be the same as that of
\check{\mathcal{X}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\check{\mathcal{X}}_{\bm{z}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}
. It follows that

\mathsf{Cov}({\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),{\phi}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t))=\mathsf{Cov}(\check{\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),\check{\phi}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)).

On the other hand, Lemma
10.6
below shows that

\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}|\mathsf{Cov}(\check{\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),\check{\phi}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t))-\mathsf{Cov}({\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),\check{\check{\phi}}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t))|\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime})\leqslant 4C\lambda(Q_{n})^{-l}.

But
\mathsf{Cov}({\phi}_{{\bm{x}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t),\check{\check{\phi}}_{{\bm{y}},r}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t))=0
since
\mathcal{P}
and
\check{\check{\mathcal{P}}}
are independent, which shows the claim for Model (L).

For Model (P), we take independent copies
\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
and
\check{\check{\Phi}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
. From the underlying Poisson processes
\mathcal{P}
and
\check{\check{\mathcal{P}}}
, we construct
\check{\mathcal{P}}
as under Model (L). Given
\check{\mathcal{P}}
, we add i.i.d. marks to form
\check{\Phi}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
in such a way that marks
r_{\bm{z},{\bm{w}}}
with
\bm{z},{\bm{w}}\in B_{2r}({\bm{x}})
or
\bm{z},{\bm{w}}\in Q_{n}^{+}\setminus B_{2r}({\bm{x}})
agree with those in
\Phi_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
or
\check{\check{\Phi}}_{Q_{n}^{+},\varnothing}^{*}
, respectively. From there, the argument proceeds the same.
∎

The following lemma was used in the proof of Theorem
3.2
. Since the proof of the lemma involves some technical disagreement coupling arguments, we postpone it to Section
13
.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
l\geqslant 1
. Under the conditions of Theorem
3.2
, there is a
C
(depending on
l
) such that if
c_{\mathsf{St}}
is chosen sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\neq\check{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime}),\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}\neq\check{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi})\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{P}(\check{\check{\mathcal{X}}}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\neq\check{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime}),\int_{\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{P}(\check{\check{\mathcal{X}}}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi}\neq\check{\mathcal{X}}_{{\bm{y}}}^{\prime\prime\prime,\Xi})\mu({\rm d}m)\mu({\rm d}m^{\prime})\bigg\}\leqslant C\lambda(Q_{n})^{-l}.

11.
Proofs for Section
5

Proof of Corollary
5.8
.

We divide
A
into disjoint sets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
such that
\iota({\bm{x}})<\iota({\bm{y}})
for
{\bm{x}}\in A_{i}
,
{\bm{y}}\in A_{i+1}
and we assume
\lambda(A_{i})={\lambda(A)}/{N}
for all
i
. Such a partition can be found by using
\iota
to map
A
into
\mathbb{R}
and dividing
\mathbb{R}
into intervals of equal push-forward measure. Let
T_{i}=T_{Q,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A_{i}
and
T_{i}^{\prime}=T_{Q,\varnothing}^{\prime}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A_{i}
. Then, using Lemma
5.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(T_{Q,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\neq T^{\prime}_{Q,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}(T_{i}\neq T_{i}^{\prime},T_{j}=T_{j}^{\prime},j<i)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}(A_{i})>1)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}(2+\lambda(A_{i}))

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbmss{1}\{T_{j}=T_{j}^{\prime},j<i\}d_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{X}\Big(Q\setminus\bigcup_{j<i}A_{i},\bigcup_{j<i}T_{j}\Big)\cap A_{i},\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\Big(Q\setminus\bigcup_{j<i}A_{i},\bigcup_{j<i}T_{j}^{\prime}\Big)\cap A_{i}\mid T_{j},T_{j}^{\prime},j<i\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}(A_{i})>1)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}(2+\lambda(A_{i}))\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A_{i}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P_{1}}\cup\bar{\eta})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}(A_{i})>1)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}2\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{A_{i}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P_{1}}\cup\bar{\eta})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda(A_{i})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}(A_{i})>0)

Here, the total variation distance is in the conditional distribution given
T_{j},T_{j}^{\prime},j<i
. This was bounded in the second inequality by constructing the two Gibbs processes on
Q\setminus\cup_{j<i}A_{j}
as follows: First construct both processes on
Q\setminus P
by some thinning of
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q\setminus P}
. Then, we use a cluster-based thinning that starts by exploring all clusters connected to
B=\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta}
. After all such clusters are explored, the PIs will look the same on
P\setminus\cup_{j<i}A_{j}
and the boundary conditions will agree since
T_{j}=T_{j}^{\prime}
for
j<i
. Thus, the result follows from Proposition
5.6
.
The first and the last sum are of order
N^{-1}
when
N\to\infty
, while the middle sum is bounded by

\sum_{i=1}^{N}2\int_{A_{i}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{x}}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\bar{\eta}\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P_{1}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})=2\int_{A}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{x}}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\bar{\eta}\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P_{1}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}),

as asserted.
∎

Next, we move to the proof of Theorem
5.9
. Recall from Section
5.2
that we divide
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{M}
into (marked) cubes of spatial diameter at most 1 and let
C_{l}=C_{l}^{0}\times\mathbb{M}
denote those cubes intersecting
Q
,
l=1,2,\ldots,L
.

Proof of Theorem
5.9
.

Both
\mathcal{X}:=T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:=T^{\prime,\mathsf{cl}}_{Q,\varnothing,\varnothing}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
visit the points of
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
in the same order.
The strategy is to bound the error probabilities for each step in the algorithmic construction. For that purpose, let
V_{i}=S_{i}\setminus S_{i-1}
be the set explored in the
i
th step and
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\mathcal{X}\cap V_{i}
,
\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cap V_{i}
be the parts of the Gibbs processes generated in the
i
th step. Moreover, let
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{i}
be the part of the graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}
obtained in the
i
th step.

Note that under the event
E_{\mathsf{dens}}^{c}
, there are at most
M:=4\lambda(Q)
steps. Under the event
E_{\mathsf{perc}}(A,s)
, if
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
agree on all clusters completely contained in
B_{s}(A)
, then they agree on
A
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}\cap A\neq\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cap A,E_{\mathsf{perc}}(A,s)^{c}\cap E_{\mathsf{dens}}^{c}\big)\leqslant\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{i}\neq\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime},E_{i}^{1}\cup E_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{P}^{*}_{S_{i-1}})\Big],

where
E_{i}^{1}
denotes the event that for all
j\leqslant i-1
with
\mathcal{P}_{V_{j}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{i-1}}\mathcal{P}_{V_{i-1}}
it holds that
\mathcal{P}_{V_{j}}\subseteq B_{s}(A)
and
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\mathcal{X}_{j}^{\prime}
(that is, we continue exploring a cluster so far contained in
B_{s}(A)
and the two Gibbs processes so far agree on the cluster) and
E_{i}^{2}
is the event that
\mathcal{P}_{V_{i-1}}=\varnothing
and
\mathcal{P}_{V_{i}}\subseteq B_{s}(A)
(so we start a new cluster from a point in
B_{s}(A)
).

Under
E_{i}^{1}
, we have the bound from Corollary
5.8

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}_{i}\neq\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime},E_{i}^{1}\mid\mathcal{P}^{*}_{S_{i-1}}\big)\leqslant 2\int_{V_{i}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}\big\{\mathcal{P}_{V_{i-1}}\subseteq B_{s}(A)\big\}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\int_{V_{i}\cap B_{2s}(A)}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})+2\lambda(N(\mathcal{P}_{V_{i-1}})\cap(Q\setminus B_{2s}(A)))\mathbbmss{1}\big\{\mathcal{P}_{V_{i-1}}\subseteq B_{s}(A)\big\}

since
V_{i}\subseteq N(V_{i-1})
.
Summing over
i
, and remembering that the
V_{i}
are disjoint, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{i}\neq\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime},E_{i}^{1}\mid\mathcal{P}^{*}_{S_{i-1}})\leqslant 2\int_{B_{2s}(A)}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})+2\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{P}_{B_{s}(A)}}\lambda\big(N({\bm{x}})\cap(Q\setminus B_{2s}(A))\big).

Taking expectations, we get

(77)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{i}\neq\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime},E_{i}^{1}\mid\mathcal{P}^{*}_{S_{i-1}})\Big]\hskip-2.84544pt\leqslant\hskip-2.84544pt2\int_{B_{2s}(A)}\hskip-22.76228pt\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\hskip-2.84544pt+\hskip-2.84544pt2\int_{B_{s}(A)}\int_{Q\setminus B_{2s}(A)}\hskip-27.03003pt\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\sim{\bm{y}}\}\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}).

Under
E_{i}^{2}
, we construct the Gibbs process on
Q\setminus S_{i-1}
by a standard Poisson embedding and take the thinning of the first Poisson point as
\mathcal{X}_{i}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{i}
, respectively. By the choice of ordering, the Poisson embedding on
Q\setminus S_{i-1}
is first made inside
(Q\setminus S_{i-1})\cap C_{L}
. Conditionally on the result, we make a Poisson embedding on
(Q\setminus S_{i-1})\cap C_{L-1}
and so on. Thus, Corollary
5.8
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{X}_{i}\neq\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime},E_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{P}^{*}_{S_{i-1}}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathbbmss{1}\big\{C_{l}\cap B_{s}(A)\neq\varnothing\big\}\mathbb{P}\Big(\inf{}_{\iota}(\mathcal{X}_{i}\cap C_{l})\neq\inf{}_{\iota}(\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\prime}\cap C_{l})\mid\mathcal{P}_{C_{l^{\prime}}\cap(Q\setminus S_{i-1})}=\varnothing,l^{\prime}>l,\mathcal{P}^{*}_{S_{i-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{l=1}^{L}2\int_{B_{s+1}(A)\cap C_{l}}\mathbb{P}\big({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta}\big)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})

(78)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\int_{B_{s+1}(A)}\mathbb{P}\big({\bm{x}}\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{\bm{x}}\cup\bar{\eta}}\mathcal{P}_{Q\setminus P}\cup\bar{\eta}\big)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}).

Adding (
77
), (
78
), and the probability that we are not under
E_{\mathsf{dens}}^{c}\cup E_{\mathsf{perc}}(A,s)^{c}
yields the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.10
.

The cluster-based thinning algorithm
T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{n},\xi_{n},\psi}
will first move through all the clusters having points connected to
\xi_{n}
. These are contained in the clusters of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{U})
intersecting
U\setminus W_{n}
. Afterwards, it explores the remaining clusters in the order of their
\iota
-smallest point. Let
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{P}
be the
\iota
-smallest point connected to
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and suppose
{\bm{x}}\in W_{n}
. Then, by the assumption on
\iota
, all clusters with
\iota
-smallest point larger than
{\bm{x}}
must also be contained in
W_{n}
. Thus, the order in which these clusters are visited will be the same for all
m\geqslant n
. When the thinning algorithm reaches
{\bm{x}}
, the unexplored part of
U
will be the points in
{U_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}
that are not neighbors to a cluster that contains a point in
\mathcal{P}_{U_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}}
. Again, this set does not depend on
m\geqslant n
. Hence, on the event
\big\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\not\leftrightsquigarrow_{\mathcal{P}_{U}}\mathcal{P}_{U\setminus W_{n}}\big\}
,
T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{m},\xi_{m},\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{m}})
will agree on all clusters connected to
A
whenever
m\geqslant n
.

For the last statement, we need to show that
T^{\infty}_{U,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})
satisfies the GNZ equation (
3
) for any
f:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbf{N}\to[0,1]
with support in
W_{s}\times\mathbf{N}
and such that
f({\bm{x}},\varphi)=f({\bm{x}},\varphi\cap W_{s})
for some
s>0
. Let
n>s
. Conditionally on
\xi_{n}
,
T^{\mathsf{cl}}_{U_{n},\xi_{n},\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U_{n}})
is distributed as a
\mathcal{X}(U_{n},\psi)
-process with PI
\kappa
, so this also holds unconditionally. Hence it satisfies the GNZ equation (
4
) for
f
. Taking limits and applying dominated convergence, we see that also
T^{\infty}_{U,\psi}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{U})
satisfies the GNZ equation (
4
) for
f
and
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
and hence is an infinite-volume Gibbs point process with PI
\kappa
, which is unique by Theorem
2.3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.11
.

In the following, we write
\mathcal{X}_{U}:=\mathcal{X}\cap U
,
B=\mathbb{X}\setminus U
, and
\mathcal{X}_{B}:=\mathcal{X}\cap B
. From the GNZ equations applied to a function of the form
f\cdot h
where
f:U\times\mathbf{N}_{U}\to[0,1]
and
h:\mathbf{N}_{B}\to[0,1]
, we get

\displaystyle\int\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{U}f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{U})\mathcal{X}({\rm d}{\bm{x}})|\mathcal{X}_{B}=\psi\Big]h(\psi)\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}_{B}}({\rm d}\psi)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{U}f({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{U}\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})\kappa({\bm{x}},\mathcal{X}_{U}\cup\psi)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})|\mathcal{X}_{B}=\psi\Big]h(\psi)\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}_{B}}({\rm d}\psi).

Thus, for
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}_{B}}
-almost all
\psi
,
\mathcal{X}_{U}\mid\mathcal{X}_{B}=\psi
satisfies the GNZ equation of
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
. Since the distribution of
\mathcal{X}(U,\psi)
is unique by Theorem
2.3
, we deduce
\mathcal{X}_{U}\mid\mathcal{X}_{B}=\psi\sim T^{\infty}_{U,\psi}({\mathcal{P}}^{*}_{U})
and hence the claim follows.
∎

12.
Proofs for Section
6

12.1.
Set-up

The model with pairwise interaction does not fit the framework described in Section
5
directly because the RCM does not come from a symmetric relation on
\mathbb{X}
. Therefore, we start by discussing the construction from
[
3
]
of an approximating Gibbs process on some enlarged space. There is a symmetric relation on this enlarged space such that (
6
) holds and the associated graph approximates the RCM.
This construction will be the main ingredient in the proofs. The approximation of the RCM is given in Section
12.1.1
and the approximating Gibbs process is defined in Section
12.1.2
.

12.1.1.
Approximation of the RCM

We first review the construction from
[
3
]
. The idea is to approximate Gibbs processes with a non-negative pair potential on
Q
by constructing an approximating Gibbs process on a marked space

Q\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}

equipped with the product measure
\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
More precisely, for each
\delta>0
we choose a partition of
\mathbb{X}
into disjoint Borel sets
D_{1}^{\delta},D_{2}^{\delta},\dots
such that for any
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{X}
there is a
\delta_{0}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
such that
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
are separated by the partition for
\delta<\delta_{0}
. The sequence should be nested in the sense that for
\delta^{\prime}\leqslant\delta
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
there is a
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
D_{i}^{\delta^{\prime}}\subseteq D_{j}^{\delta}
. The index set
\mathbb{N}
of
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
corresponds to the indices of the sets
D_{i}^{\delta}
. We write
\mathbb{U}^{\delta}=[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
to emphasize this correspondence and
Q^{\delta}=Q\times\mathbb{U}^{\delta}
for
Q\subseteq\mathbb{X}
etc. For each
\delta>0
, we have the maps
h^{\delta}:{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}\to\mathbb{X}

and
h^{*,\delta}:{(\mathbb{X}^{*})^{\delta}}\to\mathbb{X}
given by

h^{\delta}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})={\bm{x}},\qquad h^{*,\delta}({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})={\bm{x}}.

We assume throughout that
\varphi^{\delta}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}
denotes a configuration of points that satisfies
h^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})=\varphi
, and
\varphi^{\delta,0}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}
denotes the special case where all the points in
\varphi
are equipped with the zero-sequence as mark. The latter notation is used in contexts where we want to lift a given
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}}
to an element of
\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}
and the choice of marks plays no role.
In practice, it will be enough to consider a countable sequence of
\delta
’s tending to 0, so that properties that hold almost surely for each
\delta
also hold almost surely for all
\delta
simultaneous. However, we will not make this sequence explicit.

We lift the ordering
\iota
to a map
\iota^{\delta}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{U}^{\delta}\to[0,1]
by
\iota^{\delta}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})=\iota({\bm{x}})
, which defines a partial order on
\mathbb{X}^{\delta}
by
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\leqslant_{\iota^{\delta}}({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})
if
{\bm{x}}<_{\iota}{\bm{y}}
or
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})=({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})
.
Define

(79)

R^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}}))=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbbmss{1}_{D^{\delta}_{i}}({\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}_{D^{\delta}_{j}}({\bm{y}})(\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})<_{\iota^{\delta}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\}r_{j}+\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})<_{\iota^{\delta}}({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})\}s_{i})

where
{\bm{r}}=(r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
{\bm{s}}=(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Then, a symmetric relation
\sim_{\delta}
on
\mathbb{X}^{\delta}
is given by

(80)

({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\sim_{\delta}({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})\text{ iff }R^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}}))\leq\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}).

For
\varphi^{\delta},\psi^{\delta}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}
, we can define the graph
G^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta},\psi^{\delta})
on
\mathbb{X}^{\delta}
with vertex set
\varphi^{\delta}\cup\psi^{\delta}
and an edge between
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in\varphi^{\delta}
and
({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})\in\varphi^{\delta}\cup\psi^{\delta}
if
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\sim_{\delta}({\bm{y}},{\bm{s}})
.

To see how we can approximate the RCM on
Q
by graphs on
Q^{\delta}
, let
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*}
be a Poisson process on
\bar{Q}^{*}
and let
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
be finite. Define Poisson processes
\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}
on
Q^{*,\delta}:=Q^{*}\times\mathbb{U}^{\delta}
coupled with
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*}
as follows.
A point
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi}
corresponds to
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}}^{\delta})\in\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}
, where

r_{i}^{\delta}=r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}_{i}}

for
{\bm{y}}_{i}=\inf_{\iota}\big\{{\bm{y}}\in D_{i}^{\delta}\cap(\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup\psi)\big\}

when
D_{i}^{\delta}\cap(\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup\psi)\neq\varnothing
, and otherwise
r_{i}^{\delta}
is chosen uniformly at random. Then,
\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}
projected to
Q^{*}
is
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}
and hence a Poisson process. Given
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}
, the marks in
\mathbb{U}^{\delta}
are independent uniform i.i.d. sequences. Thus,
\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}
is a Poisson process on
Q^{*}\times\mathbb{U}^{\delta}
.
We can form the graph
G^{\delta}\big(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}\big)
on
\mathbb{X}^{\delta}
. If
\psi
is finite, there is a
\delta_{0}(\omega)
for almost all
\omega
such that
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup\psi
is separated by the partition whenever
\delta<\delta_{0}
. For such
\delta
, the projected graph
h^{\delta}\big(G^{\delta}\big(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}\big)\big)
on
\mathbb{X}
will agree with
\Gamma(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*},\psi)
. Therefore, we think of
h^{\delta}\big(G^{\delta}\big(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}\big)\big)
as approximations of the RCM
\Gamma\big(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*},\psi\big)
that converge almost surely when
\delta\to 0
.

12.1.2.
Approximation of the Gibbs process

Define a PI on
\mathbb{X}^{\delta}
defined for
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in\mathbb{X}^{\delta}
and
\varphi^{\delta}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}
by

(81)

\kappa^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\varphi^{\delta})=\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\not\sim_{\delta}\varphi^{\delta}\}.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{\delta}(Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0})
denote a Gibbs process on
Q^{\delta}
with PI
\kappa^{\delta}
with boundary condition
\psi^{\delta,0}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}\setminus Q^{\delta}}
where
\psi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus Q}
. This
\kappa^{\delta}
satisfies (
5
) and (
6
) with the relation
\sim_{\delta}
.
It is shown in
[
3
, Lem. 3.1]
that for finite
\psi
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\in E)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{P}(h^{\delta}(\mathcal{X}^{\delta}(Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}))\in E)

for any
E\in\mathcal{N}
. In fact, the proof shows that

(82)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(h^{\delta}(\mathcal{X}^{\delta}(Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0})),\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi))=0.

However, no explicit couplings of these processes were given in
[
3
]
. Such couplings can be provided via the coupled Poisson processes
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta}
. The processes
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0},\iota^{\delta}}(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta})

provide a coupling between the processes
\mathcal{X}^{\delta}(Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0})
and we will see in the next section how to couple these approximating processes with
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

Note here that the map
\iota^{\delta}
is not injective, but
(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\mathbb{N}})((\iota^{\delta})^{-1}(t))=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence
\iota^{\delta}
defines a total ordering on
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta}
a.s., and Proposition
12.1
shows that the Poisson embedding
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0},\iota^{\delta}}(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*,\delta})
is well-defined a.s. and that the results of Section
5.1
carry over. This was shown in the Euclidean case in
[
19
, Prop. 4.2]
. The case of general Borel spaces is similar, thus we only sketch the generalization below.

Proposition 12.1
.

Assume that
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\iota:Q\to\mathbb{R}
is a measurable map such that
\lambda(\iota^{-1}(t))=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then,
T_{Q,\psi,\iota}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
is well-defined almost surely and is distributed as
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
. Moreover, Proposition
5.4
, Lemma
5.7
, Corollary
5.8
, and Proposition
5.10
also hold with this
\iota
.

Proof.

First, we note that
\iota
defines a total ordering on
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}
almost surely and the thinning probabilities
p({\bm{x}},Q,\psi)
are measurable functions of
{\bm{x}}
and
\psi
. Hence, the standard Poisson embedding
T_{Q,\psi,\iota}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})
is well-defined almost surely.
To see that this is distributed as
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
, consider for each
k\geqslant 1
a partition of
Q
of the form
D_{i}^{k}=\iota^{-1}((i2^{-k},(i+1)2^{-k}])
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Choose an injective ordering
\iota_{i}^{k}:D_{i}^{k}\to(i2^{-k},(i+1)2^{-k}]
on each
D_{i}^{k}
(e.g. choose an injective ordering
\tilde{\iota}:Q\to(0,1]
and let
\iota_{i}^{k}({\bm{x}})=2^{-k}(i+\tilde{\iota}({\bm{x}}))
). These
\iota^{k}_{i}
are then pieced together to form an injective map
\iota^{k}:Q\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using these orderings, we obtain a Poisson embedding map
T_{Q,\psi,\iota^{k}}
for each
k
such that
T_{Q,\psi,\iota^{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
. One can now show, using the same reasoning as in the proof of
[
19
, Proposition 4.3]
, that almost surely, there is a
K(\omega)
such that for all
k>K

T_{Q,\psi,\iota^{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}){=}T_{Q,\psi,\iota}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})

and that this implies
T_{Q,\psi,\iota}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.
For Lemma
5.7
, we may assume that
\iota(A)\subseteq(-\infty,0)
and
\iota(Q\setminus A)\subseteq(1,\infty)
. Then, the lemma holds for each
\iota^{k}
with the same
A
, i.e.

\mathbb{P}\big(\inf{}_{\iota^{k}}(T_{Q,\psi,\iota^{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)\neq\inf{}_{\iota^{k}}(T^{\prime}_{Q,\psi^{\prime},\iota^{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)\big)\leqslant\Big(2+\int_{A}\alpha({\bm{x}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}})\Big)\,d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\cap A,\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(Q,\psi^{\prime})\cap A\big).

Letting
k\to\infty
, the right hand side is constant, while the left hand side converges to
\mathbb{P}\big(\inf{}_{\iota}(T_{Q,\psi,\iota}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)\neq\inf{}_{\iota}(T^{\prime}_{Q,\psi^{\prime},\iota}(\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q})\cap A)\big)
. Corollary
5.8
follows from Lemma
5.7
(since the pushforward measure of
\iota
has no atoms).
The proofs of Proposition
5.4
and
5.10
carry over immediately.
∎

12.2.
Proofs for Section
6.1

Henceforth, we let
E^{\delta}
denote the set of finite configurations
\varphi\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}}
for which
\sup_{i\geqslant 1}\varphi(D_{i}^{\delta})\leqslant 1
, that is, the
D_{i}^{\delta}
separate the points of
\varphi
.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

First, consider the case where
\psi
is finite. We show that the Poisson embeddings
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi})
project to coupled processes
h^{\delta}\big(T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi})\big)
on
Q
such that there is almost surely a
\delta_{1}(\omega)
with
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})=h^{\delta}(T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}))
for
\delta<\delta_{1}
.

If
\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\}\cup\psi\in E^{\delta}
, we have
[
3
, Lem. 3.1]
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{U}^{m}}\tilde{\kappa}^{\delta}(({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1}),\dots,({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m}),\psi^{\delta,0})(\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\mathbb{N}})^{m}({\rm d}({\bm{r}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{r}}_{m}))

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\psi}(1-\pi({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}}))\prod_{i<j}(1-\pi({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{x}}_{j}))

\displaystyle=\kappa_{m}({\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m},\psi).

It follows (see computation in
[
3
, Lem. 3.1]
) for any event
F
,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{P}(h^{\delta}(\mathcal{X}^{\delta}(Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}))\in F)=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}(Q;\psi)\in F),

and for
B\subseteq Q
,

|Z_{B^{\delta}}(\psi^{\delta,0})-Z_{B}(\psi)|\leq\mathbb{P}(\psi\cup\mathcal{P}_{B}\notin E^{\delta}).

In particular, if
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in Q^{\delta}
and
\phi^{\delta}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}_{(-\infty,({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}))}^{\delta}}
is finite, then the retention probabilities for
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}
are

\displaystyle p^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),Q^{\delta},\phi^{\delta})=\kappa^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\phi^{\delta})\frac{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta}\cup\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\})}{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})}

\displaystyle=\kappa({\bm{x}},\phi)\frac{\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}\sum_{i}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in D_{i}^{\delta},r_{i}>\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\}}{\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}))}\frac{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta}\cup\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\})}{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})}

\displaystyle=p({\bm{x}},Q,\phi)\frac{\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}\sum_{i}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\in D_{i}^{\delta},r_{i}>\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\}}{\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}))}\frac{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta}\cup\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\})}{Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}(\phi\cup\{{\bm{x}}\})}\frac{Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}(\phi)}{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})}

(where
0/0=0
). Assume that
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}
and
\phi\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}}\cup\psi
. Then, for
\delta
small enough, there is at most one
{\bm{y}}\in D_{i}^{\delta}\cap\phi^{\delta}
for each
i
, and if such a
{\bm{y}}
exists, we have
r_{i}=r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
. Letting
\delta\to 0
, we thus get almost surely

\lim_{\delta\to 0}p^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),Q^{\delta},\phi^{\delta})=p({\bm{x}},Q,\phi)\frac{\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*},\psi)}\phi\}}{\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}))}=p^{\mathsf{RCM}}({\bm{x}},Q,\phi,\Gamma(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*},\psi)).

This shows that the Poisson embeddings
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi})
project to coupled processes
h^{\delta}(T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi}))
which, whenever
\delta<\delta_{1}(\omega)
, equals
T_{Q,\psi}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})
almost surely. By (
82
), the distribution of
T_{Q,\psi}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})
must be that of
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
.

For the case where
\psi
is infinite, let
\phi_{n}=\phi\cap W_{n}
. Then
p({\bm{x}},Q,\phi_{n},\Gamma_{n})\to p({\bm{x}},Q,\phi,\Gamma)
by dominated convergence applied to the partition functions. Thus, there is almost surely an
N(\omega)
such that for
n\geqslant N(\omega)
,
T_{Q,\psi_{n}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})
will agree with
T_{Q,\psi}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})
. Taking limits in the GNZ-equation for
T_{Q,\psi_{n}}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi_{n})
we see that
T_{Q,\psi}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi})
satisfy the GNZ-equation for
\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
and hence has the claimed distribution.
∎

We note for later that the Poisson embedding has the following property: If
{\bm{x}}
is the
\iota
-smallest point in
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{Q}
and
\xi\in\{\{{\bm{x}}\},\varnothing\}
denotes the thinning of
{\bm{x}}
, then

(83)

T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\psi}(\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*})=\xi\cup T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)},\psi\cup\xi}(\Phi_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)},\psi\cup\xi}^{*}),

where
\Phi_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)},\psi\cup\xi}^{*}
is constructed from
\Phi_{Q,\psi}^{*}
in the obvious way.

12.3.
Proofs for Section
6.2

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

Clearly,
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus V
is an independent thinning of
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
with retention probabilities given by
\rho_{\nu}
, so the first claim follows from e.g.
[
34
, Prop. 3.7]
. Thus, conditionally on
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu}
,
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus V
is a Poisson process of intensity
\rho_{\nu}\lambda
, and the marks used for defining the edges in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q\setminus V,\psi\cup\nu},V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
are uniform i.i.d. and independent of
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu}
. Hence
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q\setminus V,\psi\cup\nu},V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
is an RCM
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(Q,V\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
and
\Gamma_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
for any
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu}
. Thus, the same holds unconditionally.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

Consider first the situation where
\psi
and
\nu
are finite. Lift
\psi,\nu
to
\psi^{\delta,0},\nu^{\delta,0}\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathbb{X}^{\delta}}
by adding the zero mark. This is needed for formal reasons only and we will never use these marks in what follows.
Consider a standard Poisson embedding
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0},\iota_{\delta}}\big({\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\big)
where we define the deterministic set
V_{\delta}=\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in Q^{\delta}\mid{\bm{x}}\sim_{\delta}\nu^{\delta,0}\}
and the ordering

(84)

\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in V_{\delta}\}+\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\iota({\bm{x}})\big)\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\notin V_{\delta}\}.

This ordering is used instead of
\iota^{\delta}
in (
79
) leading to a new symmetric relation
\sim^{\delta}
in (
80
) which is then used for defining the PI
\kappa_{\delta}
as in (
81
). This
\kappa_{\delta}
is used for defining
T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0},\iota_{\delta}}\big({\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\big)
.
We will show that almost surely there is a
\delta_{1}(\omega)
such that for
\delta<\delta_{1}
,
h^{\delta}(T_{Q^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0},\iota_{\delta}}({\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}))=T_{Q,\nu,\psi}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})
.

Let
E^{\delta}
be as in the proof of Proposition
6.2
.
To determine whether
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in V_{\delta}^{*}\cap{\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}
, we only need to know the coordinates
r_{i}
of
{\bm{r}}
for which
D_{i}\cap\nu\neq\varnothing
. Thus, if
\nu\cup\psi\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q}\in E^{\delta}
, then
r_{i}=r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}
whenever
{\bm{y}}\in D_{i}\cap\nu
and hence
h^{*,\delta}(V_{\delta}^{*}\cap{\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})=V
. There is almost surely a
\delta_{0}(\omega)
, such that
\nu\cup\psi\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q}\in E^{\delta}
for
\delta<\delta_{0}
and hence
h^{*,\delta}(V_{\delta}^{*}\cap{\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})=V
for
\delta<\delta_{0}(\omega)
. Moreover, under
h^{\delta}
the graph
G^{\delta}({\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi\cup\delta)
associated with
\sim^{\delta}
is mapped to
h^{\delta}(G^{\delta}({\Phi}^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\cup\psi\cup\nu))=\Gamma_{\nu}({\Phi}^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi\cup\nu)
whenever
\delta<\delta_{0}(\omega)
and the ordering of the vertices associated with
\iota_{\delta}
agrees with the thinning order for
T_{Q,\nu,\psi}^{\mathsf{RCM}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})
.

It remains to identify the retention probabilities. Recall that for a given
\delta
and
\phi^{\delta}\subseteq Q_{(-\infty,({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}))}^{\delta}\cup\psi^{\delta,0}
, the retention probabilities on
Q^{\delta}
are

p^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),Q^{\delta},\phi^{\delta})=\kappa_{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\phi^{\delta})\frac{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta}\cup\{({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\})}{Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})}.

If
\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})<\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1})<\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{2})<\dotsm<\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m})
and
\phi\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\}\in E^{\delta}
, then

\tilde{\kappa}^{\delta}(({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1}),\dots,({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m});\phi^{\delta})=\prod_{i}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}\bigg(\sum_{{\bm{y}}\in D_{j}}\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{r}}_{i})_{j}>\pi({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{y}})\}\bigg)\prod_{i<j}\bigg(\sum_{{\bm{x}}_{i}\in D_{l}}\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{r}}_{j})_{l}>\pi({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{x}}_{j})\}\bigg)

where
({\bm{r}}_{i})_{j}
is the
j
th coordinate of
{\bm{r}}_{i}
.
In particular, if
\delta<\delta_{0}
,
\phi^{\delta}\subseteq(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\cap Q_{(-\infty,({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}))}^{\delta})\cup\psi^{\delta,0}

and
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}
, it holds almost surely that

\kappa^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\phi^{\delta})=\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi^{\delta}}\mathbbmss{1}\{r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}>\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\}=\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi\cup\nu)}\phi\}.

Next, we determine the limit of
Z_{Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})
when
\delta\to 0
. Suppose
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\in V_{\delta}
. Then, the set
Q_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}^{\delta}
is necessarily of the form

\displaystyle A_{\delta}

\displaystyle=(V_{\delta}\cap(h^{\delta})^{-1}(Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}))\cup(Q^{\delta}\setminus V_{\delta})=(h^{\delta})^{-1}(Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)})\cup\{({\bm{y}},{\bm{r}})\nsim_{\delta}\nu^{\delta,0},{\bm{y}}\leqslant_{\iota}{\bm{x}}\}.

We have the Poisson expansion

\displaystyle e^{(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta})(A_{\delta})}Z_{A_{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{(A_{\delta})^{m}}\tilde{\kappa}^{\delta}(({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1}),\dots,({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m});\phi^{\delta})(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta})^{m}({\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1}),\dots,{\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m}))

Assume
\phi\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\}\cup\nu\in E^{\delta}
and write
({{\bm{r}}}_{i})_{{\bm{y}}}
for the coordinate of
{{\bm{r}}}_{i}
corresponding to
{\bm{y}}\in\phi\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\}\cup\nu
.
If
\phi^{\delta}\subseteq Q_{(-\infty,({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})]}^{\delta}
and
\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})<\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1})<\dotsm<\iota_{\delta}({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m})
,

\displaystyle\prod_{l}\mathbbmss{1}\{({\bm{x}}_{l},{{\bm{r}}}_{l})\in A_{\delta}\}\kappa_{m}^{\delta}(({\bm{x}}_{1},{{\bm{r}}}_{1}),\dots,({\bm{x}}_{m},{{\bm{r}}}_{m});\phi^{\delta})

\displaystyle=\prod_{l:{\bm{x}}_{l}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\nu}\mathbbmss{1}\{({{\bm{r}}}_{l})_{\bm{y}}>\pi({\bm{x}}_{l},{\bm{y}})\}\prod_{k\leqslant m}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\phi}\mathbbmss{1}\{({{\bm{r}}}_{k})_{\bm{y}}>\pi({\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{y}})\}\prod_{i<j}\mathbbmss{1}\{({{\bm{r}}}_{j})_{{\bm{x}}_{i}}>\pi({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{x}}_{j})\}

(85)

\displaystyle=\prod_{l:{\bm{x}}_{l}<_{\iota}x}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{l}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\nu\}\prod_{k\leqslant m}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{k}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\phi\}\prod_{i<j}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{i}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}{\bm{x}}_{j}\},

where
\Gamma_{m}=\Gamma(\{({\bm{x}}_{1},{{\bm{r}}}_{1}),\dots,({\bm{x}}_{m},{{\bm{r}}}_{m})\},\phi\cup\nu)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\bigg|Z_{A_{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})-e^{-(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta})(A_{\delta})}\bigg(1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{Q^{m}}\prod_{l:{\bm{x}}_{l}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{l}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\nu\}\prod_{l}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{l}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\phi\}\prod_{i<j}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{i}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}{\bm{x}}_{j}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\big(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta}\big)^{m}({\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{1},{{\bm{r}}}_{1}),\dots,{\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{m},{{\bm{r}}}_{m}))\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\phi\cup\nu\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q}\in E^{\delta}).

In particular, it follows from (
85
) with
m=1
and
\phi=\varnothing
that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\big(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta}\big)(A_{\delta})=\int_{Q}\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}({\bm{y}})\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{y}})=:c({\bm{x}},\nu),

so

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}Z_{A_{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})=e^{-c({\bm{x}},\nu)}\bigg(1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{Q^{m}}\prod_{{\bm{x}}_{l}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{l}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\nu\cup\phi\}\prod_{{\bm{x}}_{k}>_{\iota}{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{k}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\phi\}\prod_{i<j}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{i}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}{\bm{x}}_{j}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\big(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta}\big)^{m}\big({\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{1},{{\bm{r}}}_{1}),\dots,{\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{m},{{\bm{r}}}_{m})\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=e^{-c({\bm{x}},\nu)}\bigg(1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{Q^{m}}\prod_{l}\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}({\bm{x}}_{l})\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{{\bm{x}}_{l}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{l}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\phi\setminus\nu\}\prod_{k:{\bm{x}}_{k}>_{\iota}{\bm{x}}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{k}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}\phi\}\prod_{i<j}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}_{i}\nsim_{\Gamma_{m}}{\bm{x}}_{j}\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}_{1})\ldots\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}_{m})\bigg)

(86)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}}^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}(Q,\phi\cup\nu)}\phi\setminus\nu\}\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}(Q,\phi\cup\nu)}\phi\}\prod_{{\bm{y}}_{1},{\bm{y}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q}^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}_{i}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}(Q,\phi\cup\nu)}{\bm{y}}_{j}\}\bigg].

In the second equality, we first integrated over the
\nu
-coordinates of
{{\bm{r}}}_{l}
for those
l
with
{\bm{x}}_{l}<_{\iota}{\bm{x}}
and then with respect to the remaining
{\bm{r}}
-coordinates. In the last equality, we recognized the expression as an expectation with respect to a Poisson process
\mathcal{P}_{Q}^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda}
of intensity
\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}\lambda
.

After thinning
V
, we have that
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus V
is an independent Poisson process of intensity
\rho_{\nu}\lambda
and for
\delta<\delta_{0}
,
h^{*,\delta}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus V_{\delta})=\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus V
with the ordering given by
\iota
. Computing as above, we find when
\psi\cup\nu\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q}\in E^{\delta}
and
({\bm{x}},u,{\bm{r}})\in\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus V_{\delta}^{*}
,

\kappa^{\delta}(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\phi^{\delta})=\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{x}}\nsim_{\Gamma_{\nu}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi\setminus\nu)}\phi\setminus\nu\}

and for
({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})\notin V_{\delta}
and
\phi\subseteq\psi\cup V\cup\mathcal{P}_{Q_{(-\infty,{\bm{x}})}}

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}{Z}_{Q^{\delta}_{(({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}}),\infty)}}(\phi^{\delta})={\mathbb{E}}\bigg(\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(\mathcal{P}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda},\phi\setminus\nu)}\phi\setminus\nu\}\prod_{{\bm{y}}_{1},{\bm{y}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}}\mathbbmss{1}\{{\bm{y}}_{1}\nsim_{\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(\mathcal{P}_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda},\phi\setminus\nu)}{\bm{y}}_{2}\}\bigg)

\displaystyle=Z_{Q_{({\bm{x}},\infty)}}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(\phi\setminus\nu).

Thus, we recognize
\lim_{\delta\to 0}p^{\delta}({\bm{x}},Q^{\delta},\phi^{\delta})
as the retention probabilities (
40
) for the embedding of
\mathcal{X}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(Q,\xi\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
in an RCM
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(Q,\xi\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
.

In total, we have that for almost all realizations of
\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}
, there is a
\delta_{1}(\omega)>0
such that

h^{\delta}\big(T_{Q^{\delta},\nu^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\big)=T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})

for all
\delta<\delta_{1}
. To see that
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)
, note that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda(Q)}\mathbb{P}(h^{\delta}\big(T_{Q^{\delta},\nu^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\big)\in F)=1+

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{(Q^{\delta})^{m}}\mathbbmss{1}\{\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\}\in F\}\tilde{\kappa}^{\delta}(({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1}),\dots,({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m});\psi^{\delta,0})(\lambda\otimes\mathsf{Leb}_{[0,1]}^{\delta})^{m}({\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{1}),\dots,{\rm d}({\bm{x}}_{m},{\bm{r}}_{m})).

By the considerations above, the right hand side equals

1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{Q^{\delta}}\mathbbmss{1}\{\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\}\in F\}\tilde{\kappa}({\bm{x}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{x}}_{m};\psi)\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}_{1}),\dots,\lambda({\rm d}{\bm{x}}_{m})

plus an error term of at most
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cup\psi\cup\nu\notin E^{\delta})
. Letting
\delta\to\infty
and using dominated convergence, we get that
\mathbb{P}(T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\in F)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{P}(h^{\delta}\big(T_{Q^{\delta},\nu^{\delta},\psi^{\delta,0}}(\Phi^{*,\delta}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\big)\in F)=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}(Q,\psi)\in F)
.
By construction, we see that conditionally on
\xi
,
T^{\mathsf{RCM}}_{Q,\nu,\psi}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu})\setminus V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{X}^{\rho_{\nu}\lambda}(Q,\xi\cup(\psi\setminus\nu))
.

The case where
\psi
is infinite follows by taking limits of
\psi_{n}=\psi\cap W_{n}
for
n\to\infty
as in the proof of Proposition
6.2
. When
\nu
is infinite, the theorem holds for
\nu_{n}=\nu\cap W_{n}
. As
n\to\infty
, the set
V\subseteq\varphi
increases, and since
\varphi
is finite,
V
stabilizes as the set of all points connected to
\nu_{n}
for
n\geqslant M
for some
M(\omega)
.
Thus, it is again enough to see that the retention probabilities in both stages of the thinning procedure converge. For the thinning of
V
, note that
\rho_{\nu_{n}}
and
\rho_{\nu_{n}}^{\bm{x}}
both decrease towards
\rho_{\nu}
and
\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}
, respectively, when
n\to\infty
. To take the limit in (
43
), note that we may couple the RCMs
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu_{n}}^{\bm{x}}}(Q,\psi\cup\nu_{n})
to a common RCM
\Gamma(Q\times[0,1],\psi\cup\nu)
. Indeed, we take the vertex set of
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu_{n}}^{\bm{x}}}(Q,\psi\cup\nu_{n})
to be
\psi\cup\nu_{n}
together with those
{\bm{x}}\in Q
for which
({\bm{x}},u)
a vertex of
\Gamma(Q\times[0,1],\psi\cup\nu)
and
u\leq\rho_{\nu_{n}}^{\bm{x}}({\bm{x}})
. As edges of
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu_{n}}^{\bm{x}}}(Q,\psi\cup\nu_{n})
we take all edges induced from
\Gamma(Q\times[0,1],\psi\cup\nu)
. Then, almost surely,
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu_{n}}^{\bm{x}}}(Q,\psi\cup\nu_{n})
will equal
\Gamma^{\rho_{\nu}^{\bm{x}}}(Q,\psi\cup\nu)
for
n
sufficiently large (except for the extra points
\nu\setminus\nu_{n}
). Hence, dominated convergence shows that
Z_{Q}(\phi,\nu_{n},{\bm{x}})\to Z_{Q}(\phi,\nu,{\bm{x}})
when
n\to\infty
. A similar argument applies for the retention probabilities in (
40
).

∎

Remark 12.2
.

In the proof, we redefine the relation
\sim_{\delta}
to
\sim^{\delta}
when we change the ordering to
\iota_{\delta}
, which is different from what we do in the first step of a disagreement coupling for Model (L) where the ordering is fixed throughout. This ensures that the thinning probabilities depend only on the marks of the unexplored points. Without this assumption, the limit of
Z_{A^{\delta}}(\phi^{\delta})
would depend on the marks of
\phi^{\delta}
and we would not be able to obtain formulas for the retention probabilities in the limit
\delta\to 0
. This fact is going to complicate the formulation of disagreement coupling for Model (P) in the following.

Proof of Proposition
6.7
.

We first show by induction that conditionally on
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k})^{*}
,
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k}
is a Poisson process of intensity
\rho_{k}\lambda
on
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)}
where
\rho_{0}=1
and

\rho_{k}({\bm{x}}):=\rho_{k-1}({\bm{x}})\prod_{{\bm{y}}\in\nu_{k}}(1-\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})).

Assume that this holds for some
k
and condition on
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1})^{*}
. Then,
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus(S_{k}\cup V_{k+1})
is a Poisson process of intensity
\rho_{k+1}\lambda
on
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)}
by Lemma
6.4
. When
V_{k+1}\neq\varnothing
,
S_{k}\cup V_{k+1}=S_{k+1}
and
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)}=Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}
showing the claim in this case. When
V_{k+1}=\varnothing
,

\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k+1}=\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus(S_{k}\cup V_{k+1}\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\})=(\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus(S_{k}\cup V_{k+1}))\cap Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}.

Since
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}
is a stopping set and we have conditioned on
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k}\cap Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}
is a Poisson process of intensity
\rho_{k+1}\lambda
on
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}
showing the induction step in this case.

Define the graph

(87)

\Gamma_{k}:=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\Gamma_{\nu_{k+1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q\setminus S_{k},\psi\cup\nu},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l}).

It follows from Lemma
6.4
and the Poisson distribution of
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k}
that conditionally on
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k})^{*}
we have

(88)

\Gamma_{\nu_{k+1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma^{\rho_{k}\lambda}(Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)},S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l})).

We will show by induction that
\Gamma_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
. Note that
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{\nu_{1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
by Lemma
6.4
. Suppose it holds for some
k\geqslant 1
.
By Definition
6.3
,

\Gamma_{k}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu_{k+1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k}\nu_{l}))\cup\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}_{\nu_{k+1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},V_{k+1}\cup S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l})).

Now, condition on
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1})^{*}
. If
V_{k+1}\neq\varnothing
, then
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\Gamma^{\mathsf{Init}}_{\nu_{k+1}}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1}
. By Lemma
6.4
and the Poisson distribution of
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k}
,

\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}_{\nu_{k+1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},V_{k+1}\cup S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma^{\rho_{k+1}\lambda}(Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k},\infty)},S_{k+1}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}).

If
V_{k+1}=\varnothing
, then
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\mathcal{G}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1}
. In this case,
S_{k+1}=S_{k}\cup\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}
and

\Gamma^{\mathsf{Bulk}}_{\nu_{k+1}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k}^{*},V_{k+1}\cup S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))=\mathcal{G}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}}\cup\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k+1}^{*},S_{k+1}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))

is the RCM constructed as in Definition
6.1
, which can be constructed by first finding the
\iota
-smallest point
{\bm{x}}_{k+1}
and all its connections to
S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l})
forming the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}}
and then connecting all points in
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k+1}
to
\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}\cup(S_{k}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))=S_{k+1}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}
forming the graph
\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k+1}^{*},S_{k+1}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l})
. Thus,

\Gamma_{k}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k}\cup\mathcal{G}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k+1}\}}\cup\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k+1}^{*},S_{k+1}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))=\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1}\cup\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k+1}^{*},S_{k+1}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l})).

Since
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\setminus S_{k+1}
is a Poisson process on
Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}
of intensity
\rho_{k+1}\lambda
, we have

\Gamma(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu}\setminus\bar{S}_{k+1}^{*},S_{k+1}\setminus\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma^{\rho_{k+1}\lambda}(Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)},S_{k+1}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l})).

Thus, in both cases (
V_{k+1}=\varnothing
and
V_{k+1}\neq\varnothing
), we get that conditionally on
(\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1})^{*}
,

\Gamma_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}_{k+1}\cup\Gamma^{\rho_{k+1}\lambda}({Q_{({\bm{x}}_{k+1},\infty)}},S_{k+1}\setminus(\cup_{l\leqslant k+1}\nu_{l}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma_{k+1},

where the second equality in distribution comes from (
88
) with
k
replaced by
k+1
.
Since
\Gamma_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
unconditionally by the induction hypothesis, also
\Gamma_{k+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Gamma(Q,\psi)
unconditionally. Since
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{dc}}(\Phi^{*}_{Q,\psi\cup\nu},\psi)
equals
\Gamma_{k}
for
k\geqslant K(\omega)
, we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 12.3
.

For arguing about the clusters of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}_{\nu}
in the proofs, it is sometimes convenient to view the cluster-based graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
as a subgraph of a directed graph
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\cup\nu)
. The vertex set of
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
is
\varphi\cup\psi\cup\nu
and for
{\bm{x}}\in\varphi
and
{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\cup\psi\cup\nu
, there is a directed edge
{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{y}}
if
r_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\leq\pi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
. If we only keep edges
{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{y}}
in
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
with
{\bm{x}}>_{\iota}{\bm{y}}
(we refer to these as order-reversing edges) and ignore edge directions, we obtain
\Gamma(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi\cup\nu)
. We obtain
V_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
as the set of vertices in
\varphi
having a directed edge towards a point in
\nu
. If we keep the edges of
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
pointing from
\varphi
to
\psi
, edges pointing from
\varphi\setminus V_{\nu}
to
V_{\nu}
and order-reversing edges
{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{y}}
with
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in V_{\nu}
or
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\varphi\setminus V_{\nu}
, then we get
\Gamma_{\nu}(\bar{\varphi}^{*},\psi)
.

The cluster-based graph
\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{cl}}
is built from
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
